## Introduction: A Painter Made of Two Kinds of Evidence

No painter of the European Middle Ages has a larger reputation than *Giotto di Bondone*, and few rest on a less secure body of evidence. By the time Dante<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-dante-alighieri" class="footnote"><sup>1</sup></a> wrote the eleventh canto of the *Purgatorio*, Giotto's name already stood for pictorial modernity. His contemporaries and the next two generations kept adding to his fame. Chroniclers, poets, humanists and painters used him as the benchmark of excellence, and in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries Ghiberti<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-lorenzo-ghiberti" class="footnote"><sup>2</sup></a> and Vasari<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-giorgio-vasari" class="footnote"><sup>3</sup></a> made him the founding figure of an art history centred on Florence.

Yet the documents that fix his movements are sparse. Only a handful of surviving works carry his name, and the works most often reproduced as the essence of his art are not all securely his. The Assisi Francis cycle is the obvious case, but there are others. The Treccani medieval encyclopedia notes that the documentary situation, though relatively rich, says almost nothing that would anchor a chronology of the works. Reconstructing Giotto's career has therefore depended, to an unusual degree, on stylistic analysis, with all the disagreement that method brings.

The account proceeds by distinguishing layers of evidence rather than by narrating a life. Four layers are kept separate throughout.

The first is the archival record: notarial acts, wills, royal privileges, communal deliberations and payment registers from Florence, Assisi, Rome, Naples and Padua.

The second is testimony from the fourteenth century, which is of uneven value. Dante, Riccobaldo Ferrarese<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-riccobaldo-ferrarese" class="footnote"><sup>4</sup></a> and Giovanni Villani<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-giovanni-villani" class="footnote"><sup>5</sup></a> wrote while Giotto was alive or shortly after his death. Boccaccio<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-giovanni-boccaccio" class="footnote"><sup>6</sup></a>, Petrarch<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-petrarch" class="footnote"><sup>7</sup></a>, Antonio Pucci, Cennino Cennini<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-cennino-cennini" class="footnote"><sup>8</sup></a> and Filippo Villani wrote in the following decades.

The third layer is the fifteenth- and sixteenth-century tradition of Ghiberti's *Commentarii* and Vasari's *Vite*. These texts are indispensable, but both authors had rhetorical purposes, and Vasari in particular often converted proverbs and workshop lore into biography.

The fourth layer is modern scholarship, where some questions are settled by broad agreement and others remain open. Of these the Assisi question is the most important. The main lines of the modern debate are easiest to follow through a few landmark texts. [Richard Offner](/scholars.html#offner-richard)'s 1939 essay "Giotto, non-Giotto" gave the skeptical tradition its most influential form. [Luciano Bellosi](/scholars.html#bellosi-luciano)'s *La pecora di Giotto* (1985) is the strongest modern defence of the attribution. [Miklós Boskovits](/scholars.html#boskovits-miklos) wrote the 2001 synthesis in the Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani, the most complete account of the career as the attributionist tradition understands it.

## The Early Testimony and Its Strata

The earliest literary witness is also the most famous. In *Purgatorio* XI, among the proud, Dante has the illuminator Oderisi da Gubbio reflect on how quickly earthly glory passes:

> Credette Cimabue nella pittura / tener lo campo, e ora ha Giotto il grido, / sì che la fama di colui è scura (vv. 94–96).

The tercet is often read as a critical judgment, but its argument is moral. Fame is fleeting, and the example is that the older master has been eclipsed by the younger. What it proves historically is narrower and still important: by about 1310, the approximate date of this part of the *Commedia*, Giotto had overtaken [Cimabue](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Cimabue.html) in reputation well beyond Florence. The Treccani medieval encyclopedia suggests that the tradition, current from the late fourteenth century, that Giotto trained in Cimabue's workshop may itself have grown out of this tercet. No document confirms the apprenticeship. Critics have accepted it widely and, on stylistic grounds, not unreasonably.

A notice in the *Compilatio chronologica* of Riccobaldo Ferrarese, a notary and chronicler from Ferrara, is virtually contemporary with Dante's verses. Modern philology dates it to around 1312–1313. Riccobaldo calls Giotto *pictor eximius* and supports the praise by listing works in the Franciscan churches of Assisi, Rimini and Padua, in the Paduan Palazzo della Ragione (the communal palace), and in the Arena Chapel<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-arena-chapel" class="footnote"><sup>9</sup></a>. The passage belongs among Riccobaldo's memories of his stay in Padua in 1306–1308, so those years may serve as a *terminus ante quem* for the works he names. His text is the earliest written evidence that Giotto worked at Assisi at all. It is also frustratingly silent on what he did there. It names the Franciscan church without specifying the Upper or Lower Church, the nave or the transept, the Francis legend or anything else. The whole Assisi controversy begins in that gap.

Giovanni Villani, the Florentine merchant-chronicler, supplies the fixed points at the end of the life. Writing around 1340, under the year 1334, he records the foundation on 18 July of the new campanile of Santa Reparata. The bishop blessed the first stone, and the commune made "maestro Giotto nostro cittadino" overseer and provisioner of the works. Villani calls him

> il più sovrano maestro stato in dipintura che si trovasse al suo tempo, e quelli che più trasse ogni figura e atti al naturale.

He adds that Giotto, returning from Milan where the commune had sent him to serve its lord,

> passò di questa vita a dì VIII di gennaio MCCCXXXVI, e fu seppellito per lo Comune a Santa Reparata con grande onore.

The Florentine year began on 25 March, so Villani's January 1336 is January 1337 in modern reckoning. This is the one date in Giotto's life that no serious scholar disputes. Villani's praise also defines Giotto's achievement for the first time in terms of drawing figures and gestures from nature, which set the terms of much later criticism.

In the following decades the testimony changes character. Boccaccio's novella in the *Decameron* (VI.5), in which Giotto and the jurist Forese da Rabatta, both famously ugly, exchange barbs on a muddy road, gives what is probably the first considered critical assessment of Giotto's art. Boccaccio writes that Giotto's genius was such that nature produced nothing

> che egli con lo stile e con la penna e col pennello non dipignesse sì simile a quella, che non simile, anzi più tosto dessa paresse.

Viewers were often deceived and took the painted thing for the real one. Having brought back to light an art that for centuries had been buried

> sotto gli error d'alcuni che più a dilettar gli occhi degl'ignoranti che a compiacere allo 'ntelletto de' savi dipigneano,

he could rightly be called "una delle luci della fiorentina gloria". The passage joins two ideas that would later separate: the illusionistic power that deceives the eye, and the intellectual quality addressed to the discerning. It also introduces, well before the humanists, the scheme of revival after a long burial. Petrarch, too, acknowledged Giotto's excellence, and recorded for his own time the superiority of painting over the plastic arts.

The most technical early formulation comes from a painter. At the start of his *Libro dell'arte*, written in the last decade of the fourteenth century, Cennino Cennini, trained in the workshop of Agnolo Gaddi, says that Giotto

> rimutò l'arte del dipignere di greco in latino e ridusse al moderno; ed ebbe l'arte più compiuta che avessi mai più nessuno.

Cennini places his own training in a line descending from Giotto through Taddeo and Agnolo Gaddi. His sentence is therefore partly a claim about workshop lineage. It is also the source of the durable opposition between the "Greek" manner (Byzantine and Italo-Byzantine convention) and the "Latin" manner (Western, modern, attentive to nature and to classical models). Filippo Villani's *Liber de origine civitatis Florentiae et eiusdem famosis civibus*, near the century's end, completed the process by which Giotto became a civic emblem, inseparable from Florence's historical identity. By then he had become a humanist commonplace, cited alongside Apelles in letters by writers whose subject was not painting at all.

Lorenzo Ghiberti's second *Commentario*, from the mid-fifteenth century, was the first to assemble a catalogue. Ghiberti repeated the verdict on Greek crudeness ("Arrechò l'arte nuova, lasciò la roçeza de' Greci") and refined it: Giotto

> arecò l'arte naturale e la gentileza con essa, non uscendo dalle misure.

He also said Giotto had painted at Assisi, in the church of the Friars Minor, "quasi tutta la parte di sotto". Most modern readers take this to mean the Francis legend on the lower zone of the Upper Church nave. A minority, including prominent skeptics, have read it as the transept and chapels of the Lower Church. The phrase settles nothing. Vasari's *Vite* of 1550 and 1568 then produced the first full biography. It lists a great many works and presents Giotto as head of a school through whose workshop every major painter of the Trecento was supposed to have passed. The 1568 edition revised the 1550 itinerary, moving the Neapolitan stay to the end of the career and adding Pisa and Avignon, which shows how uncertain Vasari's own chronology was. As a bibliographer of Giotto observed in 1938, Vasari became the codifier of both the fame and the myth, and of prejudices that weighed on criticism for two and a half centuries.

## Birth, Family, and the Anatomy of the Legends

The date of birth is a small problem with large consequences. The received date of about 1267 does not come from a document. It comes from Antonio Pucci, the Florentine bell-founder, town crier and versifier. Around 1373 his *Centiloquio*, a rhymed paraphrase of Villani's chronicle in terza rima, gives Giotto's death in 1336 (Florentine style) and states that he "morì d'età di settant'anni".

Seventy years back from January 1337 gives 1266 or 1267. The Dizionario Biografico observes that Pucci could have known Giotto personally, and it has been suggested that he may have drawn on the lost original tombstone. The date is generally accepted, but only as an approximation. A round figure of seventy in a versified chronicle is a conventional expression, not a birth register, and it is a mistake to write "1267" with the confidence one gives to "8 January 1337".

The rival date of 1276, from the 1550 edition of Vasari, is still adopted by a few writers. It is very hard to reconcile with the probable dating of several important works to the last decade of the Duecento: a painter born in 1276 would have been in his mid-teens when the [Santa Maria Novella](/Content/Churches/Basilica-Santa-Maria-Novella.html) crucifix and the late biblical scenes at Assisi are usually thought to have been painted. A third proposal, based on a document reported orally in the late 1960s that would have put the birth near the mid-thirteenth century, cannot be assessed because the document cannot now be found. Everything about the early career depends on this: the plausibility of Giotto at Assisi around 1290, the [Isaac Master](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Maestro-delle-Storie-di-Isacco.html) question, and the relationship with Cimabue.

The birthplace is equally traditional. Colle di Vespignano in the Mugello, now part of the commune of Vicchio, is the place "secondo la tradizione". The documents place the family firmly in Florence. Giotto's father, Bondone di Angiolino, was a smith and lived in the parish of Santa Maria Novella from at least 1295. The 1312 will of Riccuccio di Puccio del Mugnaio, which mentions the Santa Maria Novella crucifix, calls the painter "de dicto populo Sancte Marie Novelle". This matters for the legends. Ghiberti's picture of a "poverissimo" father and Vasari's shepherd boy tending the family flock at Vespignano both depend on a rural, pastoral childhood that the documents do not support, although the Mugello connection is real and the painter kept ties to the area.

The sheep story shows how the legends accumulated in layers. It is often called a Vasarian invention, and that is inaccurate. Its first surviving form is in Ghiberti, who says a boy of wonderful talent was drawing a sheep from nature on a stone slab when Cimabue, passing by on the road "a Bologna", saw him and took him as his pupil. Vasari moved the scene to Vespignano, made Giotto ten years old, put a pointed stone in his hand, and set it against a "lastra piana e pulita". He then added a second anecdote in which the young apprentice paints a fly on the nose of one of Cimabue's figures so convincingly that the master tries to brush it away.

Bellosi deliberately took his book's title from the sheep. He argued that an anecdote reported by Ghiberti could not simply be dismissed along with the many Vasari used as rhetorical devices to round out a life. The Dizionario Biografico is more austere: the discovery of the untaught shepherd genius is plainly a topos of classical biography, the sort of story told of ancient artists, and should not be credited as history. It prefers a report by a Dante commentator of the late fourteenth century, who says that Giotto's father first placed him in the wool trade and only reluctantly let him become Cimabue's pupil once his inclinations were clear.

The commentator's version has documentary support of a kind. In 1312 the famous painter hired out a *telarium Francigenum*, a French-style loom, to a weaver named Bartolo di Rinuccio, and he stayed in contact with the textile trade long after he became famous. The fly anecdote, like the story of Zeuxis's grapes, belongs to the rhetoric of illusion that Boccaccio had already established. It is a compliment in the ancient manner, not an event.

The story of the O belongs to the same genre, and its internal contradictions expose it. Vasari tells how a courtier, sent by the pope to gather drawings from Tuscan painters in order to choose an artist for St Peter's, asked Giotto for a sample. Giotto drew a perfect circle freehand, "senza muovere il braccio e senza seste", and sent it off. The pope and his knowledgeable courtiers understood from it how far Giotto surpassed all others, and from this, Vasari says, came the proverb "Tu sei più tondo che l'O di Giotto".

Vasari himself points out that the saying depends on an ambiguity, since in Tuscan *tondo* means both a perfect circle and slowness of wit. The anecdote therefore looks like an explanation invented after the fact for a proverb that already existed and that originally mocked dullness, not a record of an event from which the proverb arose. The details do not hold together either. The 1550 edition names the pope as "Benedetto XII da Tolosa"<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-benedict-xii" class="footnote"><sup>10</sup></a>, who reigned from 1334 to 1342, when Giotto was capomaestro in Florence and near the end of his life. Later retellings quietly substitute Benedict XI<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-benedict-xi" class="footnote"><sup>11</sup></a> or Boniface VIII<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-pope-boniface-viii" class="footnote"><sup>12</sup></a>, and none of these fits the Roman commissions we can document, which came through Cardinal Jacopo Stefaneschi. The O of Giotto is a fine piece of sixteenth-century Florentine art theory about the primacy of the draughtsman's hand. It is not evidence for Giotto's life.

## Formation: Cimabue, Rome and the Navicella

Setting the legends aside leaves the question of formation more open than the traditional narrative suggests. That Giotto was close to Cimabue is likely. Bellosi even proposed, cautiously, that the young Giotto had a hand in a Madonna at Castelfiorentino usually given to Cimabue, pointing to drapery cut in sharp undercut folds that resembles the earliest works attributed to Giotto at Assisi. The Dizionario Biografico calls the idea puzzling but concedes the similarity.

Other evidence points to Rome. Whoever painted the late biblical scenes and the Francis cycle at Assisi was thoroughly versed in the figurative traditions of late-thirteenth-century Roman painting: [Pietro Cavallini](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Pietro-Cavallini.html), [Jacopo Torriti](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Jacopo-Torriti.html), the painters of the portico of San Lorenzo fuori le Mura. This has repeatedly suggested an early and probably extended stay in Rome, and the painter's Roman grounding is one reason some have wanted to make the Isaac Master a Roman. Cavallini's documentation was revised in the late 1980s, making him more than twenty years Giotto's senior. That undoes Vasari's statement that Cavallini was Giotto's pupil and makes it at least possible that the influence ran in the opposite direction.

The Roman works are documented unevenly. The fresco fragment in San Giovanni in Laterano showing Boniface VIII between two clerics, once in the Benediction Loggia of the Lateran palace, has traditionally been read as the proclamation of the Jubilee of 1300. Its subject has been questioned, since it may represent the pope taking possession of the Lateran around 1295–1297, and its attribution to Giotto has been widely rejected in favour of a Roman painter under his influence. The Navicella, the great mosaic of the apostles' boat in the storm with Christ saving Peter from the waves, which faced the atrium of old St Peter's, stands on firmer ground. Giotto's authorship is attested by the *Liber benefactorum* of the basilica, in the necrology of Cardinal Jacopo Stefaneschi, dated around 1361–1362. It records that Stefaneschi paid Giotto 2,200 florins for the work. The cardinal also composed the verse inscription, now lost but known from transcriptions.

The date is another matter. A seventeenth-century claim that the mosaic was made in 1298 has no documentary support. One modern line dates it close to 1300. Another dates it to around 1310, near a document of December 1313 in which Giotto, in Florence, appointed a procurator named Benedetto di Pace to recover clothing, bedding and household goods he had left in the house of Lippa da Rieti near the Torre dei Conti in Rome.

The mosaic itself cannot decide the matter because, in any meaningful sense, it no longer survives. It was moved several times, restored, and re-erected in 1674 in the portico of the new basilica, where it is effectively a seventeenth-century replica. The best evidence for its original appearance is the copy made by Francesco Berretta in 1628, before the transfer. The only surviving fragments of the Trecento surface are two roundels with busts of angels, in the Vatican Grottoes and in San Pietro Ispano at Boville Ernica, which were removed in 1610. Their technique belongs to the Roman mosaic workshops of the late Duecento. Their design shows Giotto's monumental modelling. So the most celebrated work of Giotto's Roman career is well documented as a commission but lost as an object. It is a reminder that the documented Giotto and the visible Giotto overlap only in part.

### The Madonna of Borgo San Lorenzo

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-56.jpg" alt="Fragmentary panel painting showing the Virgin in half length against bare, worm-eaten wood where the ground has been lost. She wears a deep blue maphorion with a gold border over a red dress, her large dark eyes gazing frontally; a child’s small right arm is raised in a caress against her cheek, his left arm reaches down toward her hands in the abraded lower zone, and a fragment of red drapery with a gold hem survives at her left shoulder where the lost Christ Child once sat. The lower zone, with the Virgin’s hands, is heavily abraded">
  <figcaption>Giotto di Bondone (attributed), Madonna and Child (fragment), c. 1290. Tempera on panel, 81.5 × 41 cm. Borgo San Lorenzo (Mugello), Pieve di San Lorenzo. From the oratory of Sant’Omobono.</figcaption>
</figure>

The *Madonna of Borgo San Lorenzo* is the most moving of Giotto’s survivals, and the most improbable: the one work by the painter preserved in the Mugello, the hill country of his birth — and for centuries nobody knew it was there. The panel hung in the oratory of Sant’Omobono beside the pieve, repainted wholesale, blackened by candle smoke, venerated under the affectionate name of the “Black Madonna”; only the restoration carried out by the Opificio delle Pietre Dure in 1982–84 stripped the accretions and found, beneath them, a face of the late Duecento of startling quality — and the scholarship of the following years, beginning from its publication in the mid-1980s, converged with growing confidence on the young Giotto himself, in the years of the Assisi scaffolding, around 1290. The find rearranged the map: the painter’s homeland, which possessed of its greatest son only the legend of Cimabue discovering the shepherd boy drawing sheep upon a stone, suddenly possessed a picture — and a picture, fittingly, of the painter’s beginnings.

What survives is a fragment, and the losses themselves have become part of the image’s eloquence. The panel — the surviving portion of a larger gabled complex, cut down and worn to the bare, worm-tunnelled wood across its margins — preserves the Virgin in half length, the blue maphorion with its gold border framing a face of grave, frontal beauty: the long Byzantine eyes still carrying the icon’s authority, but the flesh modelled with a new softness, the mouth small and living, the whole head turned the fraction of a degree that separates a formula from a person.

Of the Child in her arms the body is lost — a fragment of red, gold-hemmed drapery at her left shoulder marks where he sat — but both his small arms survive, and they carry the whole of the picture’s meaning: the right rises in a caress against his mother’s cheek, and the left reaches down, clearly legible in the panel’s lower zone, toward her supporting hands.

The gesture belongs to the Byzantine type of the Virgin of Tenderness, the Eleousa, in which Mother and Child press cheek to cheek against the sorrow to come; here, with the Child’s body lost, his two arms are all that is left of him — a caress and a reach without a figure between them, comforting a mother who has, in the picture’s ruined state as in her foreknowledge, already lost her son.

No conservator would restore it, and no one should wish to: time has edited the panel into an unintended Pietà. The early accounts of the recovered picture record a second surviving motif of the Child — the grip on his mother’s finger — though in the panel’s present legibility it is the caress that carries the image.

Critically, the panel has become a cornerstone of the young Giotto: the kinship with the Madonna of San Giorgio alla Costa — the firm oval, the grave frontality, the drapery’s new weight — binds it to the painter’s earliest Florentine panel production, and the dating around 1290 places it in the orbit of the Assisi years, when the revolution of the Upper Church was in the making; the attribution, advanced from the restoration’s findings and sustained in the literature since, now commands what may fairly be called a prevailing consensus, with the prudent “attributed” retained here as in the pieve’s own presentation.

The original destination is itself a small open question — the altar of Sant’Omobono, by the oratory’s tradition, with the hypothesis also advanced of a provenance from the convent of San Francesco in Borgo San Lorenzo: a Franciscan origin which, if confirmed, would thread even this rural fragment into the great Franciscan web that runs from Assisi to Santa Croce.

The panel hangs today on a pillar of the right aisle of the Pieve di San Lorenzo — the grand Romanesque plebs of the Florentine contado, documented from 934 — exhibited in recent decades in the major Giotto exhibitions and returned, each time, to the Mugello. Its state is that of an honourable ruin: the surviving paint film fine and stable within its archipelago of losses, the gold of the borders glinting against the naked wood. It is, of all the works discussed here, the one that most rewards the pilgrimage: a half-ruined Virgin, a child’s two surviving arms — the beginning of Giotto’s art, preserved in the landscape where he began.

### Madonna and Child (Private Collection)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-57.jpg" alt="Panel painting on gold ground showing the Virgin in half length, wearing a black mantle with broad gold-embroidered borders and fringes over a red dress, a jewelled band across her brow beneath the veil and a gold star on her shoulder. She supports the Christ Child on her left arm and raises her right hand, open, toward him. The Child, in a coral-red mantle over a pale gold-striped tunic, blesses with his right hand and holds a white scroll; both haloes are incised with dense vine ornament in the gold.">
  <figcaption>Giotto di Bondone (attributed), Madonna and Child, c. 1297. Tempera and gold on panel. Private collection.</figcaption>
</figure>

Among the panels gathered around Giotto’s beginnings, the Madonna now in a private collection is the hidden one: known to the public through images, exhibition appearances and the critical literature rather than a museum wall, it is also, instructively, the most archaic — the picture in which the young painter, if his it is, stands closest to the Byzantine inheritance he was in the act of leaving. The scheme descends from the Hodegetria, the Virgin “who shows the way”: the Child enthroned on his mother’s left arm, blessing, the scroll of the Law in his small fist, the mother’s free hand raised toward him. But the hand does not point. It opens — palm turned inward, fingers long and relaxed, a gesture hovering between presentation, adoration and the beginning of a caress — and in that single softened motif the whole program of the generation declares itself: the icon’s signpost becoming a mother’s gesture, doctrine thawing into relation, while everything around it keeps the old liturgical dress.

That dress is painted with a goldsmith’s orthodoxy that makes this the most Eastern surface in the group. The black maphorion is hemmed in broad gold-embroidered borders that travel the whole silhouette and break into fringes at the wrist — the chrysography of the icons, worn here as actual embroidery; a jewelled band crosses the brow beneath the veil, crowning the Virgin as regina with Byzantine discretion; the gold star glints at the shoulder, sign of her virginity from the oldest iconographic stock; and the two haloes are incised with dense vine-scroll ornament of notable refinement, the gold ground worked as preciously as the cloth. Within this strict armature, the faces carry the new art.

The Virgin’s head is a long, grave oval — the wide-set eyes, the strong nose, the small firm mouth — modelled with the soft transitions of flesh over bone that connect her directly to the Madonna of Borgo San Lorenzo, just discussed, and to that of San Giorgio alla Costa, treated below: the same severe sister, one register more hieratic. And the Child is no abbreviation but a small solid body — the belly’s weight in the lap, the bare feet crossed at different depths, the head’s slight turn — a baby performing the Logos, which is the young Giotto’s formula exactly.

It is on this double register — archaic program, advanced substance — that the attribution rests. The panel entered the Giotto literature in the late twentieth century, advanced for the master’s own youth by a current of criticism in which Miklós Boskovits’s name is central, and placed around 1295–1300, in the immediate orbit of San Giorgio alla Costa: slightly more conservative in its apparatus, hence read by its sponsors as standing a step earlier or addressing a more traditional commission.

The proposal has found substantial following, and the panel has figured in the Giotto exhibitions of recent decades; but a work in private hands carries its condition of knowledge with it — autopsy is limited, the restoration history less publicly documented than a museum’s, and the prudent “attributed” of the caption is retained more firmly here than for any other work discussed here. Dissenting placements in the painter’s closest circle remain on the table, and the question folds, as always at this date, into the larger Assisi problem discussed in the section on the Assisi question below.

The state, as the images show it, is honourable: the gold ground worn and patched at the edges, the black of the mantle somewhat flattened by time — black pigments age unkindly — but the faces, the gold borders and the incised haloes well preserved. The panel’s service to the corpus is its position: between the ruined tenderness of Borgo San Lorenzo and the enthroned revolution of San Giorgio alla Costa, it holds the midpoint where the icon is still fully dressed and already fully alive — a Virgin crowned in the old manner, opening her hand in the new.

## The Assisi Question

In the literature, "the Giotto question" refers mainly to one problem: whether the twenty-eight scenes of the Legend of St Francis<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-francis-assisi" class="footnote"><sup>13</sup></a> on the lower walls of the nave of the Upper Church of San Francesco at Assisi are by Giotto. A second problem is tied to it. Two scenes of the Old Testament register above, Isaac Blessing Jacob and Isaac Rejecting Esau, differ sharply in quality and spatial conception from what precedes them, and the question is whether they are by the same painter. Both questions have to be discussed together, because each answer constrains the other.

First, what the sources say. Riccobaldo places Giotto in the Franciscan church at Assisi without further detail. Ghiberti says he painted "quasi tutta la parte di sotto". Only Vasari, in 1568, speaks explicitly of the "vita e fatti di San Francesco" in the Upper Church. He adds that Giotto was summoned by Fra Giovanni da Morrovalle<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-giovanni-da-morrovalle" class="footnote"><sup>14</sup></a>, minister general of the order from 1296 to 1304, and that he painted thirty-two stories, sixteen on each wall. In fact there are twenty-eight. The Treccani medieval encyclopedia suggests that Vasari, without checking on site, added to the Upper Church count four scenes from the left transept of the Lower Church, which may say something about how he worked from notes. From Vasari to the late eighteenth century the attribution was repeated without question.

The first published doubt, in 1791, was a remark that there was "più d'una ragione per dubitare che tutte le pitture sieno di Giotto". In 1821 a German critic rejected the cycle on stylistic grounds, and another soon went further and questioned the Paduan frescoes as well. Around the turn of the twentieth century a group of German and English writers took up the rejection. One of them, in 1912, called the Assisi frescoes apocryphal, dated them as late as the third decade of the Trecento, and contrasted their surface naturalism and loose spatial construction with the unified space of Padua.

Richard Offner's two-part essay "Giotto, non-Giotto" in the *Burlington Magazine* (June and September 1939) put this skepticism in its most influential form, and its title became shorthand for the whole debate. Offner addressed the Francis cycle and the Santa Maria Novella crucifix together. He argued that both attributions rested on a false idea of how Florentine painting developed in the fourteenth century, on sources that were themselves problematic, and on scholars who, out of habit or patriotism, had taken too much for granted.

His method was that of the connoisseur of a single personality. The Arena Chapel was the secure touchstone. If the Assisi legend could not be reconciled with it, then either the Assisi painter was not Giotto or Giotto's style was implausibly unstable. He preferred the first conclusion, and later extended his skepticism to create a separate *Master of the Santa Maria Novella Cross*.

His essay divided the field into a largely Italian camp that kept the attribution and a largely English-speaking camp, joined by much German-language criticism, that doubted it. A major American study of 1960, *Giotto and Assisi*, published by New York University Press from a lecture given at the university's Institute of Fine Arts, denied the Francis legend to Giotto while accepting the Isaac scenes as his. It produced the aphorism the field has never forgotten: if the Isaac Master is not Giotto, then he, not Giotto, is the founder of modern painting. From the Italian side, the Dizionario Biografico holds that almost everyone now accepts that conclusion, whatever they think of the identification.

The Isaac problem has a technical side as well as a stylistic one. The Treccani medieval encyclopedia notes that the Isaac scenes are where true fresco painted in *giornate*, the daily patches of fresh plaster, first appears at Assisi. It replaces the older method of painting in *pontate*, broad horizontal strips following the levels of the scaffold, used in the biblical scenes before them.

The name *Isaac Master* was established by an American scholar in studies of 1923 and 1932, which identified him with [Gaddo Gaddi](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Gaddo-Gaddi.html). Other candidates have included Cavallini and, in a series of studies from the 1980s, [Arnolfo di Cambio](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Arnolfo-di-Cambio.html). That proposal rests on the Isaac painter's architecturally coherent space and the sculptural, Arnolfian character of his figures, though no source mentions Arnolfo ever painting. The Arnolfo hypothesis also contains the sharpest statement of an internal contradiction that any attributionist has to answer.

If Giotto painted both the Isaac scenes and the first Francis scenes, why do the latter look like a step backward? The Gift of the Mantle and the Dream of the Palace show, in that line of criticism's phrase, "the uncertain stammering" of a painter still learning to handle the rediscovered space that the Isaac scenes command with ease. The conclusion drawn was that if Giotto is the author of the Francis legend, as is very likely, he cannot be the author of the Isaac scenes. Within that school, the young Giotto is placed instead among the painters of the last biblical scenes and the Vault of the Doctors.

The Italian attributionist tradition answers along two lines. Boskovits, in the Dizionario Biografico, made the continuity of the workshop the key argument. The Isaac scenes were not isolated. The painter who made them went on to paint much of the first bay of the nave himself: the landscape and the retreating Cain of the Killing of Abel, the well-head and the slow-moving brothers of Joseph Sold, the classical aedicule of Christ among the Doctors, the monumental Lamentation, and the daring foreshortenings of the sleeping soldiers in the Marys at the Tomb.

The stylistic kinship of these late biblical scenes with the Santa Maria Novella crucifix, documented as Giotto's by 1312, and with the fragmentary Madonna at Borgo San Lorenzo in the Mugello, near the family's origins, suggests that this leading painter was Giotto. On this reading the Francis legend continues the same workshop's activity, and technical study of the painting procedure has been taken to confirm close links between the earliest Francis scenes (II–VII) and the Isaac group.

Boskovits accepted that these scenes are less carefully drawn than the biblical ones but saw them as later, more mature and more experimental. Their buildings are shown at an angle rather than frontally, and their details are observed from life. He also noted that the popes in the legend resemble portraits of Boniface VIII, which would mean the cycle was not begun before 1294. For him the differences from Padua reflect the master's development over nearly a decade, not a different hand.

Bellosi's contribution was chronological and contextual as much as stylistic. *La pecora di Giotto* argued for reading the question against the broader history of art around 1290, including a redating of Cimabue's work at Assisi to the pontificate of Nicholas IV<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-nicholas-iv" class="footnote"><sup>15</sup></a> (1288–1292). It proposed that the Francis cycle was painted earlier than the traditional bracket of 1296–1304 derived from Vasari's reference to Giovanni da Morrovalle.

Iconographic evidence played a part, including how Francis's beard is handled in the Upper Church compared with works in the Lower Church and elsewhere. Bellosi also stressed how closely the scenes on the signed Louvre Stigmatization, from San Francesco at Pisa, match their counterparts at Assisi. Boskovits, for his part, insisted that these are not simple copies but replicas the author updated at will, which counts as further evidence of a single inventor. Bellosi also identified what he considered one of the most disconcerting commonplaces of Giotto scholarship: the notion that the pictures Giotto signed are not by Giotto.

Some points have come to command wide agreement, and the technical campaigns of the last sixty years have contributed to that. With isolated exceptions, a date deep into the fourteenth century for the Francis cycle and the idea of a long, drawn-out campaign have been abandoned. The legend is widely accepted as the design of a single artist who followed Roman practice in page layout and ornament. The study of *giornate* published in 1962 as *The Painting of the Life of St. Francis in Assisi* reconstructed the sequence of work from the sutures between the plaster patches.

It showed that the first scene, the Homage of a Simple Man, was painted last, by the hand responsible for the last scenes of the cycle, three or four (XXV/XXVI–XXVIII) according to the authors. A study of pictorial technique published in 1996 under the title *Il cantiere di Giotto* indicated that the cycle probably took no more than about two years. It found a strict division of labour: painters of non-figurative parts moved quickly across several scenes at once, while figure painters worked more slowly over a mural drawing prepared by the chief master.

That study, it must be added, drew conclusions that sit uneasily with a single-master reading. It distinguished three different workshops and gave a large share of the cycle to a team it associated with Pietro Cavallini. From about the sixteenth scene, the Death of the Knight of Celano, a new collaborator appears whose share grows until he paints the last scenes (XXV/XXVI–XXVIII, three or four according to the authors) and scene I alone. This assistant is often identified with the *[Master of the St Cecilia altarpiece](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Maestro-di-Santa-Cecilia.html)* in the Uffizi, and he may have been Gaddo Gaddi, whose son, according to Cennini, was Giotto's godson.

There is also broad agreement that several teams worked on the cycle and that its illusionistic framework is a genuine innovation. The painted colonnade of twisted columns supports an architrave whose foreshortened coffers and brackets are centred on the axis of each three-scene bay. The scenes seem to unfold behind the plane of the wall, and the painted space relates to the actual space of the nave. The iconographic programme follows Bonaventure<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-bonaventure" class="footnote"><sup>16</sup></a>'s *Legenda maior*, the official life of Francis from 1266, and the fading *tituli* beneath each scene are drawn from it.

No consensus has been reached on the question that matters most: whether the conceiving mind was Giotto's. The Treccani medieval encyclopedia observes that interventions limiting Giotto's role at Assisi became increasingly rare in the last quarter of the twentieth century. It adds that it is becoming ever harder not to identify the single guiding personality with Giotto without postulating another master of equal stature, active in the same years, who left no other trace.

That argument is strong, but it is an argument from economy, not proof. The skeptical position also has solid ground. The Arena Chapel, securely documented, conceives space differently, and the Assisi legend on the whole lacks the unified optical system of Padua. The written sources for Assisi are late or vague. The earliest unambiguous identification of the Francis legend as Giotto's comes from Vasari, more than two and a half centuries after the event. A fair assessment is that the attribution is probable and supported by most specialists, especially in Italy. It rests, however, on stylistic reconstruction, not on documents. Anyone who presents the Upper Church frescoes as simply "Giotto's Legend of St Francis" is stating a hypothesis as a fact.

## The Legend of Saint Francis: Giotto and the Upper Church of Assisi

Around the nave of the Upper Church of San Francesco at Assisi, beneath the windows and above the painted hangings of the dado, runs the most consequential fresco cycle of the Italian Middle Ages: the Legend of Saint Francis, twenty-eight scenes of the life, death and glory of the founder, composed from Bonaventure's *Legenda maior* — the order's official biography since 1266 — and from the appendix of *Miracula* attached to it, with each scene once explained by a painted Latin titulus drawn from the text, today surviving only in fragments. The cycle was conceived as the order's manifesto in its mother church: the sacred biography staged in the piazzas, courts, hillsides and sickrooms of a recognisable Italy, before witnesses who look on, doubt, sleep, misunderstand and — one in each scene, as it often seems — truly see. Its inventions, from the first portrait of a town square to the first reverse of a painting, founded the narrative art of the Trecento; the [Bardi Chapel](/Content/Churches/Cappella-Bardi.html) in Florence is its direct descendant, and Padua its disputed sibling.

The cycle is generally placed in the last years of the thirteenth century, under the pontificates between Nicholas IV, the first Franciscan pope, and Boniface VIII, with some scholars extending the work into the opening years of the fourteenth; the still-unfinished civic tower in the first scene is among the arguments for an early terminus. Its authorship is the celebrated "Assisi question", the central controversy of early Italian painting: the tradition that runs from Riccobaldo of Ferrara — writing while the painter lived — through Ghiberti and Vasari assigns the Legend to Giotto, and this remains the prevailing view of Italian scholarship; the line of criticism classically formulated in Richard Offner's "Giotto, non-Giotto" of 1939 finds the cycle irreconcilable with the documented Giotto of Padua and gives it to an anonymous master. The debate is set out in full in the section on the Assisi question above; each scene below names the problem where it bears and is attributed accordingly. Within the cycle itself, connoisseurship distinguishes several hands executing one governing design: the body of the Legend belongs to the principal workshop, while scene I and the last scenes of the cycle, three or four (XXV/XXVI–XXVIII) according to the authors, are assigned by most of the critics to the refined painter known as the Master of Saint Cecilia; the closing scenes are treated in the section on the epilogue of miracles below.

The table below gathers the cycle in a single view: the twenty-eight scenes in their narrative order, each with its subject, its approximate dimensions, its place on the walls of the nave, and the chapter of Bonaventure from which it is drawn — the *Legenda maior* for the life, the appended *Miracula* for the four posthumous wonders that close the sequence. The reading runs from the transept end of the north wall toward the entrance, crosses the two narrower fields flanking the portal, and returns along the south wall to the transept. Dimensions are conventional and the chapter numbering follows current usage, which varies slightly between editions; each scene is analysed in its own section below.

| No. | Title | Description | Dimensions | Location | Source |
| ----- | ------- | ------------- | ------------ | ---------- | -------- |
| I | The Homage of a Simple Man | A citizen spreads his cloak before the young Francis in the piazza of Assisi, beneath the Temple of Minerva | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall, 1st scene from the transept | *Legenda maior* I, 1 |
| II | The Gift of the Mantle to a Poor Knight | The first act of charity, between the two hills of the city of men and the house of God | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall | *Legenda maior* I, 2 |
| III | The Dream of the Palace of Arms | Christ shows the sleeping youth a palace hung with crossed shields | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall | *Legenda maior* I, 3 |
| IV | The Miracle of the Crucifix of San Damiano | The painted cross speaks in the ruined church: "go and repair my house" | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall | *Legenda maior* II, 1 |
| V | The Renunciation of Worldly Goods | Father and son divided by an empty centre, the hand of God above | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall | *Legenda maior* II, 4 |
| VI | The Dream of Innocent III | The Lateran totters, and the poor man props it on his shoulder | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall | *Legenda maior* III, 10 |
| VII | The Approval of the Rule | The scroll passes from Innocent III to Francis and his eleven companions | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall | *Legenda maior* III, 8–10 |
| VIII | The Vision of the Chariot of Fire | The absent founder crosses the night over his half-sleeping brethren: the new Elijah | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall | *Legenda maior* IV, 4 |
| IX | The Vision of the Thrones | Empty seats in heaven, the highest reserved for the humblest | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall | *Legenda maior* VI, 6 |
| X | The Exorcism of the Demons at Arezzo | Seven demons rout from the towered city at Brother Silvester's command | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall | *Legenda maior* VI, 9 |
| XI | The Trial by Fire before the Sultan | The ordeal offered at Damietta, the sultan's priests in retreat | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall | *Legenda maior* IX, 8 |
| XII | The Ecstasy of Saint Francis | The saint lifted on a shining cloud, Christ leaning from a dark heaven | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall | *Legenda maior* X, 4 |
| XIII | The Crib at Greccio | The first Christmas crib, seen from behind the choir screen | c. 270 × 230 cm | North wall, 13th scene, at the entrance end | *Legenda maior* X, 7 |
| XIV | The Miracle of the Spring | Water drawn from the rock for a thirsting man; the peasant drinks full-length | c. 270 × 200 cm | Entrance wall, left of the portal | *Legenda maior* VII, 12 |
| XV | The Sermon to the Birds | The bending saint and his smallest audience, near Bevagna | c. 270 × 200 cm | Entrance wall, right of the portal | *Legenda maior* XII, 3 |
| XVI | The Death of the Knight of Celano | Prophecy at a laid table: the host confesses and dies as the company sits down | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall, 1st scene from the entrance | *Legenda maior* XI, 4 |
| XVII | The Sermon before Honorius III | The inspired sermon in the cycle's most complete interior | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall | *Legenda maior* XII, 7 |
| XVIII | The Apparition at the Chapter of Arles | Francis appears, cruciform, while Anthony preaches on the Cross | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall | *Legenda maior* IV, 10 |
| XIX | The Stigmatization of Saint Francis | La Verna: the seraph-borne Christ and the five rays | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall | *Legenda maior* XIII, 3 |
| XX | The Death and Ascension of Saint Francis | Grief, liturgy and glory in three registers; one friar's eyes follow the soul | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall | *Legenda maior* XIV, 3–6 |
| XXI | The Apparition to Fra Agostino and to the Bishop of Assisi | The transit announced to the dying and to the dreaming; the vision withheld | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall | *Legenda maior* XIV, 6 |
| XXII | The Verification of the Stigmata | The knight Jerome's hand at the side wound, beneath the Porziuncola images | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall | *Legenda maior* XV, 4 |
| XXIII | Saint Clare Mourning Saint Francis | The cortège halts at San Damiano; the embrace of the two saints of Assisi | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall | *Legenda maior* XV, 5 |
| XXIV | The Canonization of Saint Francis | Gregory IX inscribes Francis among the saints at San Giorgio, 16 July 1228 | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall | *Legenda maior* XV, 7 |
| XXV | The Dream of Gregory IX | The stigmata sealed by a phial of blood delivered to the sleeping pope | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall | *Miracula*, De sacris stigmatibus, 2 |
| XXVI | The Healing of the Wounded Man of Lerida | The saint as night physician at a bed the doctors had abandoned | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall | *Miracula* I, 5 |
| XXVII | The Confession of the Woman Restored to Life | A soul returned for the width of one unspoken sentence; the demon flees | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall | *Miracula* II, 1 |
| XXVIII | The Liberation of Peter of Alife | The suspected heretic freed from his Roman tower; the saint airborne | c. 270 × 230 cm | South wall, 13th scene, at the transept end | *Miracula* V, 4 |

*Dimensions are approximate and conventional; the fields of the entrance wall are narrower than those of the long walls. Chapter numbering of the* Legenda maior *and of the appended* Miracula *follows current usage and varies slightly between editions.*

### The Homage of a Simple Man (Legend of Saint Francis, I)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-29.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing the young Francis, haloed and in a dark blue civilian mantle, walking through the main square of Assisi as a man kneels to spread his cloak on the ground before him. Behind rises the Roman temple of Minerva with six columns and a pediment containing a rose window, flanked by the civic tower at the left and a loggia-crowned building at the right. Two pairs of onlookers converse at the edges; a dark loss from a fallen lamp scars the blue sky.">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop (this scene assigned by most of the critics to the Master of Saint Cecilia), The Homage of a Simple Man in the Piazza of Assisi, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene I, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Legend of Saint Francis — the twenty-eight scenes that run around the nave of the Upper Church beneath the windows, and the most consequential fresco cycle of the Italian Middle Ages — begins not with a vision or a miracle but with a piece of street theatre. Bonaventure opens the *Legenda maior*, the cycle's textual source throughout, with the episode: a simple man of Assisi, meeting the young Francesco di Bernardone in the town square, spread his own cloak beneath the youth's feet, declaring that he was worthy of all reverence, for he would shortly do great things and be magnificently honoured by all the faithful.

The cycle thus opens under the sign of prophecy: before the renunciation, before the stigmata, before sanctity itself, an unlettered man sees what no one else can. And the painter has taken the theological point literally — Francis, still a layman in fashionable dress, his hair uncut, already wears the halo. The viewer, like the simple man, is asked to recognise the saint in the citizen.

What made the scene immediately famous, and keeps it famous, is where it happens. The square is the Piazza del Comune of Assisi, and the building at its centre is the Roman temple the town still possesses — the so-called Temple of Minerva, its six Corinthian columns and pediment rendered with an attention to a specific, standing, identifiable monument that has no real precedent in medieval painting. This is, by common consent, something very close to the first portrait of a place in Italian art.

It is not, however, a image: the painter has slimmed the columns, roofed the porch in red tile, filled the pediment with a Gothic rose window flanked by reliefs of angels, and generally baptised the pagan structure into the Christian present — the temple as thirteenth-century Assisi used and imagined it, not as archaeology would restore it. At the left rises the battlemented civic tower with its mullioned openings, shown — as has often been observed — still without its final crowning, a detail that has entered the dating debate since the actual tower was brought to completion only in the early years of the fourteenth century; at the right a building crowned by an airy wooden loggia closes the square. Between these three structures the town becomes a stage, and the civic space itself a witness to the prophecy.

The figure composition has the lapidary economy that governs the whole cycle. Francis advances from the left, slightly larger than life against the temple steps, his right hand opened outward in a gesture of mild, almost puzzled acceptance; the simple man kneels at the lower right, both hands stretching the cloak flat upon the pavement with the care of a servant preparing a king's path. Two pairs of commentators frame the event — at the left, two young men in red caps exchange glances and gestures over the strange scene; at the right, two older citizens, one in the red of civic dignity, mirror them. Their conversation is the picture's second subject: the town deciding what to make of its most unaccountable son. The symmetry of the witnesses, the frontal temple, the measured intervals between the figures give the opening scene the character of a solemn overture — deliberately more ceremonial, more static, than the narrative turbulence to come.

The scene's place in the cycle's internal history is delicate, for the Legend is the most disputed enterprise in all of Italian painting. The attribution of the cycle to Giotto rests on an old and weighty tradition — Riccobaldo of Ferrara noted Giotto's work in the basilica while the painter was alive, Ghiberti and Vasari consolidated the claim — and it remains the prevailing view of Italian scholarship; against it stands the line of criticism, classically formulated in Richard Offner's "Giotto, non-Giotto" of 1939, which finds the Legend irreconcilable with the documented Giotto of Padua and assigns it to a distinct, anonymous master with a large workshop.

That debate is set out in the section on the Assisi question above. Within the cycle itself connoisseurship has long distinguished several hands, and on one point most critics agree: scene I, together with the last scenes of the cycle, three or four (XXV/XXVI–XXVIII) according to the authors, is assigned to the refined painter known as the Master of Saint Cecilia — active at the two ends of the cycle, and perhaps the man who brought the enterprise to completion. The elongated elegance of the figures, the pale blond tonality, the ornamental precision of the architecture distinguish this fresco from the broader, graver manner of the scenes that follow, and the attentive visitor can measure the difference within a few steps of the nave.

The cycle is generally placed in the last years of the thirteenth century, in the pontificates between Nicholas IV — the first Franciscan pope, whose patronage reoriented the decoration of the basilica — and Boniface VIII, with some scholars extending the work into the opening years of the fourteenth; the unfinished civic tower in this very scene is among the arguments for an early terminus. Beneath the scene, as beneath each of the twenty-eight, ran a painted Latin titulus drawn from the *Legenda maior*, identifying the event for the literate; these inscriptions survive today only in fragmentary state.

The fresco shares the long conservation history of the Upper Church, most dramatically the earthquake of 26 September 1997, whose destructions however fell principally upon the vaults; the Legend itself came through, and the dark scar in the sky of this scene — the shadow of a hanging lamp burnt into the plaster — is among the oldest wounds the picture carries, almost a relic of the basilica's seven centuries of continuous use.

The scene is a threshold in a double sense: it opens the Legend, and it opens the problem — the Assisi question — that every study of Giotto must traverse. Whoever painted it invented something that the Bardi Chapel, a generation later, still remembered: the sacred story staged in the recognisable, inhabited spaces of an Italian town, before the eyes of citizens who look on and judge.

### The Gift of the Mantle to a Poor Knight (Legend of Saint Francis, II)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-30.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing the young haloed Francis, in a blue-grey tunic, handing his golden mantle to a poor knight in a red robe who grasps it with both hands. Francis's grey horse stands at the left with a red saddle cloth, lowering its head to graze. Behind, two rocky hills descend in a V toward the saint: a walled city with towers and a gate crowns the left summit, a church with a campanile the right">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, Saint Francis Giving His Mantle to a Poor Knight, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene II, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The second scene of the Legend follows Bonaventure into the countryside. Riding outside the town — the *Legenda maior* places the episode in the days when Francis was still the ambitious son of a merchant, dreaming of knighthood — he met a knight, noble but reduced to poverty and shamefully clad, and, overcome by compassion, stripped off his own garment on the spot and clothed the man. Bonaventure frames the gesture as a double homage, to poverty and to courtesy, and the cycle places it as the saint's first act: the Legend's opening scene showed Francis receiving honour he had not yet earned; the second shows him beginning to earn it, with the instinctive liberality that would become, transfigured, the rule of his order. The exchange of the cloak also carries an older echo that no medieval viewer would miss — Martin of Tours dividing his mantle with the beggar — and announces Francis, from the outset, as the new knight of a different chivalry.

The event itself is staged with the fewest possible elements: a man giving, a man receiving, and a horse. Francis, haloed and still in secular dress, holds the folded golden mantle out with both hands; the knight, in worn red, grasps it eagerly, his bearded face lifted to the giver with an expression in which need and astonishment are not yet gratitude. The two figures lean slightly toward one another, the mantle slung between them like a bridge, and the composition closes around the transaction with such concentration that the gift becomes the geometric as well as the moral centre of the picture. Behind Francis the horse — one of the earliest convincingly observed animals of Italian painting — lowers its head to crop the grass, indifferent to the drama: a touch of creaturely realism that measures the distance between this workshop and the emblematic animals of the Byzantine tradition, and that generations of visitors have remembered as vividly as the miracle scenes.

The setting, however, is the fresco's true novelty, and the counterpart of the urban portrait that precedes it. Two rocky hills descend from the upper corners in converging diagonals, and their meeting point is the head of the saint — landscape itself conscripted into the composition's rhetoric, so that the valley seems to open for the act of charity performed in it. On the left summit stands a walled town, gate, towers and houses pressed within its circuit, which an old and attractive tradition reads as Assisi itself seen from the plain; on the right, alone on its hill, a small church with a campanile, in which commentators have liked to recognise a monastery or even San Damiano.

Whether or not the identifications hold — and they are tradition rather than fact — the division is eloquent: the city of men on one side, the house of God on the other, and Francis between them, at the moment of turning from the first toward the second. After the piazza of Scene I, this is, by equal right, among the first true landscapes of Western painting: not gold ground, not abstract rock-shelf, but a describable, walkable terrain with trees rooted in its slopes.

Within the cycle's divided workshop this scene belongs, by common consent, not to the refined Master of Saint Cecilia of the opening field but to the principal hand of the Legend — the broader, graver manner that the Italian tradition calls Giotto and the sceptical line calls the Master of the Saint Francis Cycle; the fuller modelling of the two heads, the weight of the bodies within their draperies, and the structural use of the landscape are its signature.

The attributional question, with its stakes and its literature, is discussed in the section on the Assisi question above and in the account of the opening scene. The dating follows the cycle as a whole, in the last years of the thirteenth century and perhaps the first of the fourteenth.

The surface has paid the price of its position and its technique. The blue of the sky, applied *a secco* over the fresco ground as throughout the cycle, has largely fallen, leaving the mottled grey-green that now stands for air; losses dapple the saint's tunic and the horse's flank, and the fragmentary state of the painted titulus below is shared with the rest of the series. The scene came through the earthquake of 1997, whose gravest destructions struck the vaults rather than the nave walls. What endures, beneath the wear, is the thing the whole Legend was built to do: the sacred biography told as something that happened on a real road, between real hills, to a young man whose horse, meanwhile, bent its neck to graze.

### The Dream of the Palace of Arms (Legend of Saint Francis, III)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-31.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing the young Francis asleep, haloed, in a canopied bed whose wooden frame is drawn in perspective; Christ, with a cross-inscribed halo, leans over the sleeper and points with his raised arm toward a tall fantastic palace at the right. The palace rises in storeys of Gothic triple arches hung with shields and banners, above a two-storey classical loggia; the sky behind has lost its blue">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Dream of the Palace Full of Arms, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene III, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The third scene of the Legend answers the second within a single night. Bonaventure relates that after clothing the poor knight, Francis — still dreaming of military glory — was shown in his sleep a splendid and spacious palace filled with arms, all of them marked with the sign of the cross; and when he asked for whom they were destined, he was told that they were for him and for his knights. He woke convinced that a great earthly campaign awaited him, and only later understood that the vision spoke of a spiritual militia — the misread prophecy being, for Bonaventure, the very proof of its divine origin, since God condescends to a young man's vocabulary before correcting it. The cycle, following the *Legenda maior*, sets the dream immediately after the gift of the mantle, as reward and as summons: the act of courtesy performed on the road is answered, that night, by the offer of a greater knighthood.

The painter's problem was the oldest in sacred narrative — how to show a man and his dream at once — and the solution adopted here is of a beautiful, confident archaism. There is no chamber: the canopied bed stands in the open, its wooden tester drawn as a transparent cage of beams whose receding edges are among the cycle's most striking essays in empirical perspective, and beside it the dream itself rises full-scale into the picture, occupying the right half of the field as solidly as the temple of Minerva occupies Scene I.

Between the two worlds stands their interpreter: Christ, making here his first appearance in the cycle, leans over the sleeping youth — who, alone in the Legend, is shown asleep yet haloed, sanctity keeping watch while the man rests — and flings his arm toward the palace in a gesture that is at once revelation and command. The sleeper does not see; the viewer sees for him. No frame, cloud or mandorla separates vision from reality, and the fresco trusts the beholder, as the dream trusted Francis, to tell the two apart.

The palace is the most extravagant architectural invention of the entire cycle, and it is built — with evident deliberateness — in two registers of meaning. Above, a tower-like structure of superimposed Gothic loggias, triple pointed arches on slender colonnettes crowned by a projecting battlemented balcony, within whose openings hang the arms of the prophecy: shields and banners charged with the cross, the armory of the future militia glimpsed between the columns as if the building itself wore them.

Below, a two-storey loggia of a wholly different, classicising vocabulary — round-headed openings, Cosmatesque-flavoured friezes, an architecture of ancient Rome rather than of chivalric fantasy. The dream-palace thus speaks both languages of the basilica that contains it, the Gothic of the new order and the Romanitas of the papacy that protected it; and the arms in its windows convert a nobleman's castle into a reliquary of crosses. Whether the painter intended so programmatic a reading may be left open, but the duality of the structure is on the wall, and it has long exercised the scene's commentators.

In the division of hands within the Legend this field belongs to the principal workshop — the manner that enters with Scene II and carries the body of the cycle — rather than to the Master of Saint Cecilia of the opening; within that workshop, analysis of the plaster and of execution has suggested the usual collaboration of several executants on cartoon-like designs of one mind. The larger question of that mind's name — Giotto for the tradition that runs from Riccobaldo through Ghiberti and Vasari to the prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the line descending from Offner — is treated in the section on the Assisi question above, and nothing in the present scene settles it; what the scene does display is the designing intelligence common to the whole: the dream staged with the same tectonic seriousness as the piazza and the hillside, the supernatural admitted into the world without any slackening of the world's structure.

The condition is that of the cycle's upper tier of misfortunes rather than its worst: the *a secco* blue of the sky has fallen almost entirely, leaving the mottled dark ground against which the palace now stands with an unintended, almost nocturnal splendour — not inappropriate, visitors have often felt, to a dream; abrasions dapple the bedclothes and the Christ, and the titulus below survives in the fragmentary state common to the series.

The scene passed through the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. Its afterlife is worth noting at the close: when Giotto — or his memory — returned to the theme of the sleeping dreamer and the standing vision, in the Stefaneschi predella's antecedents and above all in the visions of the Bardi Chapel's last wall, the grammar invented here, the dream as solid as the bed, was still the one in use.

### The Miracle of the Crucifix of San Damiano (Legend of Saint Francis, IV)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-32.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing the young haloed Francis kneeling in prayer inside the ruined church of San Damiano, shown in section: the roof is broken open to bare rafters, the walls are cracked, and the apse with its green dome shelters a large painted crucifix of Duecento type standing on the altar. A marble parapet with Cosmatesque panels closes the church at the left.">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, Saint Francis Praying before the Crucifix of San Damiano, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene IV, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

With the fourth scene the Legend reaches the turning point of the conversion, and the cycle its first speaking image. Bonaventure relates that Francis, praying one day before the painted crucifix in the half-abandoned church of San Damiano below Assisi, heard the voice of Christ come from the panel, three times:

> "Francis, go and repair my house, which, as you see, is falling wholly into ruin.”

The young man took the words at their letter and began hauling stones; only afterwards — Bonaventure is explicit on the hermeneutic lesson, the second such lesson in as many scenes — did he understand that the house to be rebuilt was the universal Church. The fresco holds both readings in suspension, and it does so by architectural means: the building is the protagonist, and its wounds are the text.

For the painter’s standing problem — how to show an interior — this scene offers the cycle’s most radical solution, because here the section is not a convention but the subject itself. San Damiano is displayed cut open, and the cut is indistinguishable from the ruin: the roof gapes to its bare rafters, the walls break off in jagged, crumbling profiles, the little gabled bell-cote hangs over a void. The device of the earlier scenes — the building opened for our eyes — has fused with the narrative condition of the building.

It is a conceit of real wit, and of real theological force: the church is ruined precisely so that we may see inside it the praying man who will repair it. Within, the essentials of a sacred interior survive intact amid the decay — the apse with its green-toned conch, the altar on its single foot, the marble parapet with Cosmatesque roundels that screens the choir at the left — an inventory of what is worth saving, drawn with the same documentary affection as the temple of Minerva three bays away.

On the altar stands the picture within the picture: a great painted cross of the Duecento type, its apron and terminals clearly described, leaning slightly forward on its support as such crosses did. The quotation is specific. The venerable crucifix of San Damiano — the very panel of the legend, a twelfth-century work of Umbrian tradition — survived and survives, kept since the thirteenth century by the Clares and today in Santa Clara’s basilica in Assisi; and the fresco’s cross, while not a literal copy, is unmistakably a portrait of its kind: the broad-shouldered living Christ of the older iconography, not the slumped dead Christ that [Giunta Pisano](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Giunta-Pisano.html) had introduced and that the Franciscans themselves had propagated.

The painter of the Legend, working at the end of the century, thus reached back an era to characterise the object — an archaeology of the image that tells the viewer, in the language of style, that this happened long ago, before the new painting; a remarkable instance of a medieval workshop historicising its own medium. Francis kneels before it in strict profile, still in secular dress, hands joined, the halo already his; the composition gives him the scale of a donor figure and the cross the scale of an apparition, and between them passes, invisibly, the sentence that founded the order.

Within the Legend’s program the scene is a hinge that the designer exploited with evident foresight. The ruined church that Francis is commanded to repair will return, transfigured, two scenes later, when Innocent III<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-innocent-iii" class="footnote"><sup>17</sup></a> dreams of the Lateran basilica tottering and of the little poor man shouldering it: the physical ruin of San Damiano and the allegorical ruin of the Lateran are the two panels of a single diptych, the literal and the spiritual sense of one command.

Such long-range correspondences — built across the bays of the nave as the thematic pairs of the Bardi Chapel would later be built across a chapel — are among the strongest arguments that a single designing intelligence governed the whole cycle, whatever the number of hands; the name of that intelligence, Giotto by the tradition that descends from Riccobaldo and Ghiberti, an anonymous master for the Offnerian line, is the Assisi question discussed in the section on the Assisi question above.

The condition is comparatively kind. The *a secco* blue of the sky has fallen, as throughout the series, leaving its mottled dark substitute; losses spot the saint’s rose-coloured garment, and the green of the apse conch may itself owe something to the alteration or loss of an original azurite layer — a point for the technical literature. The titulus below survives in fragments. The scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural harm, and remains, for the history of painting, one of the cycle’s quietest and most consequential inventions: an interior made of absence, a voice made visible as an object, and the conversion of a man staged as the survey of a building.

### The Renunciation of Worldly Goods (Legend of Saint Francis, V)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-33.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing the young Francis, half-naked and haloed, raising his hands toward the hand of God that emerges from the sky at the top of the scene, while the Bishop of Assisi wraps his mantle around him at the right, flanked by clerics. At the left his father, in a yellow robe with the renounced clothes over his arm, is restrained by a citizen; behind him press the townspeople, with two children at the lower corner. Two buildings frame the empty centre: a palazzo with an external staircase at the left, the bishop’s residence with a classical loggia at the right">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, Saint Francis Renouncing His Worldly Goods, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene V, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The fifth scene brings the conversion to its public crisis, and the Legend to one of its most celebrated inventions. Bonaventure relates that Francis, summoned before Bishop Guido of Assisi to answer his father’s demand for the return of his goods, did not wait for words: he stripped off all his clothing, down to the last garment, laid it at Pietro di Bernardone’s feet with the money upon it, and declared that from now on he could say in full truth “Our Father who art in heaven” — for Pietro di Bernardone was his father no longer.

The bishop, weeping, covered the naked man with his own mantle. The fresco stages the sentence rather than the gesture: Francis has already given everything back, and what the picture fixes is the instant of the new filiation — the saint’s raised hands, his face thrown upward, and, at the top of the field, breaking through the sky, the answering hand of God, blessing. The prayer and its addressee are both on the wall; no other scene of the cycle makes the vertical axis so literally theological.

The composition divides the world in two, and the division has become a textbook of narrative painting. At the left, the party of the earth: Bernardone, in merchant’s yellow, the repudiated garments heaped over his arm, lunges forward and is held — a fellow citizen grips his arm, and the grip tells us the rage is physical; around him the men of the commune crowd in close ranks of caps and profiles, and at their feet two children watch, one gathering the hem of his tunic in the gesture that an old tradition reads as the pocketing of stones to throw at the madman.

At the right, the party of heaven: the bishop, mitred, enfolds Francis in his blue mantle with the slightly stooped care of a man sheltering something newly born, and behind him the clergy close ranks as the citizens do opposite. Between the two groups the centre of the picture is left empty — a column of blank sky descending from the hand of God to the pavement — and across that charged void father and son do not look at each other. The device, perfected here, is the one the Bardi Chapel lunette would distill a generation later: there the void remains empty, absolute, and the divine hand is withdrawn; the comparison of the two versions, scene against scene, is among the most instructive exercises the Giotto question affords, and the two scenes may be read side by side.

The architecture performs the same division as the figures. Over the civic party rises a palazzo with an external staircase climbing to a columned loggia — the machinery of communal life, rendered with the cycle’s usual appetite for carpentry and Cosmatesque inlay; over the ecclesiastical party stands a building read since early times as the bishop’s residence, its lower block crowned by a classical tribune and a columned aedicula, the vocabulary of Romanitas that the cycle reserves for the Church. Both structures are cut by the frame, and both lean slightly inward, so that the city itself seems to take sides. That neither building can be securely matched to the standing fabric of Assisi — unlike the temple of Scene I — matters less than their rhetoric: this is topography of allegiance, not of survey.

The scene belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, whose manner entered with Scene II; the breadth of the drapery masses, the dense packing of the heads, and the structural use of the architecture are its hallmarks, and analysis of the giornate has shown here, as throughout, several executants working to a single design. The designing mind — Giotto for the tradition descending from Riccobaldo, Ghiberti and Vasari and for the prevailing Italian scholarship; an anonymous master for the line that descends from Offner — is the standing question of the Legend, discussed in the section on the Assisi question above. What belongs to the design, on any attribution, is the long-range architecture of the program: the renunciation stands fifth in the sequence as the hinge between the secular youth and the religious foundation, and its answering scene, across the span of the cycle, is the saint’s death in nakedness on the bare ground — the poverty chosen here kept to the end.

The surface carries the cycle’s habitual wounds with one addition of its own. The *a secco* blue has fallen from the sky, leaving the dark mottled ground against which the buildings now stand; and the hand of God at the summit, painted like the blue in the more fragile technique, survives damaged and is easily missed by the hurried visitor — a loss that mutilates the scene’s meaning more than its beauty, since without the hand the saint’s gesture loses its addressee. Losses spot the figures, the titulus below is fragmentary, and the field passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural harm. What endures undiminished is the dramatic construction: the first picture in Italian art to build its story on an emptiness, and to make a family’s rupture legible at fifty paces as two crowds, two buildings, and a silence between them.
