### The Dream of Innocent III (Legend of Saint Francis, VI)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-34.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing Pope Innocent III asleep in a canopied bed hung with a patterned golden curtain, wearing his mitre, while two attendants sit drowsing at the bedside. At the left the Lateran basilica, with its portico and tall campanile, tilts perilously toward collapse, and the haloed Saint Francis, in the brown habit of his order, braces it upright on his shoulder; small statues crown the rooftops and the campanile.">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Dream of Innocent III, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene VI, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The sixth scene carries the Legend from Assisi to Rome, and from the saint’s conversion to the Church’s recognition of it. Bonaventure relates that when Francis and his first companions came to Rome to seek approval for their way of life, Innocent III hesitated — until a dream decided him: he saw the Lateran basilica, mother and head of all churches, leaning to its fall, and a poor man, small and of mean appearance, propping it with his own back so that it should not collapse.

Waking, the pope recognised in Francis the man of the dream, and received him. The fresco is thus the second dream of the cycle and the deliberate completion of a diptych whose first panel hangs two bays away: at San Damiano a ruined country church and a voice commanding its repair, understood at first in the letter; here the ruin returns as allegory, the Lateran standing for the Church universal, and the repair as the founding of the order. The literal and the spiritual sense of one command face each other across the nave, and the viewer who walks between them performs Bonaventure’s exegesis with his feet.

The composition sets the two halves of the miracle side by side with the same confident grammar as the Dream of the Palace: no cloud, no frame, no diminution divides the sleeper from what he sees. At the right, the papal bedchamber — the canopied bed on its slender colonnettes, the great curtain of patterned gold drawn behind it like a cloth of honour, and the pope asleep in full dignity, mitred, his head on his hand, the sleep of office rather than of abandon. At his bedside two attendants sit on the step, slumped into their mantles, dozing — the little naturalistic counterpoint the cycle loves: around the vision, ordinary tiredness.

At the left, the dream itself, built full-scale: the basilica with its colonnaded portico, its gabled porch, its tall arcaded campanile of recognisably Roman type, all heeled over at an angle that the eye feels as imminent catastrophe; and under the slipping architrave, planted like a column, Francis — now for the first time in the cycle wearing the habit of his order, corded at the waist — takes the building’s weight on his bent shoulder and neck, his face turned calmly upward into the fall. The conceit is impossible and the painting makes it structural: the tilt of the basilica and the counter-diagonal of the saint’s braced body lock together like members of one truss, so that the picture itself performs the statics it narrates.

The architecture, as always in the Legend, is characterised rather than surveyed. The leaning church is given the insignia of the Lateran — the portico, the campanile, the Romanitas of cornices and Cosmatesque banding — without being a portrait of it; what mattered was that a Roman viewer, and the papal court above all, should recognise the mother church in peril and the Umbrian beggar as its prop. The political theology could not be plainer, nor more audacious for a mendicant order to display in its own mother house: the papacy itself, in the person of its most imperious pope, dreaming that it cannot stand without Francis. That the cycle was painted under popes — from the Franciscan Nicholas IV onward — who had made the basilica of Assisi a showcase of Roman allegiance gives the scene its institutional frame, discussed in the section on the Assisi question above.

The scene belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, and connoisseurship has long noted within it the designing habits that run through the whole: the interior opened in section, the dream admitted bodily into the field, the architecture conscripted into the drama. It carries a particular cross-reference: the same subject, in a composition of the same family, appears in the predella of the signed Stigmatization from Pisa now in the Louvre — treated below — where the Assisi invention returns in portable form; the relation between the two, replica, derivation or common source, is one of the load-bearing arguments in the attribution debate, Boskovits and the Italian line reading the panel as Giotto’s own reprise of his Assisi design, the Offnerian line as evidence of exactly the opposite. Few images in the cycle sit closer to the heart of the Assisi question.

The condition is, by the standards of the series, fortunate: the bedchamber and its gold curtain preserve their *a secco* finishes better than most fields, while the sky has suffered the habitual fall of the blue, leaving the dark green-mottled ground against which the small statues crowning the buildings — the figured *acroteria* with which the cycle likes to finish its Roman architecture. The titulus below is fragmentary, and the field passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. What endures entire is the invention, one of the most consequential in the cycle: the dream that founded an order’s legitimacy, painted as a problem in load-bearing — and solved by a man.

### The Approval of the Rule (Legend of Saint Francis, VII)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-35.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing Pope Innocent III standing at the right, in a red cope and white mitre, handing the scroll of the Rule to the kneeling, haloed Saint Francis, who receives it with covered and open hands at the head of eleven kneeling, tonsured companions. Mitred prelates stand behind the pope and a mitred prelate in a gold-patterned cope sits at the right. The audience hall is hung with a richly patterned curtain beneath an arcaded gallery on carved corbels, under a blue ceiling">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, Innocent III Approving the Rule of Saint Francis, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene VII, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The seventh scene concludes the Roman diptych begun with the pope’s dream: Innocent III, persuaded by the vision of the Lateran upheld, receives Francis and his companions and approves their way of life. Bonaventure places the approval of 1209 or 1210 in the register of speech — the pope approved the rule by word of mouth, promised more when the brothers should have grown, and sent them out to preach penance — but the fresco gives the promise its documentary form: a scroll passes from the pope into the hands of the kneeling saint, and the companions behind him already wear the tonsure, which the sources attach to this same Roman sojourn.

The picture thus compresses audience, approval and clericalisation into one act of delivery, and the old painted titulus beneath the scene recorded precisely this double grace — the rule conceded and the preaching of penance entrusted. For the order that commissioned the basilica, no image mattered more: it is the charter scene, the moment the movement became an institution, hung on the wall of the institution’s own mother church.

The staging marks a quiet revolution in the cycle. For the first time the Legend abandons the open air and the sectioned building altogether: the event takes place inside, in a hall that encloses the beholder along with the court. A great curtain of patterned roundels seals the lower zone like a cloth of honour stretched the full width of the field; above it runs an arcaded gallery on carved corbels, seen from below and from within, its round arches backed by the blue of a ceiling rather than a sky.

The space is shallow, ceremonial, airless by design — a Roman interior as liturgy, against which the earlier scenes’ piazzas and hillsides measure how wide the cycle’s registers range. Within it the composition is a diagonal balance of two societies: at the left the new order, twelve poor men kneeling in two dense files — Francis and eleven companions, the apostolic number made visible, brown habit against brown habit, all profile and attention; at the right the old hierarchy, the pope in his red cope with mitred prelates standing close behind him and, seated at the right in a gold-figured cope, a mitred prelate in whom the account of the Legenda maior would invite us to recognise either Cardinal Giovanni di San Paolo, the order’s first advocate at the Curia, or Bishop Guido of Assisi, who had brought Francis to him — though the fresco itself declines to say — textile against sackcloth, verticals against kneeling curves, and between the two the scroll, the smallest object in the picture and its entire subject.

The psychology is distributed with the Legend’s habitual economy. Innocent leans forward, both hands engaged — one delivering the document, the other raised in blessing — the imperious pope of history caught in an act of condescension that the dream two bays away has explained; Francis receives the scroll with hands covered by his habit in the ancient gesture of reverence for a sacred object, his face lifted to the giver. Behind him the companions’ tonsured heads repeat and vary like a chant; behind the pope the court watches with the guarded faces of men witnessing a precedent. Nothing moves except the document, and the fresco’s gravity lies exactly there: the Legend’s miracles elsewhere split skies and raise the dead; here the miracle is juridical, and the painter has found for it a pictorial language of equivalent solemnity.

The scene asks to be read against two relatives. The first is its own descendant in the Bardi Chapel, where the right-wall lunette shows the Rule’s approval — in most readings the later, written confirmation by Honorius III<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-pope-honorius-iii" class="footnote"><sup>18</sup></a> of 1223 — reorganised by the mature Giotto into a deeper, aedicula-framed space: set side by side, the two versions stage the verbal promise and the sealed bull, and the stylistic distance between them is the whole argument of the Assisi question in miniature.

The second is the predella of the signed Stigmatization from Pisa in the Louvre, where this composition returns in portable form among the three Assisi-derived scenes — replica of his own invention for the Italian line of scholarship, derivation from another’s for the Offnerian; the stakes of that disagreement are set out in the section on the Assisi question above. On any account, the scene belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, several hands executing one design, and the design’s intelligence — an institution portrayed as an interior, a foundation portrayed as a handover — is nowhere clearer.

The condition is among the kindest in the series: the curtain and the figures preserve their finishes well, and the losses concentrate, as usual, in the *a secco* blue of the ceiling, now fallen to its dark substrate across much of the upper zone, and in scattered abrasions along the lower border where the titulus survives in fragments. The field passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural harm. It remains the cycle’s great image of legitimacy — and the proof that the workshop which painted storms of grief and toppling basilicas could also paint, with undiminished power, the stillness of an act of government.

### The Vision of the Chariot of Fire (Legend of Saint Francis, VIII)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-36.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing Saint Francis in prayer, in profile against a great radiating aureole, borne through the night sky on a red chariot drawn by a rearing red horse. Below, a hut shown in section shelters a group of friars, some asleep and some waking in wonder, while at the right three friars stand outside, one flinging up his arm toward the apparition. A pale building complex rises at the upper left against the deep blue sky">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Vision of the Flaming Chariot, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene VIII, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The eighth scene gives the young brotherhood its first collective vision, and the Legend its most openly prophetic image. Bonaventure relates that while the brothers were gathered in their poor shelter — the tradition places it at Rivotorto, below Assisi — and Francis was absent, praying apart, about midnight a chariot of fire of marvellous brightness entered through the door of the hut and wheeled about the room, surmounted by a globe of light like the sun; and the brothers, some waking and some roused from sleep, understood that it was the soul of their father, present among them in spirit.

The typology is declared rather than implied: as the fiery chariot bore up Elijah, so it manifests the new Elijah, and as Elisha inherited the prophet’s spirit, so — the lesson runs — will the sons inherit the father’s. The fresco takes Bonaventure one step further in explicitness, as the cycle likes to do with visions: where the text speaks of a chariot and a globe of light understood as Francis’s soul, the painting sets the saint himself within the globe: a vast disc of finely spun radial striations, denser and darker toward its brown rim, fills the night sky like a woven sun, and Francis rides across it in profile, hands joined, his own gold halo distinct within the greater light.

The apparition is the most purely fantastic invention the cycle had yet to attempt, and the workshop met it with a striking archaeological literalism. The chariot is no cloud-vehicle but a built thing: a two-wheeled car of reddish wood, its side panel worked as an antique relief — a moulded, gilt-edged field filled with a dense crowding of small figures and scrolls in the manner of a Roman sarcophagus front, declaring the sculpted *currus* of the triumphs without resolving into any single legible scene, its spoked wheels described down to the hubs — Elijah’s chariot imagined through the Roman triumph, as befits a basilica whose decoration everywhere speaks the language of Romanitas.

The horse, riderless and without ground, rears into the blue with a vehemence that the painter of the grazing mount in Scene II has deliberately inverted: there the animal’s lowered neck anchored the world’s ordinariness, here its flung forelegs declare that the laws of that world are suspended. Horse, car and saint ride a diagonal across the upper field, and the deep blue of the night — better preserved in this scene than in most of the series — still carries something of the nocturne the designer intended.

Below, the composition divides the witnesses with the cycle’s habitual intelligence into two states of seeing. At the left the hut is opened in section — the device of the Legend’s interiors, here at its most diagrammatic — and within its pink cage of piers the friars who had gone to rest are caught between sleep and waking: one still slumped on his block of a pillow, others lifting their heads, the vision arriving to them as it arrives in Bonaventure, half dream and half light through the door. At the right, in the open, three friars who had been keeping vigil stand and see plainly: one throws his whole arm up along the diagonal of the chariot — the gesture that stitches earth to sky and tells the viewer where to look — while his companions press together, faces lifted. Waking sight and interrupted sleep, vigil and rest: the scene is a little treatise on the modes of vision, and the sectioned hut is its instrument.

The field belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, within the stretch of the cycle where its broad, grave manner runs most evenly; the design — the diagonal apparition over the divided witnesses — is of the same mind that built the dreams of Scenes III and VI, and the long-range architecture of the program gives the vision its place: it stands among the scenes of the order’s founding as the pledge that the founder’s spirit, like Elijah’s, would not depart with him, a promise the cycle will redeem at the far end of the nave when the friars see Francis’s soul carried to heaven at his death. The name of the designing mind — Giotto for the tradition and for prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the Offnerian line — is the standing question of the Legend, discussed in the section on the Assisi question above.

The condition is, for once, generous in the upper zone, where the blue survives in large part and the aureole keeps its radiance, and harder-worn below, with abrasions across the friars’ habits and the pink architecture; the titulus beneath is fragmentary, as throughout the series, and the scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. It remains one of the cycle’s most quietly audacious images: a miracle of presence-in-absence, staged as a Roman triumph crossing a Umbrian night, over the heads of men divided — like the picture’s own beholders — between those who sleep and those who watch.

### The Vision of the Thrones (Legend of Saint Francis, IX)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-37.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing five empty golden thrones draped with cloths of honour floating against a deep blue sky, the central one taller and set on a stepped pedestal. Below, an angel in red and green descends pointing toward Saint Francis, who kneels in prayer before the altar of a small church rendered in section, a lamp hanging on chains above the altar cross; at the lower left a companion friar looks up with raised hands, witnessing the vision">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Vision of the Thrones in Heaven, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene IX, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The ninth scene turns from the order’s public foundation to the secret economy of its founder’s soul. Bonaventure relates that while Francis prayed in a deserted church, a companion of proven holiness, rapt in ecstasy, saw many thrones in heaven, and among them one more glorious than the rest, adorned with every splendour; and as he wondered whose it might be, he heard a voice: this seat had belonged to one of the fallen angels, and was now reserved for the humble Francis.

Coming to himself, the friar asked the saint — by way of test — what he thought of himself, and received the answer the vision had already sealed: that he held himself the greatest of sinners. The episode is Bonaventure’s parable of humility exalted, built on the oldest of reversals — the throne forfeited by Lucifer’s pride restored to the poor man’s self-abasement — and the cycle gives it the sixth chapter’s structural place: after the chariot of fire had shown the brothers their father’s spirit, the thrones show them, and us, his destination.

The composition is the Legend’s purest diagram of vision, and its most daring use of emptiness since the Renunciation. The upper third of the field belongs wholly to the apparition: five thrones of goldsmith’s Gothic — pinnacled backs, draped cloths of honour, the central seat raised on a stepped and ornamented pedestal, taller, redder, more jewelled than its companions — stand on nothing, against a blue that here, by rare fortune, survives in much of its original depth, so that the thrones truly float in night as the designer intended. They are empty, and their emptiness is the entire narrative: the picture’s subject is a reservation, a place kept. Where the text says “many,” the painter says five, and the reduction is rhetoric — four equal seats to establish the norm, one exalted seat to break it, the hierarchy legible at the distance of the nave floor.

Between heaven and earth the angel descends on the diagonal, red-tunicked, green-mantled, his wings still beating, one hand gathered to his breast and the other pointing down — not at the visionary but at Francis: the gesture is the voice of the legend, assigning the throne. And the lower zone divides, once more, the modes of seeing. At the lower left kneels the companion, head thrown back, both hands flung up toward the apparition — the ecstatic, to whom the vision is given.

At the right, within a little church rendered in the cycle’s habitual section — the arch with its Cosmatesque lattice, the altar with its cross raised on steps, and, suspended above the altar on its chains, a hanging lamp — the light that burns before the Sacrament, telling us that the deserted church is deserted only of men — Francis kneels in profile, hands joined, seeing nothing: the humble, about whom the vision is. The seer looks up, the subject looks down; the throne is promised precisely to the one who does not see it. Few scenes in the cycle state their theology so completely through the mere distribution of gazes.

The field belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, in the even stretch of its central scenes, and its designing intelligence is continuous with the dreams and visions that precede it: the supernatural admitted full-scale into the field, the witnesses divided, the architecture opened in section — here reduced to a single aedicula, so that the sky may carry the thrones unimpeded. The question of that intelligence’s name, Giotto for the tradition and for prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the line that descends from Offner, is discussed in the section on the Assisi question above.

The condition is among the most fortunate in the series precisely where fortune mattered: the blue ground, elsewhere the cycle’s first casualty, holds across most of the field, mottled but present, and with it the nocturnal strangeness of the invention; abrasions touch the habits of the two friars and the angel’s draperies, and a pale blemish above the companion’s hands is damage rather than design. The titulus below is fragmentary, and the scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. It remains the cycle’s quietest summit: a picture of five empty chairs, and one of the profoundest images of humility in Western art.

### The Exorcism of the Demons at Arezzo (Legend of Saint Francis, X)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-38.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing a tall Gothic church with a campanile at the left, before which Saint Francis kneels in prayer while the friar Silvester stands and raises his arm toward the walled city of Arezzo at the right. Above the city’s packed towers and houses seven dark, bat-winged demons flees into the sky. Two citizens appear in the arched gates of the rusticated city wall.">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Expulsion of the Demons from Arezzo, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene X, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The tenth scene carries the Legend into the public miracles of the mature saint, and it opens with the cycle’s great image of the city possessed. Bonaventure relates that Francis, coming to Arezzo and finding it torn by civil strife, saw from the suburb where he lodged demons exulting above the town and inflaming its citizens to mutual slaughter. He summoned Brother Silvester, a priest — the first priest to enter the order, a man of dove-like simplicity — and commanded him, in the name of obedience, to go before the city gate and order the demons to depart in the name of God. Silvester obeyed, cried out, and the demons fled; peace returned to the city, and its people, the legend notes, reformed their civic ordinances in tranquillity. The episode is Bonaventure’s parable of discord as possession — faction, the endemic disease of the Italian commune, diagnosed as the devil’s work — and the fresco hung it, with pointed timeliness, before the citizens of every faction-ridden town who entered the basilica.

What the painting fixes, with a subtlety that commentary has always savoured, is the structure of the miracle: it is performed by proxy. Francis kneels at the left, behind and below, hands joined, his halo the only one in the field; before him stands Silvester, unhaloed, and to him belong the action and the grandeur — the body planted frontally against the church, the arm flung up and out toward the towers in the oratorical gesture of command. The saint prays, the delegate expels; power descends through obedience, and the composition draws the chain of it in a single rising diagonal from the bent friar to the standing friar to the fleeing swarm. For a cycle so largely built on the exaltation of its one hero, the inversion is remarkable: the picture’s protagonist, in every formal sense, is the subordinate — sanctity portrayed as something transmissible, which is, after all, the claim of the order itself.

The two cities of the composition divide the field like the two parties of the Renunciation. At the left, the Church: a tall gabled front with sculpted arcading, rose-lit storeys and a banded campanile, cool white and rose, standing outside the walls as Bonaventure’s account requires — Francis had lodged *in suburbio*, in the borgo beyond the gates — and thus inviting identification with Arezzo’s old cathedral on the Colle del Pionta, the great extramural episcopal complex razed in the sixteenth century; the demolition has made the proposal unverifiable, and the painted front remains characterised, like the Lateran of Scene VI, rather than surveyed. At the right, the commune: Arezzo packed inside its circuit like a basket of towers — ochre, white, teal and rose, battlements, jettied balconies on timber brackets, house crowded on house with the claustrophobic vitality of the real Duecento townscape — and its wall drawn as a great curved sheet of rusticated ashlar, block by block, swinging across the foreground.

Above the towers seven demons pour out — dark, naked, bat-winged, horned, clawed — streaming up and away to the right in a rout that empties the sky; a number, seven, in which it is hard not to hear the capital vices that civil discord lets loose. And in the two gates below, the painter sets his quiet coda: citizens emerge into the doorways, tentative, looking out — the first stirring of a town coming back to itself, peace shown not as allegory but as people daring the street again.

The field belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, and its designing intelligence is continuous with the whole: the miracle staged between two architectures, the diagonal of action binding the zones, the anecdotal margin — the figures in the gates — carrying the meaning’s last word. The name of that intelligence, Giotto for the tradition that descends from Riccobaldo, Ghiberti and Vasari and for prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the Offnerian line, is the standing question of the Legend, discussed in the section on the Assisi question above; this scene, with its city-portrait appetite and its command of civic rhetoric, has always weighed on the Italian side of the scales for those who hear in it the painter of the Paduan Vices and Virtues.

The condition is uneven in the familiar pattern: the blue of the sky survives in broad mottled passages — enough to keep the demons legible as silhouettes, though their *a secco* blacks have thinned — while abrasions wear the church front and the friars’ habits, and losses touch the wall’s lower courses; the titulus beneath is fragmentary, and the scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. It remains among the cycle’s most quoted inventions: the Italian city given its first great portrait as a moral subject — beautiful, towered, and sick — and healed from outside its walls by two men, one of whom only prays.

### The Trial by Fire before the Sultan (Legend of Saint Francis, XI)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-39.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing the Sultan of Egypt enthroned at the right under a gabled Gothic canopy, pointing across the scene toward two or three Saracen priests who withdraw at the left, glancing back; a fire blazes on the ground between them. At the centre the haloed Saint Francis, with a companion friar behind him, stands beside the flames, hand on his breast, ready to enter them. Soldiers of the court stand behind the throne; above, a columned loggia with a coffered vault and its piers crowned by small gilded statues of winged figures.">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, Saint Francis before the Sultan (The Trial by Fire), Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XI, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The eleventh scene carries the Legend beyond Christendom, to the episode of 1219 that the order never ceased to meditate: Francis, crossing the crusader lines before Damietta, stood in the court of al-Malik al-Kāmil, Sultan of Egypt, and preached Christ. Bonaventure relates that when words reached their limit the saint proposed the ordeal — let a great fire be kindled, and he would enter it with the sultan’s priests, that the true faith might be shown; and when the eldest of those priests slipped away at the mere proposal, Francis offered to enter alone, on terms: "if I am burned,” the saint stipulates in Bonaventure’s account, “let it be imputed to my sins; but if God’s power protects me, you shall acknowledge Christ, the power and wisdom of God, as true God and Lord, the Saviour of all” (Legenda maior IX, 8). The sultan, Bonaventure says, dared not accept, fearing sedition among his people — but he conceived for the poor man an admiration he did not hide. The fresco seizes the proposal at its moment of maximum tension, with the fire already lit and no one yet in it: an ordeal suspended, which is precisely what history records.

The composition is a sentence read from right to left, against the grain of the eye, and the inversion is expressive. The sultan, enthroned at the right beneath a gabled canopy, dark-bearded, white-turbaned under his crown, leans off the axis of his seat and throws his arm across the whole breadth of the scene — not at Francis but past him, at the departing priests: the gesture is a command to stay and a judgement on those who will not.

The two or three priests, at the far left, are already gone in spirit: muffled in their mantles, white-bearded, they withdraw with sidelong backward glances in which the painter has concentrated the whole psychology of the scene — fear dressed as dignity, retreat pretending to be exit. Between court and clergy burns the fire, low on the ground, a nest of red tongues rendered with startling directness; and beside it stands Francis, the calm centre of the storm of gestures: one hand on his breast in the oath-taker’s gesture, the other opened toward the flames, inviting; behind him his companion — tradition names Brother Illuminato — watches with clasped stillness. The saint neither points nor pleads; he offers himself, and the composition isolates his quiet verticality between the sultan’s flung arm and the priests’ turning backs.

The setting speaks the cycle’s language of characterised architecture at its most ornamental. The sultan’s tribune is a Gothic aedicula of white marble — trefoil gable, coffered arch, inlaid rosettes — raised on a dais whose cloth of honour pools in gilded folds; at the left a two-storey loggia on slender columns carries a black coffered vault, and its piers are crowned by small gilded statues of winged figures in human form — Victories in the antique manner, the figured *acroteria* with which the cycle finishes its princely architecture, here crowning the court of the East with the classical emblems of worldly glory. The East is rendered, as in the cycle generally, by dignity rather than fantasy: a court of marble, textiles and order, whose magnificence raises the stakes of the poor man’s wager.

The scene demands the comparison with its descendant: the Trial by Fire of the Bardi Chapel, painted by the mature Giotto a generation later and treated below. There the composition is rebuilt around a central throne, the sultan’s two arms distributing judgement to either side, the fire moved to Francis’s feet; the Assisi version strings the same actors along a lateral frieze, the drama carried by the long diagonal of the sultan’s arm. Read together, the two frescoes are the Assisi question in a single subject: continuity of invention, transformation of syntax — the replica-or-rethinking dilemma on which the attribution debate, set out in the section on the Assisi question above, has long turned. The scene belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, several hands to one design, and the design’s economy — the ordeal as a geometry of courage and flight — is among the cycle’s most admired.

The condition is uneven: the blue grounds, *a secco* as always, have thinned and mottled across the upper zone, taking with them some of the legibility of the loggia’s recession, and broad pale abrasions wear the wall behind Francis and the dais below; the figures keep their finishes better, the fire best of all. The titulus beneath survives in fragments, and the field passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. What endures is the invention Bonaventure’s chapter deserved: faith and fear given equal space, a fire burning between them, and the unbeliever’s pointing arm — the most honest gesture in the picture — showing where the truth stands.

### The Ecstasy of Saint Francis (Legend of Saint Francis, XII)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-40.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing Saint Francis lifted into the night sky on a luminous white cloud, arms raised wide, face turned upward toward Christ, who leans down in half-length from a dark opening of heaven at the upper right, extending his hand in blessing. At the lower left four friars react with wonder before a fortified building with towers and an arched gate; at the right, trees rise on a rocky slope">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Ecstasy of Saint Francis, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XII, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The twelfth scene turns from the saint’s public trials to his secret life, and gives the Legend its image of prayer itself. Bonaventure, in the chapter of the Legenda maior devoted to Francis’s zeal in prayer, relates what the brothers saw when they watched him at night: praying in deserted places, he was more than once found lifted from the ground — raised sometimes, the text says, to the height of the trees, and sometimes to a height beyond seeing, wrapped in a shining cloud, as though the body were drawn after the soul.

The fresco condenses the testimony into a single levitation: Francis stands upon — almost within — a cloud of white light that has gathered beneath him like a nest, his arms flung wide and upward, his face lifted; and heaven answers. From a dark opening in the upper right corner Christ leans down in half-length, foreshortened, his hand extended toward the saint — not summoning, not crowning, but reaching, as a man leans from a window toward someone he knows.

The apparition of Christ is the boldest passage in the scene, and among the boldest in the cycle. Elsewhere the Legend’s heavens send angels, hands, chariots; here, for the first time since the dream of Scene III, Christ comes in person — and he comes bodily, diving out of the dark segment of the sky with his red mantle still moving, the glory reduced to the arc of shadow from which he emerges.

The invention gives the mystical encounter the structure of a mutual approach: the saint rising, the Lord descending, and between the raised hands and the extended hand a gap of night that the composition charges exactly as it charged the void of the Renunciation — except that here the distance is closing. Francis’s open-armed posture carries the program’s long memory: it is the orant-cruciform gesture of his apparition at Arles, and the attitude his body will assume on La Verna, two bays ahead; the ecstasy is painted as the rehearsal of the stigmatization, prayer bending the body, in advance, into the shape of the Cross.

Below, the picture keeps its feet on the ground with the cycle’s habitual division of witness and world. Four friars cluster at the lower left — the brothers of Bonaventure’s testimony, who spied their father at prayer — and their reactions are graded with the usual care: one flings up a hand, one presses his palm to his breast, two confer, faces tilted along the diagonal of the miracle.

Behind them stands a fortified complex — an ochre gatehouse between battlemented towers of grey-green and rose, a convent or hermitage characterised rather than named — the architecture of enclosure from which the saint has, in every sense, risen. And at the right, on the rocky slope, the painter sets the detail that reads like a draughtsman’s note on the text itself: trees — and Francis, on his cloud, has already cleared their crowns. The cloud itself is Bonaventure’s word made paint: "nubecula fulgente circumdatus".

The grading of the levitations by the height of the trees belongs instead to the later Leonine tradition of La Verna, where Brother Leo watches his father lifted now a span above the ground, now to the treetops, now beyond sight; those texts are younger than the fresco, so that painter and tradition converge here on the same literalism rather than the one illustrating the other. It is the kind of quiet literalism this workshop loves: the miracle calibrated against the landscape, so that the eye can verify what the text asserts.

The field belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, in the even central stretch of the cycle, and the designing intelligence is continuous with the visions that precede it — the supernatural full-scale in the field, the witnesses divided, the architecture standing for the world left behind. The name of that intelligence, Giotto for the tradition and for prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the Offnerian line, is the standing question of the Legend, discussed in the section on the Assisi question above; it may only be noted here that the plunging half-length Christ, entering the field from beyond its frame, is a solution of the same audacity as the foreshortened angels of Padua, and has always counted among the connoisseurs’ exhibits — on both sides of the question.

The condition is kinder than the average of the series: the blue of the night survives across most of the field, deep and only lightly mottled, keeping the nocturne legible and the cloud luminous against it; abrasions wear the friars’ habits and the lower rocks, the dark segment of heaven at the upper corner has suffered losses along its rim, and the titulus below is fragmentary. The scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural harm. It remains the cycle’s most inward image — a man alone with God, watched from the edge by his sons — and the proof that this workshop could make transcendence visible with three elements only: a cloud, a gap of night, and two hands that do not quite touch.

### The Crib at Greccio (Legend of Saint Francis, XIII)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-41.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing the first Christmas crib, seen from inside the choir of a church: Saint Francis, haloed and in a deacon’s vestment, kneels to lay the haloed Christ Child in a manger beside an ox and an ass, while a priest celebrates at the altar under a gabled ciborium and friars sing open-mouthed around a lectern. Behind rises the back of the choir screen, with a pulpit upon it and a great painted crucifix seen from the reverse, tilted forward on its wooden support; women look in through the screen’s doorway and townspeople crowd at the left.">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Institution of the Crib at Greccio, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XIII, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The thirteenth scene closes the first wall of the Legend with the episode that gave Christendom one of its most durable devotions. Bonaventure relates that three years before his death, Francis determined to celebrate the memory of the Child born at Bethlehem with all possible solemnity, and — having first asked and obtained licence from the pope, a scruple the Legenda maior is careful to record — had a manger prepared at Greccio, hay brought, an ox and an ass led in. The brethren were summoned, the people came with candles and torches, the woods rang with voices; Mass was sung over the manger, and Francis, who was a deacon, chanted the Gospel and preached on the nativity of the poor King.

Then the miracle, given on the testimony of a witness Bonaventure names: the knight John of Greccio, a virtuous soldier who had left the world’s militia for love of the saint, saw a beautiful child asleep in the manger, whom Francis approached and seemed to waken. The fresco paints the vision as fact — the Child in the saint’s hands wears a halo and stirs with life — so that we see, as so often in this cycle, with the eyes of the one who saw truly.

What has made the scene immortal, however, is not the miracle but the viewpoint. The painter has set the event not in a hermitage grotto but inside a great contemporary church — and he has placed us, with unheard-of audacity, behind the choir screen, in the liturgical enclosure reserved to the clergy. The marble tramezzo runs across the composition as its backbone, and we see its working side: the pulpit mounted upon it, reached from within; the cornice with its candle-spikes; the doorway through which a crowd of laywomen, excluded from the choir, press and peer — the barrier of medieval liturgical space drawn, for once, from the privileged side.

And above all, the fresco’s most celebrated invention: the great painted crucifix of Duecento type, suspended over the screen facing the nave, shown here from the back — its battens, its struts, the timber prop that holds it tilted forward over the people. It is, by common consent, the first reverse of a painting in Western art: a workshop telling us, with quiet pride, that it knows exactly how such objects hang — and telling the historian, incidentally, how the great tramezzo crosses of the age were actually displayed, evidence already put to use above in discussing the destination of Giotto’s Santa Maria Novella Crucifix.

Around the manger the ceremony proceeds with documentary density. Francis kneels in the vestment of his diaconal office, lowering the Child toward the manger-chest, the ox and ass couched beside it; at the altar, beneath a gabled ciborium on tall columns — its spandrels carved with angels, its summit crowned by a lantern — the celebrant stands at the sacring, attended by clerics, one of whom wears the red hat of rank; by the lectern a knot of friars sings, and their singing is the scene’s second miracle: heads thrown back, mouths open on different vowels, the chant made visible with an observational candour that nothing in earlier painting approaches. At the left the laity of Greccio crowd in — merchants’ caps, furred collars, among them, surely, the knight John, though the fresco declines to single him out; the women stay at the door. Every order of Christian society is present, and each in its canonical place: the picture is at once a nativity, a Mass, and a map of the medieval church interior.

For the Franciscans of the mother basilica the subject carried a double charge. Greccio was the order’s claim on the affections of all Christendom — the crib devotion spreading from this night into every church in Europe — and it was also a manifesto of Francis’s sacramental obedience: the licence sought from Rome, the Mass at the centre, the deacon-saint subordinate to the celebrating priest.

Painted on the nave wall of the order’s own church, before a screen like the one it depicts, the scene folded the pilgrim’s present into the legend’s past: what Francis did at Greccio, the friars do here, above this very crowd. The field belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend; its designing intelligence — the interior built from the forbidden side, the metapictorial cross, the graded choirs of participants — has always stood high among the exhibits in the attribution debate, Giotto for the tradition and for prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the Offnerian line, as the section on the Assisi question above sets out.

The condition is good by the cycle’s measure: the blue of the upper zone has thinned and mottled in the usual way, with losses along the left margin and scattered abrasions in the crowd, but the screen, the cross’s reverse, the singers and the central group keep their finishes, and the scene reads today almost entire. The titulus below is fragmentary; the field passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. It remains the Legend’s most quietly revolutionary picture — the one in which this workshop, asked to paint a memory of Bethlehem, painted instead the inside of its own world, and trusted the manger to consecrate it.

### The Miracle of the Spring (Legend of Saint Francis, XIV)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-42.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi, in vertical format, showing a steep rocky mountainside with pale, silvery trees against a dark sky. At the centre Saint Francis kneels in profile on the slope, praying toward the rock above, his halo radiating fine lines of light. At the lower left two friars stand beside a saddled ass; at the lower right a peasant in a grey tunic and cap stretches full-length on the ground, propping himself on his hands to drink from the spring that has broken from the rock">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Miracle of the Spring, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XIV, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 200 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, entrance wall, left of the portal.</figcaption>
</figure>

With the fourteenth scene the Legend turns the corner of the nave: the long north wall is complete, and the narrative crosses to the entrance wall, where two fields flank the great portal. The format changes with the place — taller, narrower, a mountain’s proportions rather than a stage’s — and the designer met the new frame with one of the cycle’s most radical compositions: a scene with almost no architecture, no city, no interior, only rock, climbing diagonally across the whole height of the field against a sky of deep nocturnal blue, with pale trees standing on the ledges like silver flames. After thirteen scenes of piazzas, courts and churches, the Legend’s first pure wilderness.

Bonaventure sets the episode on a journey to a mountain hermitage. Francis, worn by illness, rode a poor man’s ass up the slope; the owner, toiling behind in the heat, broke down — he was dying, he cried, of thirst. The saint dismounted, knelt, prayed, and told the man to hasten to the rock, where he would find living water: and the spring was there, granted — Bonaventure draws the moral — to the prayer of the poor man of Christ, water drawn from the stone as once for Moses in the desert. The Legenda maior adds the note of the singular: the spring was never found before and never found again. A miracle without witnesses to convert, without a city to pacify: the cycle’s one act of pure, almost domestic mercy — God troubling the geology of a mountain so that one tired man may drink.

Bonaventure's own words deserve quotation, for the fresco follows them clause by clause (*Legenda maior* VII, 12):

> Tempore quodam, cum vir Dei ob infirmitatem corpoream ad eremum quendam, ut inibi contemplationi vacaret, vellet accedere, quia debilis erat ad gradiendum, cuiusdam pauperis homuncionis asello vehebatur. […] "En," inquit, "morior siti, nisi alicuius liquoris beneficio confortetur statim." Descendit sine mora de asello vir Dei et, flexis genibus in terram, levatis in caelum manibus, orare non cessavit, donec se exauditum intellexit. "Festina," inquit, "ad petram, et illic invenies aquam vivam, quam tibi hac hora Christus de lapide ad bibendum misericorditer propinavit." […] Nec fons ibi fuerat prius, nec ultra, diligenter quaesitus, inventus est.

"Once, when the man of God, because of his bodily infirmity, wished to go to a certain hermitage to give himself there to contemplation, being too weak to walk he rode the small ass of a poor man. […] 'See,' the man cried, 'I shall die of thirst unless some draught revives me at once.' Without delay the man of God dismounted, knelt on the ground, raised his hands to heaven, and did not cease praying until he understood that he had been heard. 'Hasten,' he said, 'to the rock, and there you will find living water, which Christ in his mercy has this hour drawn from the stone for you to drink.' […] No spring had been there before, nor was one ever found there afterwards, though diligently sought."

The composition distributes the story along the diagonal of the slope with a clarity that has become a textbook of narrative construction. At the lower left, the point of departure: two friars wait with the ass — the patient animal again, saddled in red, its rope halter carefully observed — their faces turned to each other in quiet speech, the ordinary world pausing at the edge of the extraordinary.

At the centre, raised on the path, the engine of the miracle: Francis kneels in strict profile, hands joined, face lifted to the rock, and his halo — alone in the scene — is drawn as a disc of fine radiating striations, light made of lines, the sun-like nimbus this workshop reserves for its moments of highest grace.

And at the lower right, the miracle’s destination and the picture’s immortal invention: the peasant, flung full-length on the ground, propped on his spread hands, cap still on his head, drinking. The figure is built from behind and in profil perdu — shoulders, haunch, the blue-stockinged feet — a body entirely given over to its need, with a physical candour that nothing in medieval painting had attempted; generations of critics have singled him out as the cycle’s purest piece of observation, and the whole realist lineage of Tuscan painting, down through Masaccio’s shivering neophytes, has been read as descending from this one thirsty man. The water itself, a thin thread at the rock’s foot, has worn faint with the centuries — the viewer today takes the drink almost on faith, which the composition, built entirely of converging attentions, still compels.

The three groups never exchange a glance — the friars talk, the saint prays, the peasant drinks — and this mutual unawareness is the scene’s deepest stroke: the miracle passes through the picture like the water through the rock, unobserved by its own cast, seen whole only by us. The field belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend; its design — the diagonal mountain, the isolated orant, the great foreshortened drinker — has always ranked among the cycle’s masterpieces, and weighs correspondingly in the attribution debate, Giotto for the tradition and for prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the Offnerian line, as the section on the Assisi question above sets out.

The condition is strong in the figures and the rock, which keep their modelling; the blue ground, *a secco* as always, has darkened and mottled, taking the night closer to black than the designer intended, and the spring’s water has thinned to near-invisibility; scattered losses fleck the margins, and the titulus below is fragmentary. The scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. It remains the Legend’s quietest perfection: a mountain, four men, an ass, and a thread of water — the whole grammar of the cycle, architecture and crowds set aside, proving it can hold the wall with rock and thirst alone.

### The Sermon to the Birds (Legend of Saint Francis, XV)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-43.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi, in vertical format, showing Saint Francis bending deeply toward a flock of birds gathered on the ground beneath a tall leafy tree, his hands lowered toward them in speech and blessing. Behind him a companion friar watches with a gesture of astonishment. The birds — doves, crows and jackdaws — stand in rows facing the saint, while others fly down the hillside to join them; pale silvery trees stand against a whitened sky over a dark slope">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, Saint Francis Preaching to the Birds, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XV, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 200 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, entrance wall, right of the portal.</figcaption>
</figure>

The fifteenth scene, facing the Miracle of the Spring across the great portal, closes the entrance wall with the image that has carried Francis further into the world’s imagination than any other: the sermon to the birds. The episode entered the tradition with Thomas of Celano<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-thomas-celano" class="footnote"><sup>19</sup></a> and passed into Bonaventure’s Legenda maior, which sets it near Bevagna, in the valley below Assisi, in the days when Francis — the chapter is that on the efficacy of his preaching — was traversing the Spoleto valley; local tradition fixes the spot at Pian d’Arca, on the road between Assisi and Bevagna, an identification the countryside’s piety has maintained, though the text itself names only the town.

Coming upon a great multitude of birds gathered together — doves, little crows, and the birds the people called monaclae, jackdaws, as Thomas of Celano’s first account specifies the congregation, he ran to them, greeted them as though they shared reason — fratres volucres, my brother birds — and exhorted them to praise their Creator, who had clothed them in feathers, given them wings for flight and the pure air for their realm, and who feeds them, though they neither sow nor reap. The birds, Bonaventure testifies, stretched their necks, spread their wings, opened their beaks and gazed at him; he walked among them, his tunic brushing their heads, and not one moved until, with the sign of the cross, he gave them leave to fly. The Legenda draws its moral for the friars — the creatures’ obedience a reproach to deaf mankind; the painter drew something else, and simpler: the most tender picture of the Middle Ages.

The composition is built on a single bend. Francis inclines his whole body toward the flock — not a bow but a deep, deliberate stooping, the spine curved, the head lowered to the level of his listeners, the hands brought down and forward in the double gesture of speech and blessing. No scene in the cycle hinges so completely on one silhouette: the saint’s profile curve against the pale sky is the sermon — authority descending to the smallest of audiences, the great theme of Franciscan humility stated as posture.

Before him the birds stand in loose ranks on the ground beneath the tree, the white doves and the darker crows and jackdaws of Celano’s list together, heads lifted — and it is hard to believe the age missed the wit of the monacla, the “little nun” named for her grey hood: cowled birds attending a cowled preacher; and down the slope behind them more arrive, wings spread mid-descent, the painter giving the Legenda’s multitude its stragglers with the same observational candour that fed the ox of Greccio and the grazing horse of Scene II. Between the lowered hands and the lifted beaks remains the small interval the cycle keeps for its miracles — here not a charged void but a breathing space, across which, for once in the Legend, nothing passes but words.

The second figure is the measure of the first. The companion stands behind, upright where the saint is bent, his hand raised in a half-gesture of amazement checked by reverence: he does what we do, watching a man talk to birds and understanding that it is not folly. His verticality is compositionally indispensable — without it the saint’s bend would read as stumble rather than condescension — and his face, caught between bafflement and faith, is among the cycle’s finest psychological notations.

The setting is pared to the essentials of the roadside: the great tree sheltering the flock, its crown a cloud of pale leafage; two slighter trees at the left balancing the mass; the dark hillside rising behind, with a whitened sky above. The scene’s palette — silvery greens, greys, the brown of the habits — has shifted with time, the ground darkening and the sky bleaching, so that the fresco now wears a ghostly tonality; but even this alteration has served its fame, lending the image the air of something remembered rather than witnessed.

The scene carries a cross-reference of the first importance: it returns, in portable form, in the predella of the signed Stigmatization from Pisa now in the Louvre, treated below — the third of that panel’s three Assisi-derived compositions, with the Dream of Innocent III and the Approval of the Rule. The relation between wall and panel — the master replicating his own invention for the Italian line of scholarship that runs through Boskovits, a follower deriving from another’s for the tradition descending from Offner — is among the load-bearing arguments of the Assisi question, set out in the section on the Assisi question above; no single composition figures in that debate more often than this one. The field itself belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, and its design — one curve, one vertical, one tree, one flock — is the cycle’s economy at its most absolute.

The condition requires honest statement, for this is among the more altered fields of the series: the surface is extensively abraded, and the birds themselves — painted largely *a secco* — survive in varying degrees of legibility, some reduced to silhouettes. The titulus below is fragmentary, and the scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. Yet no amount of wear has touched what the picture is: the Legend’s gentlest invention, and the one in which the cycle’s whole argument — that holiness happened in this world, on a road, under a tree — needs neither architecture nor crowd to be believed.

### The Death of the Knight of Celano (Legend of Saint Francis, XVI)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-44.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing a banquet interrupted by death. At the left, under a coffered canopy with a colonnaded loggia above, a table laid with a white cloth carries bread, a fish on a platter, knives and glasses; a companion friar sits at its end and Saint Francis stands behind it, haloed, his hand opened toward the right, where the knight of Celano lies collapsed in red robes amid his kneeling family. Behind them stand mourning women in white veils and young women with loose hair, and a man in red lifts his hands in grief">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Death of the Knight of Celano, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XVI, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

With the sixteenth scene the Legend begins its homeward run along the second long wall of the nave, and its tone darkens: the cycle of foundations and public miracles gives way to the chapters of prophecy, death and glory. Bonaventure places the episode in his chapter on Francis’s spirit of prophecy.

A knight of Celano, devout and hospitable, invited the saint to dine; Francis, before the meal, drew him aside and told him what God had shown: let him confess his sins and set his house in order, for he would not eat of this dinner — that very day he was to die. The knight obeyed in everything, confessed to the saint’s companion, disposed of his affairs, and gave himself over to God’s mercy; and as the company went to table, suddenly, he breathed out his soul. Bonaventure’s moral is double and deliberate: the prophecy proved, and the host repaid — the man who had received the poor man of Christ into his house was received, prepared, into eternity. Death came as catastrophe to the household and as grace to its master; the fresco holds both truths at once, which is its genius.

Bonaventure's sequence — confession, the setting of the house in order, the sudden death at table — is worth giving in his own words (*Legenda maior* XI, 4):

> Cum apud Celanum vir Dei a quodam milite devote fuisset ad prandium invitatus, […] "Confitere," inquit, "frater hospes, peccata tua contritionis dolore compunctus, quia hodie non hic tantum, sed alibi pransurus es. […] Dispone domui tuae, quia priusquam de isto prandio comedas, spiritum exhalabis." Acquievit protinus ille verbis sancti viri, […] peccata sua socio eius sacerdoti confessione diluit, domum disposuit suam, et misericordiae Dei se totum commisit. Intrantibus autem illis ad mensam, […] ipse subito spiritum exhalavit.

"When at Celano the man of God had been devoutly invited to dinner by a certain knight, […] 'Confess your sins, brother host,' he said, 'pierced with the sorrow of contrition, for today you shall dine not here only, but elsewhere. […] Set your house in order, for before you eat of this dinner you will breathe out your spirit.' The man at once yielded to the holy man's words, […] washed away his sins by confession to the saint's companion, a priest, set his house in order, and committed himself wholly to the mercy of God. And as they were going in to table, […] he suddenly breathed out his spirit."

The composition is cut in two with surgical candour, and the cut runs through the middle of a dinner. At the left, the meal that will never be eaten: a long table under its white cloth, laid with bread, glasses, knives, a great fish on its platter — a passage of such limpid, loving description — the tablecloth’s woven borders, the folded napkin at its corner, rendered as if the painter had waited for the servants to finish — that [Roberto Longhi](/scholars.html#longhi-roberto), tracing the ancestry of Italian still life, placed at this laid table the beginning of the genre.

The companion friar sits at the table’s end, knife in hand, caught at the instant before the instant; Francis stands behind the board, the scene’s calm vertical, his face grave and unsurprised, his hand opened toward the right in a gesture that is neither alarm nor blessing but statement — this is what I told him. He alone in the picture already knew; his stillness, against the storm breaking beside him, is the prophecy made visible.

At the right, the storm. The knight has gone down in his red robes, his body slack along the picture’s lower edge, his head fallen back into the hands of his kin; a young woman bends to his face, another presses her cheek in the immemorial gesture of grief, a man supports his shoulders searching the face for what is no longer there.

Above them the household crowds in — matrons in white veils, unmarried girls with loose golden hair, a man in red flinging up both hands — a chorus of lament built in close ranks, and built — as the literature on the cycle from Alastair Smart onward has observed — on the armature of a Lamentation: the dead man mourned like a deposed Christ, the bending women like Maries. The borrowing is theology as much as design — the good death of the shriven man dignified with the composition of the holiest of deaths — and it is also prophecy of another kind: this knot of grief around a body, perfected, will become the Mourning of the Clares at the cycle’s end, and beyond Assisi, whatever name one gives the designer, the Lamentation of Padua.

The architecture seconds the division: the coffered canopy and airy colonnaded loggia shelter the table and the saint, while the mourning group spills beyond its shelter into the open blue — the house of hospitality at the left, and at the right, under bare sky, the human creature at its end.

The field belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, opening the second wall in the same broad manner that closed the first; the designing intelligence is continuous — the interior opened to us, the event split into cause and effect, the margins carrying the meaning. Its place in the program is a hinge: from here to the transept the Legend will show Francis increasingly as the man through whom death is foreseen, met and overcome, and this first death — not the saint’s own, but a supper guest’s — sounds the theme in a domestic key.

The attribution question, Giotto for the tradition and prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the Offnerian line, is set out in the section on the Assisi question above; the still life of the table and the Lamentation-structure of the mourners are among the passages most often cited, on both sides, as the work of a very great designer indeed.

The condition asks a note of caution: the figures and the architecture keep their finishes well, the tablecloth and the fish among the best-preserved passages of the entire cycle, but the blue ground at the upper right is disfigured by a tall dark swathe and a greenish blotch — damage and staining in the a secco sky rather than painted elements. The titulus below is fragmentary, and the scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. What endures is one of the Legend’s profoundest constructions: a laid table, a standing prophet, a fallen host — hospitality, foreknowledge and mortality seated at the same dinner.

### The Sermon before Honorius III (Legend of Saint Francis, XVII)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-45.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing Saint Francis preaching in a Gothic vaulted hall of three bays, with blue rib vaults, trefoil arcading and a patterned hanging along the walls. The saint stands at the left, haloed, gesturing as he speaks; a friar sits on the floor below him. At the centre Pope Honorius III, in a red mantle and tiara, listens with his head leaning on his hand, surrounded by six cardinals seated to either side, variously attentive and conversing">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, Saint Francis Preaching before Honorius III, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XVII, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The seventeenth scene returns Francis to the papal court, but with the terms reversed: in Scene VII he knelt to receive; here he stands to give. Bonaventure, in the chapter on the efficacy of the saint’s preaching, relates that when Francis was to speak before Pope Honorius and the cardinals, he prepared — at the urging of the Lord of Ostia, Cardinal Ugolino, the order’s protector and the future Gregory IX<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-gregory-ix" class="footnote"><sup>20</sup></a> — a sermon composed with care and committed to memory; and when he stood in their midst, every word of it left him.

He confessed the failure openly, invoked the Holy Spirit, and began to speak as the Spirit gave: with such fervour, Bonaventure says, that he could not contain himself, and moved his feet as if dancing — not in levity but burning with divine love, so that the hearers were pierced.

Bonaventure's account is worth giving in his own words (*Legenda maior* XII, 7):

> Cum coram domino Papa Honorio et Cardinalibus esset sermonem facturus, ad suggestionem domini Ostiensis episcopi sermonem quendam diligenter compositum memoriae commendavit. Cumque in medio consisteret […] omnium quae conceperat oblitus est. Quod cum humiliter veraciter esset confessus, ad Spiritus Sancti gratiam se convertit invocandam, coepitque tam efficacia verba effundere […] ut prae fervore spiritus etiam pedes quasi saltando moveret, non ut lasciviens, sed ut igne divini amoris ardens, ita ut audientium corda ad compunctionem converteret.

"When he was to preach before the Lord Pope Honorius and the cardinals, at the suggestion of the Lord Bishop of Ostia he committed to memory a sermon composed with care. And when he stood in their midst […] he forgot everything he had prepared. Having humbly and truthfully confessed this, he turned to invoke the grace of the Holy Spirit, and began to pour out words of such power […] that in the fervour of his spirit he moved his feet as if dancing — not in wantonness, but burning with the fire of divine love — so that he turned the hearts of his hearers to compunction."

The episode is the order’s manifesto on inspired speech — eloquence as grace, not art, learning forgotten so that God may talk — and the cycle sets it here, among the chapters of the saint’s maturity, as the counterweight to the sermon to the birds across the portal: the smallest audience in creation on one wall, the loftiest in Christendom on the other, and the same unstudied word for both.

The painter’s answer to Bonaventure is an interior of unprecedented ambition. The hall is a complete Gothic structure displayed in section: three bays of rib vaulting painted deep blue, trefoil arcading on the front plane with pierced quatrefoils in the spandrels, lancet windows in the rear wall, a figured hanging running behind the seated court — and, decisively, two freestanding columns rising through the middle ground, in front of the figures, so that the company sits convincingly inside the room, air on every side of them. No earlier painting in the cycle, and arguably none in Italy, had enclosed its cast so completely; the scene is the Legend’s most strenuous essay in constructed space, the ancestor of the chapter house at the Bardi Apparition of Arles, and a standing exhibit in every discussion of where the new painting of interiors began.

Within this architecture the drama is entirely of listening. Francis stands at the left, outside the court’s seated order, his profile intent, one hand gathering his habit, the other opened mid-phrase — the preacher at the point where preparation has failed and inspiration has taken over; below him, on the floor, his companion sits hunched in his habit, chin on hand, the humble order’s humble seat amid the prelacy.

Facing him, the court is a graded scale of attention that generations of visitors have read like a page of character studies: at the centre Honorius III, tiara on his head, leans his cheek upon his hand in the ancient posture of profound audition — the pope not enthroned in judgement but captured in thought, one of the first great images of listening in Western art; around him the six cardinals divide, some bent gravely toward the words, one gesturing in discussion with his neighbour, another folded in his mantle, arms crossed, reserving judgement. The sermon itself is invisible, as the water of the spring is invisible; what the painter gives us is its effect, distributed across seven faces — and the dancing feet of Bonaventure’s account withheld, with a court painter’s tact, in favour of the stillness it produced.

The field belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, in the run of scenes that opens the second wall, and its designing intelligence — the full interior, the psychology of audience, the saint placed off-centre so that attention itself can occupy the middle — weighs heavily in the attribution debate: those who hear Giotto at Assisi point to this hall as the seed of Padua’s painted chapels, while the line descending from Offner reads its very sophistication otherwise; the question is set out in the section on the Assisi question above. The identification of the pope as Honorius rests on Bonaventure’s text and the cycle’s old tituli,.

The condition is strong across the figures and the architecture, whose blues — for once structural rather than atmospheric — survive well; the principal wound is the long branching crack that descends through the left bays, an old structural fissure running down through vault, arcade and the figure of the saint’s companion, stabilised but left legible. The titulus below is fragmentary, and the scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without further structural loss. It remains the Legend’s great picture of the word at work: a man who forgot his sermon, a pope with his head on his hand, and between them a room built so solidly that the silence in it can be heard.

### The Apparition at the Chapter of Arles (Legend of Saint Francis, XVIII)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-46.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing the chapter house of Arles in section: a Gothic hall with pointed arches, pierced quatrefoil roundels and columned windows opening onto night, under a blue lean-to ceiling. Saint Anthony of Padua, haloed, preaches standing at the left; in the central doorway Saint Francis appears with arms extended in the form of a cross, visible above the crowd of hooded friars who sit on benches and on the floor, most with their backs to him, only a few turning or lifting their eyes">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Apparition of Saint Francis at the Chapter of Arles, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XVIII, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The eighteenth scene paints the miracle of presence at a distance, and it paints it — the paradox is the picture’s engine — mostly as absence of notice. Bonaventure relates that while Anthony of Padua was preaching to the provincial chapter at Arles on the inscription of the Cross, Jesus Nazarenus Rex Iudaeorum, Francis — far away in body — appeared at the doorway of the chapter house, lifted in the air with his arms extended as on a cross, and blessed the brethren; and the apparition was seen by one of them, a friar of proven virtue whom the Legenda maior names, brother Monaldo, while the others knew of it only by the consolation that flooded them, and by the saint’s own later confirmation.

The scene thus completes a sequence the second wall has been building: the chariot of Scene VIII showed the father’s spirit present to his first sons; here, with the order grown into provinces and chapters, the presence crosses half of Christendom — the founder keeping watch over an institution now too large for any one room, except miraculously.

The composition stages the theology of the event as a problem in attention, and solves it with a daring the Legend nowhere exceeds: the miracle happens at the exact centre of the picture, and almost no one sees it. The chapter house is opened in section — a Gothic hall of pointed arcades on slender shafts, pierced quatrefoils in the gables, columned windows giving onto the blue of night, a lean-to ceiling of painted boards — and in its central doorway Francis materialises, frontal, arms stretched wide in the cruciform orant that is his signature gesture in this cycle, from the ecstasy to La Verna; and lifted up, as the text says — a suspension the picture declares not by the feet, which the seated ranks conceal, but by the arms themselves: outstretched level with the spring of the arches, carried higher than a standing man’s could reach, the open cross of the gesture floating in the doorway.

Around him the chapter sits in its hooded ranks — on the benches, on the floor, cowl beside cowl, a congregation built from the backs and shoulders that this workshop modelled like no other — and the ranks are turned the wrong way: toward Anthony, at the left, who is the lawful object of their attention.

A few heads only begin to turn; one friar lifts his eyes — and somewhere among these few is Monaldo, the friar of proven virtue whom Bonaventure names as the vision’s sole witness, though the fresco, with a discretion that may be deliberate, declines to single him out: the beholder must search the chapter for the one who sees, as the chapter itself would have to. Between the preacher expounding the Cross and the founder appearing as the Cross runs the picture’s silent wit: Anthony’s sermon has, at that moment, its illustration standing in the door — and the audience, intent on the words, misses the Word made visible. Only the beholder in the nave, and one friar, receive both.

Two haloes measure the picture’s historical moment: Anthony, canonised within a year of his death in 1232, preaches already nimbed — the cycle’s homage to the order’s second saint — while Francis’s nimbus crowns an apparition, not a man. The comparison with the Bardi Chapel version of the subject, painted a generation later and treated below, is among the most instructive available.

There the mature Giotto rebuilds the hall as a measured, airy section of round arches, enlarges the friars into grave volumes, and sharpens the division between those who see and those who do not; here the architecture is Gothic, ornamental, the crowd denser, the drama more diffused. Read in sequence, the two frescoes are either one mind revisiting its own invention across thirty years — the Italian line of scholarship — or a model and its magisterial rethinking by another: the Assisi question, set out in the section on the Assisi question above, in a single pair of rooms. The field itself belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, in the even run of the second wall.

The condition is among the better of the series: the architecture and the friars keep their finishes, the blues of windows and ceiling — structural here, as in the Honorius scene — survive with their depth, and the losses concentrate in abrasions across the habits and in the darkened band above the roofline. The titulus below is fragmentary, and the scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. It remains one of the Legend’s profoundest constructions: a room full of men facing a sermon, a cross-shaped presence at their backs, and the whole doctrine of the order — the founder present wherever two or three of his sons are gathered — stated by the simple, audacious means of making the miracle easy to miss.

### The Stigmatization of Saint Francis (Legend of Saint Francis, XIX)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-47.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing Saint Francis kneeling on the rocky slope of La Verna, body turned to the right and face lifted, his hands raised and opened toward the crucified Christ who appears in the sky at the upper right, borne on six red seraph wings within a radiance; fine golden rays run from Christ’s wounds to the saint’s hands, feet and side. At the left stands a small pink chapel with its door open onto an altar; at the right a golden aedicula with a rose window shelters a seated friar absorbed in reading a book. Trees crown the faceted mountain.">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, Saint Francis Receiving the Stigmata on La Verna, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XIX, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>
