The nineteenth scene is the summit toward which the entire Legend climbs: the impression of the wounds of Christ on Mount La Verna, in September 1224, two years before the saint’s death. Bonaventure — who made the stigmata the keystone of his theology of Francis, the seal of the alter Christus — relates in the thirteenth chapter of the Legenda maior that the saint, keeping a fast in honour of Saint Michael on the solitary mountain, saw descend from heaven a Seraph with six wings of flame, and between the wings the likeness of a man crucified; and as the vision vanished, understanding kindled in his flesh: the marks of the nails began to appear in his hands and feet, and in his side the wound of the lance.

The fresco renders the apparition in the form the Franciscan tradition had fixed and this cycle canonised for all painting to come: not a seraph resembling the Crucified but Christ himself, cross-nimbed, arms spread, borne on the three pairs of carmine wings — and between his body and the kneeling saint the five golden rays, wound answering wound across the night, the mystical conformity drawn as geometry — and drawn with the text in hand: the ray of the side seeks Francis’s right flank, the dextrum latus where Bonaventure places the lance-wound. Francis, kneeling on the bare rock, twists his whole body up and back into the light, hands lifted and opened — receiving, not shielding: the gesture of a man accepting a weight of glory.

The mountain is the third protagonist. La Verna rises at the left in the cycle’s faceted rock-language, trees clinging to its summit, and on its slopes stand the two small buildings that organise the story’s meaning: at the left a pink chapel, its door open upon a glimpsed altar — the oratory of the saint’s retreat, the Church present even in the wilderness; at the right a golden aedicula crowned by a cross and pierced by a rose window, before which sits the scene’s quiet masterstroke: the companion — tradition, following the sources, names Brother Leo — absorbed in his book, head bent to the page, reading on while heaven opens above him.

The Legenda is explicit that the vision was the saint’s alone; the painter states the exclusivity by the most human of means — a man present at the greatest miracle of his age, and missing it for a book. The Legend has graded its witnesses through twenty-eight scenes — the sleeping, the waking, the one who sees; here, at the summit, the grading reaches its final irony: proximity is not vision, and the reader at the foot of La Verna stands for every beholder who looks without seeing.

Bonaventure's account of the vision and of the wounds' appearing deserves quotation entire, for the fresco follows it member by member (*Legenda maior* XIII, 3):

> Vidit Seraph unum sex alas habentem, tam ignitas quam splendidas, de caelorum sublimitate descendere. […] Apparuit inter alas effigies hominis crucifixi, in modum crucis manus et pedes extensos habentis et cruci affixos. […] Disparens igitur visio mirabilem in corde ipsius reliquit ardorem, sed et in carne non minus mirabilem signorum impressit effigiem. Statim namque in manibus eius et pedibus apparere coeperunt signa clavorum, quemadmodum paulo ante in effigie illa viri crucifixi conspexerat. […] Dextrum quoque latus quasi lancea transfixum, rubra cicatrice obductum erat.

"He saw a Seraph with six wings, as fiery as they were resplendent, descend from the height of heaven. […] There appeared between the wings the likeness of a man crucified, his hands and feet stretched out in the form of a cross and fastened to a cross. […] As the vision vanished, it left in his heart a marvellous fire, and impressed upon his flesh a no less marvellous likeness of its signs. For at once there began to appear in his hands and feet the marks of the nails, just as he had seen them a little before in the figure of the Crucified. […] His right side, too, as though pierced by a lance, was covered over with a red scar."

The composition’s two diagonals — the mountain descending leftward, the saint’s torqued body and the rays rising rightward — lock the field into the same X-shaped tension the cycle’s dreams and visions have rehearsed, but with a concentration none of them attains; and its afterlife is the history of the subject. This is the design that fixed the Stigmatization for Italian art: the kneeling three-quarter saint, the plunging Christ-seraph, the cord of rays.

It stands as the first panel of a triptych of versions: the signed altarpiece from Pisa in the Louvre, which repeats this composition — with its Assisi-derived predella — almost clause for clause, and the fresco over the Bardi Chapel entrance, where the mature design is pruned to rock, saint and seraph; each is treated below, and the three together are the Assisi question made visible, the sequence reading either as one master revisiting his own invention across three decades — the line of Italian scholarship through Boskovits — or as the Legend’s master furnishing the model that Giotto, or another, then signed and transformed: the alternatives, and their stakes, are set out in the section on the Assisi question above.

The scene belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, closing the great central run of the second wall before the final scenes change key — and change hands, the last fields of the cycle returning to the Master of Saint Cecilia of the opening. The dating follows the whole, in the last years of the thirteenth century and perhaps the first of the fourteenth. The old titulus beneath recorded the vision of the Crucified in the form of the Seraph and the impression of the stigmata; it survives, like its fellows, in fragments.

The condition is uneven in the familiar pattern, with a particular mercy: the figures, the buildings and the rock keep their finishes, the reading Leo among the best-preserved passages of the wall, while the a secco blue of the sky has thinned to a mottled, bruised depth around the apparition, and the golden rays — the scene’s very nerves — have faded in stretches, so that the eye must sometimes reconstruct the thread it is meant to follow. The field passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. It remains, whatever name one writes under it, among the most consequential images in European art: the picture that taught the West what it looks like when a body is answered by its God — and, at the mountain’s foot, the man with the book, to remind us how easily it is missed.

### The Death and Ascension of Saint Francis (Legend of Saint Francis, XX)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-48.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi in three registers. Below, Saint Francis lies dead on a bier in his habit, surrounded by kneeling friars who kiss his stigmatized hands and press close in grief. In the middle zone stand rows of friars and clergy in white, with processional crosses and candles, one deacon in a crossed stole; a friar at the centre lifts his gaze upward. Above, the saint’s soul — a half-length Francis in a round glory — is carried to heaven by angels over a small white cloud against the blue">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Death of Saint Francis and the Ascension of His Soul, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XX, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The twentieth scene is the Legend’s pivot from life to glory: the death of Francis at the Porziuncola on the evening of 3 October 1226, fused in a single field with the ascent of his soul. Bonaventure devotes to the transit the fourteenth chapter of the Legenda maior: the dying saint, in extremity of weakness, had himself laid naked upon the naked earth, blessed his brothers, bade the Passion be read and the psalm Voce mea be sung, and passed; and at that hour one of the friars saw his soul borne up over many waters — a star of exceeding brightness, carried on a white cloud straight to heaven.

The fresco keeps the cloud and promotes the star: above the little white nimbus of vapour the soul appears not as light but as the man himself, a half-length Francis in the brown habit, hands opened, framed in a round glory and carried by angels in a convoy of wings — the same hermeneutic generosity the cycle showed at the fiery chariot, where the text’s globe of light became the father in person. The two scenes are, in fact, a deliberately matched pair across the nave: the chariot was the pledge, made while he lived, that his spirit would remain with his sons; the transit is the pledge redeemed — Elijah taken up at last, with the Elishas kneeling below.

The composition is a liturgy in three registers, and its order is its meaning. At the base, grief at its most physical: the bier runs the width of the field, and around the dead man — clothed now in the habit, the body composed, the face at peace — the friars break into the postures the workshop had rehearsed on the knight of Celano: one kisses the stigmatized hand, another bends to the feet, others crowd brow to brow, a kneeling ring of backs and cowls modelled with the breadth that is this painter’s signature. In the middle register the community rises into order: files of friars and of clergy in white, processional crosses tilted over the heads, tapers, a deacon in his crossed stole — the obsequies already liturgical, grief marshalled into rite.

And at the centre of this zone stands the hinge of the whole picture: a single friar, face lifted, eyes raised past the crosses to the sky — Bonaventure’s unnamed witness, the one who saw. He is the only vertical gaze in a field of lowered heads, and along his line of sight the beholder climbs to the third register, where the angels receive what the friars below are losing. Death at the bottom, the Church in the middle, heaven at the top: the scene is a cross-section of the Franciscan cosmos, with one man’s glance for a stair.

The fresco asks to be read against its great descendant: the Death and Ascension of the Bardi Chapel, treated below, where the mature design strips the registers to a single horizontal elegy and lifts the soul in a small mandorla. The comparison — invention and distillation, or model and rethinking — belongs to the standing question of the Legend, Giotto for the tradition and prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the Offnerian line, as the section on the Assisi question above sets out. The field itself belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, in the last stretch before the final scenes pass to the Master of Saint Cecilia; and the Celano scene four bays back now reveals its full function in the program — the rehearsal, on a layman’s body, of exactly this grief.

One further detail has entered the scene’s modern legend. During the restoration campaigns, close photography of the clouds flanking the ascending soul revealed, worked into the grey vapour at the right, a profile that reads as a demon — hooked nose, sly half-smile, horns — hidden in the cloud’s folds; the finding, announced in 2011 and associated with [Chiara Frugoni](/scholars.html#frugoni-chiara), with the basilica’s chief restorer Sergio Fusetti commenting, anticipates by nearly two centuries the cloud-figures of Mantegna.

Its intention is debated — a painter’s private jest, a portrait-spite, or a theological note, since the medieval sky was precisely where demons were believed to contest the ascending soul, so that a devil lurking in the clouds of a transit is iconographically less whimsical than it first appears. The prudent course for a caption is to report the find as a find, invisible from the floor and legible only in macro-photography.

The condition is worn in the way of the cycle’s crowded fields: the surface is abraded throughout, the blues thinned and bruised around the glory, the whites of the clergy’s vestments scoured toward the plaster, and the gold of the haloes dulled; yet the structure — the three registers, the lifted face at the centre, the convoy of angels — reads entire. The titulus below is fragmentary, and the scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. It remains the Legend’s great image of a community at the moment of its orphaning — and of the single pair of raised eyes that turns a funeral into a proof.

### The Apparition to Fra Agostino and to the Bishop of Assisi (Legend of Saint Francis, XXI)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-49.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing two deathbed visions side by side. At the left, within a golden loggia-like infirmary with arches, an external staircase, a tower and small statues on its gable, the dying friar Agostino sits up suddenly in bed, arms raised, supported and watched by astonished friars. At the right, in a chamber hung with a white swagged pall over a diapered curtain, the mitred Bishop of Assisi lies asleep in bed. A long branching crack runs through the right half of the scene">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Apparition of Saint Francis to Fra Agostino and to Bishop Guido of Assisi, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XXI, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The twenty-first scene doubles the proof of the transit: at the hour of Francis’s death, Bonaventure relates in the fourteenth chapter of the Legenda maior, the news travelled by vision to two witnesses far apart. In the Terra di Lavoro, Fra Agostino, minister of the province, lay at his own last hour, and had long since lost the use of speech; suddenly he cried out, for all to hear, “Wait for me, father — wait, I am coming with you!” — and, asked in amazement whom he addressed, answered that he saw his father Francis going to heaven; whereupon he died, and followed.

And the Bishop of Assisi, then on pilgrimage to the shrine of Saint Michael on Monte Gargano, saw Francis in his sleep, who said to him: “Behold, I leave the world and go up to heaven” — and waking, the bishop learned that the passing had been at that very hour. Two visions, two modes: the dying man sees waking, the healthy man sees dreaming; the friar cries out, the bishop is told. The designer of the Legend, who had graded the forms of seeing through the whole cycle, closes the series of visions with its systematic summa.

Bonaventure gives the two visions in sequence, and the fresco keeps his order, left to right (*Legenda maior* XIV, 6):

> Minister tunc fratrum in Terra Laboris erat frater Augustinus, qui, in hora ultima constitutus, cum iam dudum loquelae usum amisisset, audientibus qui astabant exclamavit: "Exspecta me, pater, exspecta: ecce, iam venio tecum!" Mirantibus autem fratribus et quaerentibus cui tam audacter loqueretur: "Nonne videtis," inquit, "patrem nostrum Franciscum, qui vadit ad caelum?" Et statim anima eius sancta, de carne egrediens, patrem sanctissimum est secuta. Episcopus vero Assisinas, qui tunc ad ecclesiam Sancti Michaelis in Monte Gargano peregrinationis causa accesserat, vidit per visionem beatum Franciscum sibi dicentem: "Ecce, relinquo mundum et vado ad caelum." […] Diligenterque inquirens, invenit indubitanter, illa hora patrem de saeculo transivisse, qua ei per visionem apparuit.

"The minister of the brothers in the Terra di Lavoro was then Fra Agostino, who, being at his last hour, though he had long since lost the use of speech, cried out in the hearing of those who stood by: 'Wait for me, father, wait: see, I am coming with you!' And when the brothers, amazed, asked to whom he spoke so boldly, 'Do you not see,' he said, 'our father Francis, who is going to heaven?' And at once his holy soul, leaving the flesh, followed the most holy father. And the Bishop of Assisi, who had then gone on pilgrimage to the church of Saint Michael on Monte Gargano, saw in a vision the blessed Francis saying to him: 'Behold, I leave the world and go up to heaven.' […] And inquiring diligently, he found beyond doubt that the father had passed from this world at the very hour in which he had appeared to him in the vision."

The scene’s deepest stroke is a renunciation: Francis does not appear. Alone among the cycle’s visions — the dreams, the chariot, the thrones, the ecstasy, all of which admit the apparition bodily into the field — this one withholds its object. The beholder is given only the seers: Agostino’s flung arms and risen torso at the left, the bishop’s closed eyes at the right, and between them nothing but architecture. The vision has moved inside; and the viewer, who through twenty-eight scenes has been privileged to see with the saints, is at the last demoted to the company of the astonished friars — witnesses of witnesses. It is the subtlest construction in the Legend, and the comparison with the Bardi Chapel version, treated on its own page, turns precisely here: there the great loss at the centre leaves open whether the mature design carried the ascending Francis between the two chambers; here the omission is intact, deliberate, and total.

The two visions share one golden architecture, and the sharing is itself exegesis. The left half is a grand loggia-infirmary displayed in section — arches on slender shafts, pierced parapets, an external stair climbing to a tower, and on the gable and parapets small statues in human form — the figured *acroteria* with which the cycle crowns its noblest buildings, from the dreamed Lateran onward; within it Agostino’s bed stands among the friars, who crowd the arcades in attitudes the painter has graded from clinical to stunned: one supports the dying man’s back, feeling the impossible strength of the mute who speaks; others press hands to cheeks, confer, lean in — the amazement of men hearing a silence break.

The right half, continuous with the left yet curtained into privacy, is the bishop’s chamber: a diapered hanging of red lozenges walls the bed, a white swagged pall is looped above it, and the bishop sleeps in his mitre — the office kept even in sleep, so that we may know the dreamer, and know that the dream goes to the Church of Assisi itself. Waking vision in an open arcade, dream vision behind a curtain: the architecture speaks the difference the text states.

The field belongs to the principal workshop of the Legend, in the final bay before the last scenes of the cycle pass to the Master of Saint Cecilia; its designing intelligence — the double chamber, the withheld apparition, the choral astonishment — is continuous with the whole, and the question of its name, Giotto for the tradition and prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the Offnerian line, is set out in the section on the Assisi question above. Within the program the scene performs the office its Bardi descendant would inherit: the death seen from afar, as promise — placed, here as there, beside the death seen from within.

The condition requires a word of its own: the right half of the field is traversed by a long, branching structural crack, descending through the bishop’s chamber and bed — an old fissure, stabilised, whose course the restorations have left legible — and the surfaces around it are abraded, the diapered hanging worn, the blues above thinned to the usual bruised dark; the left half fares better, Agostino and his friars among the well-preserved passages of the bay. The titulus below is fragmentary, and the scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss beyond its inherited scars. It remains the Legend’s quietest proof of glory: two beds, two closed rooms, a voice restored and a dream delivered — and the saint himself already beyond picturing.

### The Verification of the Stigmata (Legend of Saint Francis, XXII)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-50.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing the dead Saint Francis on his bier inside a church, before a screen beam from which hang lamps and three images: a gabled panel of the Virgin and Child enthroned, a great painted crucifix of Duecento type tilted forward on its support, and a panel of an archangel. Around the bier crowd friars with tall candles, clergy in white with books, and laymen in caps; at the centre the knight Jerome kneels over the body, his hand at the saint’s side wound">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Knight Jerome Verifying the Stigmata, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XXII, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The twenty-second scene gives the stigmata their notary. Bonaventure, recounting the obsequies, relates that among the crowd that came to the body was a knight named Jerome — a learned and prudent man, famous and of great name — who, doubting the sacred signs as Thomas the Apostle had doubted, moved the nails and handled the hands, the feet and the side before the friars and the citizens; and in handling the wounds of truth he cut away every wound of doubt from his own heart, becoming thereafter, with others, a sworn witness to what he had touched.

Bonaventure's account of the verification deserves quotation, for it supplies the scene its cast, its publicity and its Apostle (*Legenda maior* XV, 4):

> Inter quos miles quidam litteratus et prudens, Hieronymus nomine, vir utique famosus et celebris, cum de sacris illis signis dubitaret, more Thomae apostoli, […] clavos movit manusque, pedes et latus sancti coram fratribus et civibus palpavit, ut, dum vulnerum Christi palparet signa veritatis, de corde suo et omnium dubietatis vulnus amputaret. Propter quod postmodum, inter alios, huius rei testis extitit constantissimus, et ad veritatem tanti miraculi iuramento firmandam accessit.

"Among them a certain knight, learned and prudent, Jerome by name — a man of note and renown — doubting those sacred signs after the manner of Thomas the Apostle, […] moved the nails, and before the friars and the citizens handled the saint's hands, feet and side, so that, in touching the true signs of the wounds of Christ, he might cut away the wound of doubt from his own heart and from the hearts of all. For this reason he afterwards stood forth, among others, as a most constant witness of the fact, and came forward to confirm the truth of so great a miracle upon oath."

The cycle, which had already given the stigmata their heavenly attestation on La Verna, here supplies the earthly one — the miracle submitted, with juridical seriousness, to the probing of an educated layman’s hand; and the designer stages the probe at the exact centre of the field, the kneeling knight bent over the body, his fingers at the breached side, in the composition’s one moment of contact amid a forest of witnesses. The Doubting Thomas parallel, explicit in the text, is the picture’s armature: Francis, conformed to Christ in his wounds, is conformed to him also in this — that his body, too, must suffer the believer’s finger.

Above the crowd, the painter has built the scene’s second subject, and one of the most precious documents in the cycle: the church interior in which the wake takes place, given by its furnishings alone. Along the top of the field runs the beam of a choir screen, and from it the liturgical sky of a Duecento church hangs complete: lamps on their chains, a pierced metal corona, and three images — at the left a gabled panel of the Virgin and Child enthroned; at the centre a great painted cross of the old type, the living Christ on its apron, leaning forward over the nave on its visible support; at the right a winged figure read as an archangel.

And the sources place the wake at the Porziuncola — Saint Mary of the Angels: the suspicion, advanced in the literature, that the hanging panels of the Virgin and the Angel are the chapel’s own dedication made visible, the generic interior quietly signed with its address, is too elegant to suppress and too clever to assert; it remains a hypothesis.

The ensemble is the deliberate counterpart of Greccio: there the cycle showed us a tramezzo cross from behind, battens and props exposed, from the clergy’s side of the screen; here it shows the same equipment from the front, from the people’s side, as the faithful saw it — the two scenes together forming a little treatise, unique in medieval painting, on how the sacred image was actually mounted and displayed. For the archaeology of the Italian church interior, and for the history of the painted cross from Giunta’s lineage onward, no two frescoes are more instructive.

Below, the crowd is marshalled with the Legend’s late breadth. The bier runs the field’s width on its patterned pall; at its head the saint lies in the habit, the stigmatized hands composed, the face — worn by abrasion — at rest. Friars press in from the left in deep ranks, their tall candles crossing like lances; at the centre right the clergy attend in white, a deacon with the open book chanting the office, acolytes beside him; and threaded through the religious, the laity of Assisi — caps, mantles, the red of civic dignity — for Bonaventure insists that the verification happened before the people, publicity being the point. Jerome kneels among them in his furred robe, a layman given the centre of a sacred picture: the cycle’s last and most pointed instance of its constant theme, the holy delivered into the witness of ordinary men.

The field stands in the last bay of the second wall, where the Legend approaches its close; with the closing scenes (XXV/XXVI–XXVIII, three or four according to the authors) the execution passes, in the view of most critics, into the orbit of the Master of Saint Cecilia, and already here analysis has sensed the transition, the handling tightening toward that refined manner. The designing intelligence, however, is continuous, and the great screen-beam conceit binds the scene to Greccio across the nave with a deliberateness no change of hands dilutes; the question of the design’s name — Giotto for the tradition and prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the Offnerian line — is set out in the section on the Assisi question above. In the Bardi Chapel, a generation later, the mature design would fold Jerome and his probing hand back into the death scene itself, compressing the cycle’s two frescoes into one; the comparison is drawn on that page.

The condition is laboured: the surface is extensively abraded, the saint’s face and the pall worn, the blues of the upper zone bruised and darkened around the hanging images, and pale vertical streaks — the cords and chains of the suspended furnishings, their metal leaf largely lost — now read as scratches across the dark. The titulus below is fragmentary, and the scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. It remains the Legend’s great picture of faith examined: a church’s images looking down, a city looking on, and between them a hand in a wound, doing — on our behalf — what pictures cannot.

### Saint Clare Mourning Saint Francis (Legend of Saint Francis, XXIII)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-51.jpg" alt="Fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing the funeral procession of Saint Francis halted before an ornate white church front with rose windows, sculpted gables and a statue-filled portal. The saint’s body lies on a bier draped with a gold-patterned pall; Saint Clare, haloed in her dark veil, bends over him and embraces him, while another nun lifts his hand and the Poor Clares crowd from the doorway and the right, grieving. At the left presses the lay cortège with tall candles; behind, a man in white climbs a tree, and a bird perches on the topmost gable.">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, Saint Clare and Her Companions Mourning Saint Francis at San Damiano, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XXIII, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The twenty-third scene is the Legend’s farewell between its two saints. Bonaventure relates that on the morning after the transit the people of Assisi came down with the clergy to the Porziuncola and bore the holy body toward the city with hymns and praises, branches and a forest of candles; and that the procession turned aside at San Damiano, where Clare<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-chiara-assisi" class="footnote"><sup>21</sup></a> and her daughters were enclosed, so that the window through which the handmaids of Christ received the Lord’s Body might be opened, and the sisters might see and kiss the hands adorned with the jewels of the Crucified.

Bonaventure's account of the halt at San Damiano gives the scene its window, its kiss and its jewels (*Legenda maior* XV, 5):

> Mane autem facto, […] populus civitatis Assisii cum clero universo corpus sacrum de loco, in quo decesserat, assumentes, cum hymnis et laudibus, tubis clangentibus, ad civitatem deferebant. […] Cumque ad locum pervenissent, in quo sanctae virgines morabantur, sistentes paulisper, sacrum corpus illud, gemmis caelestibus adornatum, ad fenestram obtulerunt, donec domina Clara cum filiabus suis illud conspiceret, deoscularetur manusque illas signis Crucifixi ditatas intueretur.

"When morning was come, […] the people of the city of Assisi with all the clergy took up the sacred body from the place where he had died, and with hymns and praises, trumpets sounding, bore it toward the city. […] And when they had come to the place where the holy virgins dwelt, they halted a while and presented that sacred body, adorned with its heavenly jewels, at the window, until the Lady Clare with her daughters might gaze upon it, kiss it, and behold those hands enriched with the signs of the Crucified."

The painter, it will be noticed, has unlocked what the text keeps enclosed: Bonaventure’s sisters receive the body at their window; the fresco brings them over the threshold, into the open, around the bier — the claustration suspended for the sake of the embrace, and of the picture.

The cycle gives the pause the full weight of a station: the cortège halts, the bier is set down before the church of the women, and the painter composes, around the dead father and the living mother of the movement, the most intimate grief in the twenty-eight scenes. Clare bends over the body and gathers it to herself — not the ritual kiss of the clergy but an embrace, cheek seeking cheek; beside her a sister lifts the stigmatized hand toward her lips, performing to the letter the text’s deosculari; others press from the convent door, still crossing its threshold, and from the right, a chorus of veiled grief graded from stunned stillness to open weeping. For the one scene in the cycle, the drama belongs entirely to women.

The church behind them has exercised every commentator, for it is nothing like the real San Damiano. The humble, half-ruined sanctuary of Scene IV — the very building whose repair began the saint’s mission — returns here transfigured: a sumptuous white front banded with Cosmatesque inlay, twin rose windows, crocketed pinnacles, sculpted angels climbing the gable-slopes, a statue-filled tabernacle over the portal, reliefs in the tympanum, a bird — the painter’s quiet signature of life — perched on the topmost finial.

The contrast with the fresco three bays away cannot be accident, and the readings converge from two directions: the facade as the ideal church, the ruin rebuilt — Francis’s first commission shown complete at the hour of his death, the repair my house of San Damiano answered at last over his bier; and, for those who look toward the women themselves, an anticipation of the great sanctuary of the Clares that Assisi would soon raise. Either way the architecture preaches: the body passes, the building stands finished.

Around the station the painter sets the procession’s forward life. At the left the cortège crowds in — citizens in caps and furred mantles, the clergy’s tall golden candles rising like a palisade, one dignitary in red bending to steady the bier’s pall; and behind, in the scene’s most quoted detail, a man in white has climbed into a tree, breaking the leafy boughs. The motif is a quotation, and it is meant to be recognised: the climber of the Entry into Jerusalem — Zacchaeus of the Gospel adventus — transplanted to Assisi, so that the saint’s last journey to his city reads as a Palm Sunday entry, the Christus patiens of La Verna received like his Lord. The cycle’s habit of conforming Francis to Christ, pressed through stigmata and doubting knight, here annexes the very iconography of the triumphal entry; few strokes in the Legend declare the alter Christus program more boldly, or more gracefully.

The field stands in the closing stretch of the second wall, where the Legend’s principal workshop approaches its handover: with the closing scenes (XXV/XXVI–XXVIII, three or four according to the authors), the execution passes, in the view of most critics, to the refined manner of the Master of Saint Cecilia who had opened the cycle, and analysis has long discussed how far his orbit already touches this bay. The design, in any case, speaks the whole cycle’s language — the halted procession, the architecture as argument, the margin that carries the meaning — and the question of the designer’s name, Giotto for the tradition and prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the Offnerian line, is set out in the section on the Assisi question above. Among its descendants, the embrace of Clare over the body stands behind a century of Tuscan lamentations, and the fresco has always been among the most loved of the series.

The condition is strong in the figures and the great facade, whose whites and inlays survive with unusual freshness; the green-toned ground that now serves for sky is the familiar ruin of the a secco blue, here darkened to a deep mottled verdigris that, by accident, sets the white church glowing. Abrasions wear the crowd at the left and the pall’s gilt pattern; the titulus below is fragmentary, and the scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without structural loss. It remains the Legend’s tenderest invention: a locked community given back, for one hour, the body of its founder — and a city, a church and a tree-climber to tell us that what passes here is a triumph.

### The Canonization of Saint Francis (Legend of Saint Francis, XXIV)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-52.jpg" alt="Heavily worn fresco from the Upper Church at Assisi showing the canonization ceremony: a tall wooden tribune with a canopy and a gold-patterned hanging rises at the left, its occupants almost effaced; below stands an altar draped in white. Around it crowds the assembly — friars, citizens in caps, two mitred bishops at the right, and in the foreground kneeling veiled women. The upper zone and the papal figure are largely lost to abrasion">
  <figcaption>Attributed to Giotto di Bondone and workshop, Gregory IX Proclaiming the Canonization of Saint Francis, Legend of Saint Francis, Scene XXIV, c. 1290–1300. Fresco, c. 270 × 230 cm. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Upper Church, nave.</figcaption>
</figure>

The twenty-fourth scene carries the Legend past death into glory: the canonization. Bonaventure closes the fifteenth chapter of the Legenda maior with it — Gregory IX, the Hugolino who as cardinal had been the order’s protector and the saint’s own friend, came in person to Assisi in 1228, less than two years after the transit, and, after rigorous examination of the miracles and on the counsel of his brethren, inscribed Francis in the catalogue of the saints.

The ceremony was held on 16 July 1228 — septimo decimo Kalendas Augusti, as the editions of the Legenda give the day — and Thomas of Celano, who was present to the events as Bonaventure was not, supplies the place: the church of San Giorgio, where the body had rested since the burial — the same church, he does not fail to note, where the boy Francis had learned his letters and where he had first preached, so that the circle of the life closed where it had opened.

For the basilica this was the founding charter twice over: the canonization called the great double church into being, and on the following day, 17 July, the tradition of the basilica records, Gregory himself laid the first stone of the church that would house both the body and this painted Legend — so that the fresco shows, in effect, the event that caused the walls it is painted on. The Legend’s program, which began with a simple man’s prophecy in the piazza of Assisi, here reaches the prophecy’s fulfilment: magnificently honoured by all the faithful, and by the Church’s supreme voice.

In Bonaventure´s own words (*Legenda maior* XV, 7):

> Interea gloriosis beati viri miraculis mundo fulgentibus, […] felicis recordationis dominus Gregorius papa nonus, […] diligenti habita de miraculis examinatione ac fratrum suorum praelatorumque consilio et assensu, ipsum sanctorum catalogo adscribendum esse decrevit. Veniens igitur personaliter ad civitatem Assisii, anno Dominicae incarnationis millesimo ducentesimo vigesimo octavo, […] cum solemnitate magna et gloria, quam longum esset enarrare, ipsum in sanctorum numero conscripsit.

"Meanwhile, as the glorious miracles of the blessed man shone out upon the world, […] the lord Pope Gregory the Ninth, of happy memory, […] having held a diligent examination of the miracles, and with the counsel and assent of his brethren and of the prelates, decreed that he should be inscribed in the catalogue of the saints. Coming therefore in person to the city of Assisi, in the year of the Lord's incarnation 1228, […] with great solemnity and a glory it would take long to recount, he enrolled him in the number of the saints."

Time, with bitter irony, has treated no scene of the cycle worse, and has taken precisely the voice. The field is ruled by a great timber tribune — a two-storeyed stage with its canopy and gold-diapered hanging, the temporary architecture of papal ceremony rendered with the cycle’s usual carpentering candour — and within it, where Gregory must have sat enthroned in the act of proclamation, the surface is scoured nearly to the plaster: of the pope and his immediate court only ghosts remain, pale silhouettes dissolving into the preparation.

The beholder today attends a canonization whose celebrant has become invisible — a loss that mutilates the subject while, strangely, distilling the theme, for what survives is the thing canonization is actually made of: the assembly. Below the tribune stands the altar under its white cloth, a green frontal glimpsed beneath, a small cleric beside it; and around these fixed points the painter has built the congregation in depth — friars pressing at the stage’s edge, one in profile caught mid-chant; the citizens of Assisi in their caps, rank behind rank; at the right two bishops in white mitres among the prelates and friars; and in the foreground, kneeling, the veiled women of the city, a bank of white headcloths bowed toward the unseen word. The crowd is the best-preserved zone of the fresco, and it is a late masterpiece of the cycle’s crowd-craft: attention itself, graded through dozens of heads, surviving the loss of its object.

The scene stands at the threshold of the cycle’s final change of hands. With the dream of Gregory and the three miracles after it, the execution moves, in the view of most critics, into the orbit of the refined master who had opened the Legend and who closes it — the Master of Saint Cecilia — though how early his share begins, whether here or in the scene following, remains discussed, and the ruin of the surface makes this field the hardest in the cycle to adjudicate.

The design, in any case, keeps faith with the whole: the temporary tribune answers the built architecture of the earlier courts, the kneeling women answer the mourners of San Damiano, and the whole is the work of the single intelligence whose name, Giotto for the tradition and prevailing Italian scholarship, an anonymous master for the Offnerian line, is the question set out in the section on the Assisi question above.

The condition, already described, dominates every account: beyond the effaced tribune, the a secco blue has fallen to a bruised slate and broad abrasions wear even the surviving crowd; the old titulus beneath, which recorded the pope’s coming in person and the solemn inscription among the saints, is as fragmentary as its fellows. The scene passed the earthquake of 1997 without further structural loss. It remains, even as a ruin — perhaps especially as a ruin — a moving conclusion to the Legend’s civic arc: the piazza crowd of Scene I grown into the Church universal, kneeling before a glory that the wall can no longer show and the assembly’s faces still reflect.

## The Epilogue of Miracles: The Legend Passes to the Master of Saint Cecilia

With the canonization the run of the Legend's principal workshop reaches its close. The four scenes that remain — the epilogue of posthumous miracles drawn from the appendix of *Miracula* that Bonaventure attached to the *Legenda maior* — pass, in the view of most critics, wholly or from the second of them on (three or four scenes, XXV/XXVI–XXVIII, according to the authors), to a different and highly individual hand: the refined painter named, from his altarpiece in the Uffizi, the Master of Saint Cecilia, who had opened the cycle with the Homage of the Simple Man and now returns to seal it. The attenuated elegance of his figures, the blond, ornamental key of his palette, the goldsmith's precision of his textiles and towers set the final bay audibly apart from the broad, grave manner that has carried the narrative from the Gift of the Mantle to the saint's obsequies; and the handover itself — one enterprise, several masters, a single governing design — is among the primary data of the Assisi question discussed in the section on the Assisi question above.

The epilogue he painted is a canonization dossier made visible, and its order is a graded demonstration of the new saint's reach. The Dream of Gregory IX heals the last doubt in the highest place, the stigmata attested by a phial of blood delivered to the sleeping pope; the Healing of the Wounded Man of Lerida carries the pierced hands to a sickbed at the far end of the Mediterranean; the Confession of the Woman of Benevento reaches past death itself, retrieving a soul for the width of one unspoken sentence; and the Liberation of Peter of Alife ends the cycle with a suspected heretic freed from a Roman tower — the advocate of popes revealed, in the last field, as the advocate of prisoners. The symmetry with the opening is exact and surely designed: the Legend begins with a layman recognising the saint before anyone, and ends with the saint answering the least promising layman in Christendom; prophecy is closed by mercy.

The four scenes stand, with the eponymous altarpiece and the opening scene of this cycle, at the core of the personality reconstructed for the Master of Saint Cecilia. Here it is enough to mark the threshold: whoever designed the twenty-eight scenes — Giotto, for the tradition that runs from Riccobaldo through Ghiberti to the prevailing Italian scholarship; an anonymous master, for the line that descends from Offner — the Legend closes as it began, in the hands of the most graceful of its executants, with Francis airborne over Rome, going to the one who called.

## Padua: The Arena Chapel as Documented Touchstone

Assisi is disputed. The Arena Chapel in Padua is the anchor. All scholars accept it as the fundamental and most largely autograph work of Giotto's career, and its date is more tightly bracketed than any other major work's. On 6 February 1300 the Paduan Enrico Scrovegni<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-scrovegni-enrico" class="footnote"><sup>22</sup></a> bought land on the site of the Roman arena to build a private chapel dedicated to Santa Maria della Carità, consecrated on the feast of the Annunciation.

Construction probably began in 1302, after Ottobono de' Razzi, newly patriarch of Aquileia, gave his authorisation. On 1 March 1304 Pope Benedict XI granted an indulgence to those who visited "ecclesiam b. Mariae Virginis de Caritate de Arena", which shows the building was then usable. On 9 January 1305 the friars of the neighbouring Eremitani<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-eremitani" class="footnote"><sup>23</sup></a> convent protested that Scrovegni was turning a private oratory into a public church with a bell tower.

Their complaint refers to

> alia multa quae ibi facta sunt potius ad pompam, et ad vanam gloriam,

which some have read as an allusion to the frescoes. On 16 March 1305 the Venetian Great Council agreed to lend Scrovegni hangings "de pannis Sancti Marci" for the consecration of his chapel. That points to the feast of the Annunciation on 25 March 1305, and the decoration was then complete or nearly so. A range of about 1303–1305 for the execution is therefore well founded. Riccobaldo's recollection of the years 1306–1308 confirms that the chapel was then known as Giotto's.

The social and moral context has been read, reasonably, through the lens of usury, of which both Enrico and his father Reginaldo were held guilty. The chapel has been interpreted as an act of reparation and expiation, symbolised in the group at the bottom of the Last Judgment where Scrovegni offers a model of the building to the Virgin: a restitution of wrongly gained wealth turned into an offering. It was probably also meant as a family mausoleum. These readings are plausible and widely held. They are interpretations of the programme, not statements recorded in any document.

The programme itself can be described precisely. On three registers run the stories of Joachim and Anna, of the Virgin, and of Christ's infancy, ministry and Passion. The chancel arch carries the Mission of Gabriel above and the Annunciation divided between the two sides, with the figure of God the Father painted on a panel set into the wall. The entrance wall is filled by the Last Judgment, with the Scrovegni offering and a model of the church that includes a transept the real building lacks. The barrel vault is a starry blue sky with medallions of the Madonna and Child, Christ and prophets.

In the ornamental bands small scenes and figures from the Old Testament are set against the Gospel narratives in a typological relationship. On the dado, painted in monochrome to imitate marble relief, are fourteen personifications of the Virtues and Vices, labelled above and originally accompanied by Latin inscriptions below. The narrative sources include the *Legenda aurea*, the Protevangelium of James and the Gospel of pseudo-Matthew.

Scrovegni apparently intended an even larger cycle. The Death and Glorification of the Virgin were painted in the presbytery by a local artist about a decade later, and the interrupted state of the eastern end may be connected with the Eremitani protest. The two illusionistic "coretti", painted vaulted chambers on either side of the chancel arch that seem to open into the wall, are usually celebrated as the first trompe-l'œil architecture in post-antique painting. The Dizionario Biografico raises the more pointed possibility that they refer to funerary chapels for Enrico and his father that were planned but never built.

The Arena frescoes have been studied from technical and conservation perspectives more intensively than any other work of Giotto. The full restoration by the Istituto Centrale per il Restauro began in the summer of 2001, after years of preparatory study, environmental monitoring and treatment of the exterior. By its first phase in November 2001 it had treated twenty-six narrative scenes and much of the Last Judgment and the starry vault, about forty per cent of the painted surface, identified as the areas at greatest risk. The chapel reopened in March 2002.

The restoration and the studies that accompanied it made it possible to state that execution proceeded in the natural order of fresco, from top to bottom, register by register, probably on a single scaffold spanning the whole chapel. That arrangement let the master intervene in any scene at a given level. The Dizionario Biografico's reconstruction of the division of labour holds that Giotto seems to have been present in every phase and every part, painting chiefly the faces and figures in complex poses. Assistants carried out the preliminary tasks of snapping cords, incising outlines and raising haloes, along with the gilding and the less demanding passages. Assistants' hands can be detected in the Meeting at the Golden Gate, the Entry into Jerusalem, the Last Supper, parts of the Last Judgment and parts of the ornamental bands. They do not emerge as independent personalities.

What makes the Arena Chapel the central statement of Giotto's art is not only its survival but the spatial and narrative intelligence it shows. Painted architecture is usually constructed in oblique perspective. Figures are modelled as solid bodies with strong chiaroscuro and firm contour, so that they stand like reliefs against the blue ground. Above all, the stories are told through gesture and gaze.

The exchange of looks between Christ and Judas at the centre of the Betrayal, the silent tenderness between Mother and Child in the Nativity, and the mourners bent over the dead Christ in the Lamentation are what Villani meant by drawing "ogni figura e atti al naturale", and what Boccaccio meant by an art addressed to the intellect of the wise. The work also contains the elements that later readers found most modern: crudely realistic portrait heads among the resurrected in the Judgment, rough comedy among the damned, and the matter-of-fact account of violent crime in the allegory of Injustice.

The chapel also carries the most widely repeated modern claim about Giotto that has no documentary basis. The comet that stands in for the star above the stable in the Adoration of the Magi is routinely described as a portrait of Halley's comet, which Giotto is supposed to have seen at its apparition in the autumn of 1301. The European Space Agency named its 1985–1986 probe to Halley's comet "Giotto" on that premise. Its own page on the painter still states that he "gave us the first unambiguous portrait of a comet which must have been Halley". The identification is modern.

It was proposed in May 1979 in an article in *Scientific American* titled "Giotto's Portrait of Halley's Comet", which called the painted comet "a naturalistic portrait of Halley's Comet as seen during its spectacular apparition of 1301". No fourteenth-century source connects Giotto's painted comet with any particular celestial event. Giovanni Villani does describe a comet with "great rays of fumes behind", and it is quite plausible that a painter who had seen a real comet chose to paint the star of Bethlehem as one. But the specific link to Halley has been questioned.

A 1993 paper in the *Quarterly Journal of the Royal Astronomical Society* already asked whether Giotto's comet was "the Comet of 1304 and not Comet Halley". A recent article in the *Journal of the British Astronomical Association* concludes that the 1301 apparition "was theoretically a poor one", with "the calculated magnitude always fainter than +1". Two comets of 1304 have been proposed as more likely models.

One, C/1304 C1, was visible for about two and a half months between February and April. The other, a brief "Christmas comet" designated C/1304 Y1, was recorded over six days that included Christmas Eve and Christmas Day, which some find especially suggestive for a devout painter at work on the chapel. The honest conclusion is that Giotto's comet is a striking early naturalistic depiction of a comet. Its identification with Halley is a modern hypothesis that can be argued but not proven.

The Arena Chapel was part of a larger Paduan activity that has almost entirely disappeared. Riccobaldo records work in the Franciscan church, that is, the Santo. Michele Savonarola, around 1440, says Giotto painted the chapter house there, and damaged fragments of a Crucifixion, a Stigmatization, a martyrdom of Franciscans and monochrome prophets and saints have been recovered in that room. Their autograph status is disputed.

The astrological cycle in the Palazzo della Ragione, recorded by Riccobaldo and by Giovanni da Nono around 1320, is completely lost. The double-sided painted crucifix made for the chapel's rood screen, now in the Musei Civici, is part of the decoration, although its date and the extent of Giotto's own hand in it are debated. Paduan illuminators, who adapted compositions from the chapel in the cathedral antiphonaries, show how quickly the frescoes became models.

## Panel Painting: Crucifixes, Polyptychs, Madonnas, and the Problem of the Signature

### Crucifix of Santa Maria Novella: The Body Made True

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-01.jpg" alt="Monumental painted cross by Giotto. The dead Christ hangs heavily with bowed head, sagging body and overlapping feet against a red apron patterned in gold; half-length figures of the mourning Virgin and Saint John occupy the lateral terminals, a trilingual titulus the summit, and the rock of Golgotha with the skull of Adam the trapezoidal foot">
  <figcaption>Crucifix, c. 1290–1295, tempera and gold on panel, 578 × 406 cm. Florence, Basilica di Santa Maria Novella.</figcaption>
</figure>

The painted crucifix of Santa Maria Novella is the first securely documented work by Giotto on panel. Its documentation shows the value of wills as evidence. In 1312 a Florentine named Riccuccio di Puccio del Mugnaio left money to buy oil for a lamp to burn before the cross in Santa Maria Novella, painted, the will says,

> per egregium pictorem nomine Giottum Bondonis.

This is a *terminus ante quem*, certain but distant. Stylistic and circumstantial arguments suggest a date in the 1290s. The Lucchese painter [Deodato Orlandi](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Deodato-Orlandi.html) still followed the Cimabuesque type in a cross dated 1288 but adopted something very like the Santa Maria Novella type in one dated 1301. A cross of the same kind appears in the Assisi scene of the Verification of the Stigmata.

The carpentry preserves the traditional apparatus of the Tuscan *croce dipinta* while quietly transforming its proportions. The cross-field is bordered in deep blue within gilt and vermilion mouldings; behind the body extends the *tabellone*, painted as a crimson textile strewn with gold ornament, a fictive cloth of honour whose flatness measures, by contrast, the astonishing projection of the figure. At the summit a stepped tablet carries the titulus in three superimposed bands of Hebrew, Greek and Latin characters, a learned rendering of the trilingual inscription recorded in John 19:19–20.

The shaft descends to a trapezoidal suppedaneum in which the painter has set the rock of Golgotha, split open to reveal the skull of Adam. The restoration conducted by the same Opificio delle Pietre Dure established that the structure was first conceived according to the conventional measurements of Duecento production: had the design not been modified, the cross would have closely resembled Cimabue's in Santa Croce. The slimming of the format belongs, in other words, to the genesis of the work itself, as though the new conception of the body demanded a new economy of frame.

That body is the revolution.

The Christ of Santa Maria Novella abandons the elegant lateral arc of the Cimabuesque *Christus patiens*, whose torso swings out beyond the cross-field in a great calligraphic curve, and hangs instead almost vertically, pulled downward by its own mass. The innovation is often misdescribed: the iconographic type — the heavily suspended dead Christ with bent knees and overlapping feet — had already appeared in the sculpture of [Nicola Pisano](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Nicola-Pisano.html), and the painter's debt to contemporary sculpture has long been recognised in the scholarship. What is unprecedented is pictorial: a body that seems genuinely nailed to the wood, its weight transmitted through the stretched arms, the shoulders dragged upward, the abdomen slackened forward, the knees buckling under the dead load. The faithful standing in the nave saw, for the first time in Florentine painting, not a sign of the Crucified but the physical fact of a crucified man.

The modelling sustains this claim at every point. Light falls evenly across the greenish-ivory flesh, describing the cage of the ribs, the hollow of the stomach, the tendons of the stretched arms, without recourse to the linear striations of the maniera greca. The loincloth is a passage of pure painting intelligence: a veil of nearly transparent linen through which the thighs remain legible, gathered at the hip by a saffron sash whose opacity measures, by contrast, the gauze it binds. The head, treated below, completes the effect: nothing in the figure appeals to the viewer, and its pathos is entirely a function of its truth.

The blood completes the theological argument with the same literalness that governs the anatomy: it obeys gravity, as the body does, and descends to the skull of Adam. For a Dominican public formed by a preaching order's insistence on the concrete humanity of Christ, the cross functioned as a visual sermon, and it is reasonable to suppose — though no document states it — that the friars' own devotional culture shaped the commission.

The lateral terminals hold the mourning Virgin and Saint John, reduced to two half-length figures isolated against gold and treated below.

The attribution, so firmly anchored in 1312, nonetheless became one of the battlegrounds of twentieth-century criticism. Richard Offner, in the 1939 essay "Giotto, non-Giotto", denied the cross to the master along with the Assisi Francis cycle, eventually constructing a separate "Master of the Santa Maria Novella Cross" to receive it; the darkened and uneven condition of the surface lent his scepticism a certain plausibility.

The Opificio restoration dissolved that plausibility. The cleaning recovered the full quality of the painting and its underdrawing, and the technical studies that accompanied it placed the cross in the closest relation to the latest biblical frescoes of the Upper Church at Assisi, above all the Isaac scenes — a convergence that has persuaded most recent criticism of the work's autograph status and made it, for the attributionist tradition, a documentary bridge between the Florentine Giotto of the will and the disputed painter of Assisi. The cross thus carries an evidentiary weight far beyond itself: whoever accepts it as Giotto's, and registers its kinship with the Isaac Master, has already taken a position in the Assisi question.

Function and placement, finally, explain the scale. A cross of nearly six metres was made to be seen from a distance and from below, and the scholarship connects it with the *tramezzo*, the great screen that divided the friars' choir from the lay nave of the Dominican church. The present suspension in the middle of the nave, adopted at the conclusion of the restoration, was determined on the basis of historical research into the position of the lost screen, so that the modern visitor encounters the work approximately where the fourteenth-century layman did: high, frontal, and inescapable. Seen there, the cross makes concrete what Cennino Cennini meant when he wrote that Giotto translated painting from Greek into Latin. The format, the gold, the mourners and the titulus all belong to the inherited language; the body that hangs among them speaks the new one.

#### The Head of Christ: Death Without Rhetoric

The head is where the new conception of the image announces itself most quietly and most completely. It does not incline in the graceful lateral tilt of the Cimabuesque tradition, where the face remains presented to the viewer even in death; it falls, pulled forward and down by its own weight, so that the chin approaches the chest and the features are seen foreshortened from below — precisely the view a spectator standing beneath a six-metre cross would have of a real suspended body. The foreshortening is not a display of skill but a consequence of truth: the painter has accepted that a dead head obeys gravity, and has drawn what follows.

The face itself is emptied of the expressive apparatus of the maniera greca. The eyes are closed without contraction, the brows unknotted, the mouth slack within the short beard; the long hair, parted at the crown, slides over the shoulder in heavy auburn strands whose fall again registers weight rather than pattern. The flesh is modelled in the pallid, faintly greenish tonality of death, built up in gradual transitions of light and shadow across the cheekbones, the orbits and the bridge of the nose, with none of the linear striations that earlier painting used to signify volume. What results is a face that does not perform its suffering: the pathos lies entirely in the fact that suffering is over.

Behind the head, the halo is raised in gilded pastiglia relief, its cruciform arms ornamented and projecting physically from the panel, so that actual light — the candlelight of the Dominican church — would have caught and moved across it. The contrast is calculated: the most material, most goldsmith-like element of the whole cross frames its most naturalistically observed passage. Tradition and innovation are held in a single span, the inherited sacred apparatus enclosing a human head painted as no Florentine had painted one before.

The restoration completed by the Opificio delle Pietre Dure in 2001 recovered this passage from the darkened varnishes that had long muted it, and it is above all in the head that the kinship with the Isaac scenes noted above shows. The comparison is a stylistic argument, not a document, but it is the argument on which much of the current consensus about the young Giotto rests.

#### The Mourning Virgin: Grief Contained

The left terminal of the cross holds the Virgin, reduced to a half-length figure isolated against burnished gold within the moulded frame of the *capocroce*. The reduction is itself significant: the crowded narrative compartments that earlier Tuscan crosses set at the arm-ends, with their files of mourning women, have contracted here to a single presence, as though the painter had decided that one truthfully observed grief outweighs a chorus of conventional ones.

The Virgin is wrapped in a maphorion of deep blue — now somewhat darkened, as the blues of this period commonly are — over a crimson garment, the mantle drawn up over her head in the traditional manner. Her head inclines toward the crucified body at the centre of the cross, and one hand rises toward her face, gathering the edge of the mantle in a gesture that belongs to the oldest Mediterranean vocabulary of mourning and yet is here slowed, weighted, made almost inward. Nothing is torn or contorted; the sorrow is carried in the pressure of the hand and the angle of the head.

The face is modelled with the same gradual, luminous transitions as the body of Christ, the features drawn fine and the gaze lowered, and it is in such passages that one measures the distance from Cimabue's mourners, whose grief is written in the graphic contraction of brow and mouth. Giotto's Virgin grieves as a person does when grief has lasted: with economy. The figure anticipates, at small scale and a decade in advance, the mourning women of the Paduan Lamentation, and it addresses the lay congregation of Santa Maria Novella at exactly the point where Dominican preaching sought them — compassion before the humanity of Christ, modelled by the mother who stands perpetually at the cross.

#### Saint John the Evangelist: The Witness

The right terminal answers the Virgin with the beloved disciple, the two half-lengths framing the dead Christ as the abbreviated cast of the Johannine Crucifixion — the mother and the witness to whom she was entrusted. John appears in his canonical youth, beardless, with short auburn hair, clothed in a rose mantle whose warm tonality balances the cold blue of the Virgin opposite; the chromatic dialogue across the four metres of the crossbeam is precise, and it binds the terminals to the crimson and gold of the central apron.

His attitude is the ancient one of melancholy and mourning: the head inclined, the cheek supported against the raised hand. It is a formula with a long Byzantine ancestry, and the painter does not discard it — he inhabits it. The hand genuinely presses the flesh of the cheek; the head genuinely rests its weight upon the hand; the gesture, in short, is re-grounded in the physics of an actual body, which is the same operation performed on the crucified figure at the centre. Even where Giotto retains the inherited sign, he insists that the sign be structurally true.

The modelling of the young face, softer and fuller than the Virgin's, shows the painter adjusting his light to the age and substance of each sitter — a differentiation of flesh that the maniera greca, with its uniform graphic conventions, could not attempt. Within the economy of the cross, John carries a double charge: he mourns, completing the affective triangle that the viewer is invited to enter, and he witnesses, for it is on his Gospel's authority that the scene's every element — the mother, the disciple, the lance wound, the trilingual titulus above — stands. The terminal thus quietly certifies the historicity of the image it flanks.

#### The Suppedaneum: Golgotha and the Skull of Adam

The shaft of the cross terminates in a trapezoidal suppedaneum, and here the painter sets the final clause of the image's theological sentence. The field is occupied by the bare rock of Golgotha, painted in sober greys and earth tones, its mass modelled by the same even light that describes the body above — a piece of observed geology rather than the stepped, ornamental mound of earlier convention. At its base, in a dark cavity, lies the skull of Adam with scattered bones beside it.

The blood makes the connection literal. From the nailed feet it descends the lower shaft in long, thin rivulets — obedient, like everything in this cross, to gravity — until it reaches the rock and runs down over it toward the skull. The motif renders visible the exegetical tradition, already ancient by the thirteenth century, that located the tomb of the first man beneath the place of the Crucifixion, so that the blood of the second Adam might wash the bones of the first: redemption depicted as a physical event, a liquid actually arriving at its destination. On a cross whose governing idea is the truth of the body, even doctrine is made to flow.

The trapezoidal suppedaneum belongs to the carpentry tradition of the Tuscan *croce dipinta*, and as the broadest surviving expanse of the cross outside the apron it preserves something of the inherited scheme while receiving the most radically unconventional of its images: not a saint, not an ornament, but a landscape of death awaiting its redemption.

Seen from the nave floor — and this detail hung closest to the viewer's eye level of any part of the six-metre machine — the Golgotha panel was the point where the faithful's gaze entered the image and began its ascent along the track of the blood toward the body. The composition, in other words, is also an itinerary of devotion, and it starts here, at the skull.

#### The Trilingual Titulus: The Inscription of the Kingship

At the summit of the shaft, above the halo, the cross terminates in a stepped tablet whose red field carries three superimposed bands of gold lettering. The detail is easily passed over from the nave floor, six metres below, and yet it is among the most learned passages of the whole machine: the painter has rendered the titulus of the cross not in the single Latin line customary on Tuscan painted crosses of the Duecento, but in the three scripts — Hebrew, Greek and Latin — that the Gospel of John specifies. The source is exact: "scriptum est autem hebraice, graece et latine" (John 19:19–20), the evangelist's notice that Pilate's inscription, "Iesus Nazarenus Rex Iudaeorum", was written in the three languages of the ancient world so that all who passed might read it.

The choice is first of all a philological act, and it belongs to the same intellectual programme as everything else on the cross. Just as the body obeys gravity and the blood finds the skull of Adam, so the inscription obeys its textual source: the image is correct not only anatomically but scripturally. The upper band imitates the square Hebrew letters, the middle the Greek, the lower the Latin, with a care for the distinct physiognomy of each script that presupposes either direct models or informed counsel — and in a Dominican convent of the 1290s, a house of studies where Scripture was the daily instrument of preaching and disputation, such counsel was close at hand. It is reasonable to see in the trilingual tablet the trace of the commissioning friars' learning meeting the painter's appetite for the verifiable, though no document records the exchange, and the observation must remain an inference from the object itself.

Theologically, the three scripts carry the universal claim of the image. The titulus was written, John insists, for every passer-by to read; on the tramezzo of Santa Maria Novella the tablet performed the same office for the city, proclaiming the kingship of the Crucified over the three tongues — and by extension the whole order — of human knowledge. There is an irony folded into the iconography that medieval exegesis did not fail to savour: the inscription composed by Pilate in mockery becomes, fixed above the dead Christ, the truest line in the picture. The painter honours that paradox by giving the tablet the richest chromatic accent of the upper cross, gold on vermilion, so that the proclamation burns above the pallid body it names.

Within the carpentry, the tablet also marks the point where the structural history of the cross remains most open. Many Duecento crosses crowned the titulus with a further element — a cimasa with the blessing Redeemer or other imagery — and the Opificio delle Pietre Dure restoration established that the present silhouette of the cross results from modifications to an initial layout of traditional proportions. What the summit originally comprised, and whether anything stood above the tablet, are questions for the technical literature of the restoration rather than for inspection of the surface; the same holds for the exact epigraphic transcription of the three bands, since the legibility of the Hebrew and Greek characters at this height, and the degree of their philological accuracy as opposed to evocative imitation, is precisely the kind of detail on which the published studies are authoritative and a photograph is not.

Other panels are placed close to the crucifix: the fragmentary Madonna from the pieve of Borgo San Lorenzo and the Madonna from San Giorgio alla Costa, now in the diocesan museum of Santo Stefano al Ponte. Ghiberti already listed the latter among Giotto's works, and the Opificio has restored both in recent decades.

### Madonna of San Giorgio alla Costa

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-07.jpg" alt="Panel painting by Giotto on gold ground showing the Virgin enthroned frontally, in a blue-black mantle over a red dress, holding the Christ Child on her left knee; the Child, in a pale blue tunic and pink mantle, raises his right hand in blessing and holds a scroll. Behind the throne’s patterned cloth of honour two angels lean inward over the backrest, hands resting on it. The throne bears cosmatesque inlay and a red cushion, and a pink finial crowns the gold ground; the panel’s edges are cut, with the modern support visible around the painted field">
  <figcaption>Madonna and Child Enthroned with Two Angels, c. 1295. Tempera and gold on panel, 180 × 90 cm (cut down on all sides). Florence, Museo Diocesano di Santo Stefano al Ponte; from the church of San Giorgio alla Costa.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Madonna of San Giorgio alla Costa is the throne on which the young Giotto’s revolution first sat down. Painted around 1295 for the little church of San Giorgio on the slope of the Costa San Giorgio in the Oltrarno — Ghiberti, in the Commentari, already records a panel by Giotto there, the earliest documentary thread the picture possesses — it belongs to the same explosive moment as the Crucifix of Santa Maria Novella treated above: the years in which a painter formed on Cimabue began replacing the weightless grammar of the icon with bodies that sit, cloth that hangs, and furniture that occupies room. The panel has been cut down on every side — the gable lost, the throne’s architecture truncated, the stepped base gone, so that the surviving field floats on its modern support like a window onto a larger lost wall — and yet what remains is complete in the way that matters: a Maestà reduced to its thesis.

The thesis is the throne. Where the great Duecento Madonnas enthrone the Virgin on goldsmith’s structures seen flat against the gold, Giotto builds a piece of joinery in depth: the cosmatesque inlay of the uprights runs obliquely into space, the red cushion compresses under real weight, the patterned cloth of honour hangs behind the figures as a true backdrop — and, decisive stroke, the two angels do not float beside the throne but stand behind it, leaning in over the backrest with their hands laid on its edge, like courtiers looking over a chairback. That small domestic liberty — angels with somewhere to stand — is the whole future of Florentine painting in miniature: the sacred image has acquired a behind. The pair incline toward the axis with a symmetry that is almost, not quite, mirror-true, their dalmatics patterned, their wings cropped by the panel’s reduced edges; above, at the summit of the gold, a pink finial survives from the throne’s lost crowning, the one trace of the architecture the cutting took away.

Virgin and Child hold the axis with the gravity of the painter’s first manner. She sits frontal, monumental under the blue-black mantle whose few folds fall with the new weight, the red dress at the throat and the long, extraordinary right hand — fingers of an almost Mannerist length, resting on the Child’s knee — supplying the warm notes; the face, firm-jawed and wide-eyed, is sister to the San Lorenzo fragment and the Santa Maria Novella Christ, the same grave oval that criticism takes as the signature of the Assisi years.

The Child sits high on her left knee, pale blue and pink, blessing with raised right hand and gripping the scroll of the Law in his left — not an infant playing, as at Borgo San Lorenzo or Washington, but the small Lord of the older formula, retained here at the threshold of the new style: the picture stands exactly at the hinge, iconic in program, revolutionary in construction. Attribution follows that hinge: assigned to the young Giotto by the line of criticism that binds it to the Santa Maria Novella Crucifix and the first Assisi campaigns, it has served the scholarship — [Previtali](/scholars.html#previtali-giovanni), Boskovits, Bellosi among its sponsors — as a cornerstone of the painter’s beginnings, with the usual dissents registered and the Assisi question, as ever, adjoining; the section on the Assisi question above sets out the debate.

The panel’s modern history includes a wound: on 27 May 1993 the mafia bomb of Via dei Georgofili, which struck the heart of Florence, gravely damaged the church of Santo Stefano al Ponte where the painting was kept, and the Madonna itself was injured in the blast — restored at the Opificio delle Pietre Dure, it returned to hang in the Museo Diocesano established in that same church, bearing, invisibly now, the scar of the Republic’s darkest season of terror. It hangs there today as the museum’s chief treasure: the earliest surviving throne of the new painting — cut, bombed, restored — still doing, after seven centuries, the simple unprecedented thing it was built for: holding weight.

### Badia Polyptych: The Archetype of the Trecento Altarpiece

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-03.jpg" alt="Five-panel gabled polyptych by Giotto on gold ground: at the centre the half-length Virgin holds the Christ Child, who reaches toward her; to the left Saint Nicholas in bishop's vestments and Saint John the Evangelist with book and pen, to the right a saint in a pallium with a staff and a book, and Saint Benedict in a dark habit; a small bust in a medallion crowns each pointed gable.">
  <figcaption>Madonna and Child with Saints Nicholas, John the Evangelist, Peter and Benedict (Badia Polyptych), c. 1295–1300, tempera and gold on panel, 142 × 337 cm. Florence, Galleria degli Uffizi, from the Badia Fiorentina.</figcaption>
</figure>

Among the panels of Giotto's early maturity, the Badia Polyptych enjoys a documentary fortune that most of its companions can envy. Ghiberti, in the second *Commentario*, records a panel by Giotto on the high altar of the Badia Fiorentina, the Benedictine<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-benedictine-order" class="footnote"><sup>24</sup></a> abbey at the heart of Florence, and Vasari repeats the notice; Ghiberti also remembered, separately, a half-length Madonna frescoed by Giotto over the abbey's door, of which fragments survive. The polyptych itself then slipped out of sight. Replaced on the high altar in the sixteenth century, displaced within the convent, and swept out of the Badia altogether by the Napoleonic suppressions of 1810 — a journey that took it first to San Marco and then, by 1813, into the holdings of Santa Croce — it spent more than a century in the wrong church under the wrong assumptions, its only link to its origin a cartouche reading "Badia di Firenze" applied at the moment of the suppression.

The recognition belongs to one of the celebrated episodes of twentieth-century Florentine connoisseurship. In 1940, as works of art were being evacuated to wartime deposits, [Ugo Procacci](/scholars.html#procacci-ugo) saw the polyptych being carried out of Santa Croce and understood what the label implied: that this was the lost high-altarpiece of the Badia recorded by Ghiberti, removed under the French occupation and casually reabsorbed into Santa Croce's patrimony. Only with that identification did the attribution cease to rest on style alone and acquire the support of an early source — the same shift in evidentiary status that the will of Riccuccio di Puccio performs for the Santa Maria Novella Crucifix. A heavy-handed accretion history was subsequently undone: the restoration of 1958 removed layers of overpaint and a later superstructure that had concealed the gables, returning the altarpiece to its five-pointed silhouette. Having escaped the 1966 flood, it entered the Uffizi, where it has been exhibited since that year; a further campaign of restoration in the Uffizi laboratories, around 2008–2009, prepared it for the great Giotto exhibitions of the following seasons.

What the 1958 intervention recovered is, structurally, the point of the work. Five panels, each closed by a steep crocketed gable with a trilobed arch on slender colonnettes, rise from a continuous moulded predella-base; a small bust-length figure in a medallion occupies each cusp, and a red titulus at the top of each field names the saint below.
