The whole is carpentered as a single horizontal machine, and it is in this coherence — five half-length figures of equal dignity, unified by architecture, inscription and gold — that recent scholarship has seen the archetype of the Trecento polyptych, the format that Florentine and Sienese workshops would repeat for a century. The identification of the small cusp figures, and the degree to which the present framing elements preserve original carpentry as against nineteenth- and twentieth-century reintegration, are matters for the technical literature.

The centre belongs to the Madonna and Child, and Giotto fills the inherited half-length format with an invention of quiet audacity. The Virgin, in a deep blue mantle edged in gold over a red dress, does not present the Child frontally to the worshipper; she turns toward him, and he toward her, his small hand reaching up toward the neckline of her dress in a gesture drawn from the ancient intimacy of the Galaktotrophousa tradition and here naturalised into infant behaviour. The exchange of gazes between mother and son — a closed circuit of attention into which the viewer looks as a witness rather than an addressee — converts the icon into a relationship. It is the same narrative intelligence that animates the Paduan frescoes, compressed into the still format of the altarpiece.

The flanking saints address the abbey's own devotions. At the far left stands Nicholas of Myra — or of Bari — in full episcopal vestments, mitred, his crozier against his shoulder and a red-bound book in his draped hand, his pallium falling in crisp white bands over a chasuble whose ornament is rendered with a goldsmith's patience. Beside him the young John the Evangelist, auburn-haired and beardless as on the terminal of the Santa Maria Novella cross, holds his book and raises his pen — the writer caught between inspiration and act. Across the centre, the saint identified as Peter, shown with the pallium, the staff and the book, has a short grey beard and a broad skull modelled with the blunt portrait-like candour that Giotto reserves for the prince of the apostles; and at the far right Benedict, titular of the abbey's order, closes the sequence in the dark monastic habit, his aged face austere, a scroll in his covered hand. The five figures are differentiated not by hieratic rank but by age, temperament and flesh: the painter has distributed humanity across the row as evenly as the gold behind it.

The pictorial language confirms the dating debate's terms rather than settling them. The figures possess the full, column-like volume, the gradually modelled flesh and the grave psychological presence of the Santa Maria Novella Crucifix and the San Giorgio alla Costa Madonna, and details of the drawing and ornament have long been connected with the Rimini crucifix and, inevitably, with Assisi — so that the polyptych too sits on the attributionist bridge between documented Florence and the disputed Upper Church. Critical datings range from the mid-1290s, favoured by those who read the work as contemporary with the crucifix, to the opening years of the fourteenth century; the Uffizi's own materials have endorsed dates both around 1300 and as precise as 1301–1302, while a strand of criticism has pushed the panel after Padua. The bracket 1295–1300 adopted here represents the centre of gravity of recent opinion, but it is a critical judgment, not a documented fact — the only document is Ghiberti's notice, a century and a half after the event.

The published dimensions of the polyptych vary appreciably across authoritative sources — figures of 137.5 × 345 cm and measurements excluding the frame near 91 × 334 cm circulate alongside the 142 × 337 cm of the caption — a spread that evidently reflects different decisions about whether the carpentry is included in the measurement.

Seen whole, the Badia Polyptych occupies a strategic position in the reconstruction of the early Giotto. With the Santa Maria Novella Crucifix it shares a documentary anchor in an early source and the demonstration of the weighted body; with the San Giorgio alla Costa Madonna it shares the humanised address of the sacred half-length; and beyond both, it adds the dimension of the altarpiece as architecture — the painter as designer of a liturgical machine, ordering five presences into a single colonnade of gold. The monks of the Badia, singing the office daily before it, possessed in one object the summary of what Florentine painting had just become; and the modern visitor to the Uffizi, standing before the same object between Cimabue's and [Duccio](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Duccio-di-Buoninsegna.html)'s great Maestà panels and Giotto's own Ognissanti Madonna, can measure in a single room the translation — Cennini's word — of painting from Greek into Latin.

### Crucifix of the Tempio Malatestiano: The Cross Refined

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-08.jpg" alt="Painted cross by Giotto in Rimini: the dead Christ hangs with bowed head and nearly vertical body against a dark field edged in gold, over an apron of gold damask patterning; a white translucent loincloth wraps the hips, a red tablet at the summit bears the Latin titulus, and the lateral terminals are lost">
  <figcaption>Crucifix, c. 1300, tempera and gold on panel, 430 × 303 cm. Rimini, Tempio Malatestiano (Cathedral Basilica of Santa Colomba), from the church of San Francesco.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Rimini Crucifix rests on the earliest external testimony that any work by Giotto outside Florence can claim. Riccobaldo Ferrarese, writing around 1312–1313 and drawing on memories of his Paduan years of 1306–1308, lists among the proofs of Giotto's excellence his paintings in the Franciscan churches of Assisi, Rimini and Padua; and the great cross that still hangs in the former church of San Francesco — transformed in the fifteenth century into the Tempio Malatestiano and, since 1809, bearer of the cathedral title of Santa Colomba — is the one surviving candidate for that Riminese notice. The chain is not quite a document in the archival sense: Riccobaldo names the church, not the object. But a monumental cross of the highest Giottesque quality, in the very building the chronicler indicates, within years of his writing, constitutes as strong a circumstantial case as the early Trecento ever provides, and the attribution has commanded broad agreement in modern criticism.

The chronology is bracketed by the work's own progeny. A leaf signed by the illuminator Neri da Rimini and dated 1300 has been read as already reflecting the cross, which would place it at the very turn of the century; and the Crucifix painted by [Giovanni da Rimini](/Content/Artists/XIV-c/Giovanni-da-Rimini.html) for San Francesco at Mercatello sul Metauro, bearing a date read as 1309 (or by some as 1314), presupposes Giotto's model fully absorbed. The Fototeca Zeri's cataloguing adopts 1301–1302, and most recent opinion sets the cross around 1300, immediately before the Paduan enterprise — a station, as it were, on the road to the Arena Chapel. The reading of the Neri leaf as a terminus ante quem is an inference from style; but the convergence of the miniature evidence, the Mercatello date and the cross's own position between Santa Maria Novella and Padua gives the dating c. 1300 an unusually firm triangulation.

Like its Florentine elder sibling, the Rimini cross survives mutilated, though differently. The lateral terminals that once held the mourning Virgin and Saint John are lost, so that the arms now end abruptly where the half-length *dolenti* should answer one another across the body; the summit, however, preserves its red tablet with the titulus — here in Latin alone, disposed in three lines of gold Gothic lettering, without the trilingual display of Santa Maria Novella. The crowning element had a happier fate than the terminals: the cimasa with the blessing Redeemer, sawn from the cross at an unknown date, was recognised by [Federico Zeri](/scholars.html#zeri-federico) in an English private collection and published in his *Due appunti su Giotto* of 1957, restoring to the monument, at least on paper, its vertical conclusion.

The carpentry marks a further step in the evolution of the type. At Santa Maria Novella the restoration revealed a structure first laid out to traditional Duecento measurements and then slimmed; at Rimini the slimming has become the design itself. The silhouette is profiled — *sagomata* — in steps and lobes that follow the figure, the field contracting around the body so that cross and Christ approach a single contour; the dark ground, edged in bands of gold and fine ornament, reads less as a panel on which a body is painted than as a shadow the body casts. The apron behind the torso and legs is a continuous sheet of gold damask patterning, the fictive cloth of honour now so luminous that the pale flesh stands against it as against light itself.

The body, too, has been rethought since Florence. The Riminese Christ is slenderer, longer in proportion, the arms stretched nearly straight along the horizontal, the torso less massively projected and more continuously luminous, the legs descending with only the gentlest flexion to the overlapped feet. The weight is still truthful — the shoulders still pull, the head still falls — but the demonstration of gravity that governed the Santa Maria Novella cross has been absorbed into an ideal of refinement: the modelling is softer, the transitions of light longer, the pathos quieter. Criticism has long registered in this cross a Gothic inflection, a courtly elegance, within Giotto's naturalism, and it is this very tempering that has persuaded most scholars to set it after the Florentine cross and on the threshold of Padua, where the same luminous, continent manner governs the Christ of the chancel-arch and the painted crucifix of the chapel itself.

The details sustain the reading. The head, fallen forward within its burnished halo, is modelled with extreme delicacy — the fine brows, the shadowed orbits, the thin bridge of the nose, the parted hair and short beard drawn with a miniaturist's control that explains why the Riminese illuminators were the first to respond. The loincloth is no longer the saffron-sashed gauze of Florence but a fuller cloth of white, knotted at the hip and falling in soft translucent folds through which the thigh remains visible — a passage of extraordinary technical daring whose whites, greys and shadows anticipate the drapery intelligence of the Arena frescoes. Even the blood is reticent: thin threads at the hands and feet, a brief note at the side wound, the rhetoric of redemption reduced to its quietest sufficient signs.

No work by Giotto generated a school more directly. The cross descended into the painting of Romagna like a seed: Giovanni da Rimini translated it at Mercatello and in the Diotallevi cross; [Giuliano da Rimini](/Content/Artists/XIV-c/Giuliano-da-Rimini.html), Neri the illuminator, Pietro da Rimini and later Giovanni Baronzio built upon it the distinctive Riminese Trecento — intimate, silvery, tender — whose independence from Florence is precisely the measure of how completely the local painters digested the single Giottesque monument in their midst. The descent can be followed in the pages on Giovanni da Rimini and Giuliano da Rimini; the present cross is the fountainhead they share. Riccobaldo's notice, the Neri leaf and the Mercatello date thus interlock with the visual evidence into one of the most coherent chapters of early Trecento geography: a visit attested by Riccobaldo, a surviving masterpiece, and a school to prove it.

The later history of the building has wrapped the cross in an unexpected frame. The Franciscan church for which it was painted was reclothed in the 1450s by Leon Battista Alberti and the artists of Sigismondo Malatesta's court into the Tempio Malatestiano, the key monument of the Riminese Renaissance, and raised to cathedral rank with the title of Santa Colomba — inherited from the demolished old cathedral — in 1809. Giotto's cross is the principal survivor of the medieval church inside the humanist shell, the Trecento heart of a Quattrocento body; the accounts in circulation differ on its exact present placement within the Tempio and on the history and date of its modern restorations.

### Ognissanti Madonna: The Maestà Translated

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-04.jpg" alt="Gabled panel by Giotto: the Virgin enthroned in a Gothic marble tabernacle holds the blessing Christ Child; two angels kneel in the foreground offering vases of lilies and roses, two more hold crowns beside the throne, and ranks of angels and saints gather in depth on either side against a gold ground">
  <figcaption>Virgin and Child Enthroned with Angels and Saints (Ognissanti Madonna), c. 1305–1310, tempera and gold on panel, 325 × 204 cm. Florence, Galleria degli Uffizi, from the church of Ognissanti.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Ognissanti Madonna is the panel on which the whole critical tradition agrees. It carries no signature and no contemporary document; its earliest mention dates only from 1418, and its attribution to Giotto rests on style alone — yet that attribution has never been seriously contested, surviving intact even the most radical scepticism of the twentieth century, which denied the master the Assisi cycle and the Santa Maria Novella cross but left this panel untouched. The reason is visible at a glance: nothing else in Florentine painting of the first decade of the Trecento commands this fullness of volume, this gravity of presence, this intelligence of space. The painting has therefore come to function as a touchstone second only to Padua, and the Uffizi's decision to hang it in a single room with Cimabue's Santa Trinita Maestà and Duccio's Rucellai Madonna deliberately stages the Vasarian narrative of origins — three generations of the enthroned Virgin, and between the first two and the third, a change of world.

The provenance ties the panel to the Umiliati, the wool-working order whose Florentine church of Ognissanti stood at the edge of the textile district along the Arno. For which altar it was painted is not known: the scholarship has proposed a side altar or a position on the screen dividing the lay nave from the religious' choir, and the Uffizi's own materials note that the three-quarter turn of the blessing Child has been read as a clue to a lateral placement, addressing a faithful approaching from one side. These are reconstructions, not records. What the documents do establish is the exit: the Napoleonic suppressions removed the panel from Ognissanti in 1810, and after a century's station in the Accademia it entered the Uffizi in 1919, where it has anchored the museum's first room ever since.

The format is the inherited one — the great gabled Maestà, the Virgin enthroned at colossal scale among her court on gold — and the painting's historical force lies precisely in how much of the inheritance it keeps while changing its meaning. The throne is no longer the inlaid Cosmatesque seat of the San Giorgio alla Costa panel but a complete Gothic tabernacle: a pointed arch on slender piers, crocketed pinnacles, trefoil openings piercing the flanks, polychrome marble revetments, a projecting step veined like the fictive marbles of the Arena Chapel dado. And this architecture is built, not drawn: its sides recede in coherent foreshortening, its interior contains shadow, and through the trefoil openings one sees — a detail of quiet audacity — the half-figures of the court standing behind, cut by the frame of the opening as real bodies are cut by real windows. The throne has become a habitable structure, and the gold ground around it has begun its conversion from surface into light.

The court is organised in depth, and this is the panel's second revolution. The angels and saints do not stack in the vertical tiers of the Duecento Maestà; they stand in ranks, the foremost overlapping those behind, haloes eclipsing haloes, so that the eye reads rows receding beside the throne — a crowd with a floor under its feet. The foreground pair kneel in profile, grey-white against the gold, offering vases from which lilies and roses rise, the Marian flowers of purity and charity; above them two standing angels extend crowns toward the Virgin, the insignia of her queenship held out but not yet conferred, suspending the image between homage and coronation. At the outer edges, prophets and saints — bearded, aged, individualised — close the assembly. Every gaze converges on the centre, and the convergence is itself the composition: the picture depicts attention.

At the centre, the Virgin and Child absorb and justify it. The Virgin is monumental beyond all her companions — the hierarchic scale of the tradition retained, but now read naturally, as nearness — and she sits with the full weight the San Giorgio alla Costa panel first demonstrated: the cushion compresses, the knees spread the fall of the mantle, the torso rises like a column. Over a red dress she wears the deep blue mantle edged in gold, open across the breast upon a white veil through which — another passage of daring — the modelling of the body remains legible. Her face, broad and candid, with the slightly parted lips that criticism has never ceased to remark, looks directly out: the one gaze in the picture that leaves the circuit of the court and meets the worshipper. The Child, in pale rose, sits enthroned on her knee as on a second throne, the scroll of the Word in his left hand, the right raised in the blessing gesture with extended fingers; he is an infant in body — the plump limbs, the small bare feet — and a sovereign in bearing, the double nature stated as a double truth.

The painting's language is that of the Arena Chapel brought to panel. The chiaroscuro is continuous and directional, the draperies fall in long structural folds, the whites and greys of the kneeling angels are orchestrated with the same tonal intelligence as the Paduan garments, and the throne's architecture belongs to the same Gothic vocabulary as the painted frames and coretti of the chapel. On this kinship rests the dating: critical opinion ranges from about 1305 to 1310, with most scholars placing the panel after the Paduan frescoes, and the Uffizi's own dating stands at circa 1310. No document decides the question, and a minority has argued for the years just before Padua; but the predominant reading sees here the master returned to Florence, translating the conquests of the fresco cycle into the most conservative of Florentine formats — which is why the caption gives c. 1305–1310 rather than an earlier bracket.

Within the iconographic tradition, what the panel accomplishes is best measured against its room-mates. Cimabue's Maestà builds its majesty out of the throne's fantastic architecture and the calligraphy of gold-struck drapery; Duccio's out of the melodic line and the tenderness of surface. Giotto's majesty is of another order: it is the majesty of existence. His Virgin is sovereign because she is undeniably there — a body in a structure in a space, attended by other bodies — and the sacred is made to coincide with the real rather than to transcend it. Cennini's formula finds here its cleanest demonstration on panel: the Greek Maestà has been translated into Latin, every element of the type retained and every element re-founded on observation.

In the reconstruction of the early career, the Ognissanti Madonna closes the arc that opened with the Santa Maria Novella Crucifix. There the body on the cross was made true; at San Giorgio alla Costa the enthroned Virgin acquired weight; in the Badia polyptych the altarpiece became architecture; at Rimini the new language was refined toward Padua; and here, after Padua, the oldest and most demanding of Florentine formats receives the completed style. That the one panel on which sceptics and attributionists agree should be the one that gathers all the others' innovations is, for once, a tidy fact in an untidy field: whatever else is disputed about the young Giotto, the painter of the Ognissanti Madonna is the fixed point from which every argument departs.

### Madonna and Child (National Gallery of Art, Washington)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-02.jpg" alt="Panel painting by Giotto on gold ground showing the Virgin in half length within an arched frame, wearing a deep blue mantle edged in gold over a green-lined veil, her head inclined; the Christ Child sits on her left arm in a white cloth, reaching with one hand for the white rose she holds up, while his other hand rests on the white cloth about his hips. Both haloes are tooled in the gold">
  <figcaption>Madonna and Child, c. 1320. Tempera and gold on panel, 85.5 × 62 cm. Washington, National Gallery of Art, Samuel H. Kress Collection. Centre panel of a dispersed polyptych.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Washington *Madonna* is the most serene statement of Giotto’s late maturity on panel: a half-length Virgin and Child built with the same quiet largeness as the Ognissanti altarpiece, but scaled to the centre of a polyptych and warmed into intimacy. The Virgin rises within the arched field like a tower draped in lapis blue, the mantle falling in a few broad, unbroken planes whose weight is the picture’s architecture; the green lining that flashes at the throat and wrist is the single chromatic liberty, and the gold-hemmed border travels the silhouette like a slow line of light. Her head inclines toward the Child by only a few degrees — enough to bend the whole composition toward him without surrendering its frontality — and the face, wide at the brow and firm at the mouth, carries that unsentimental gravity which is Giotto’s signature piety: a mother who is also a throne.

The Child is the panel’s event. He sits high on her left arm, wrapped at the hips in a white cloth, and acts with an observational freshness that had no precedent in the Dugento icons this format descends from: with one hand he reaches for the white rose his mother holds up between thumb and forefinger; with the other he rests his hand on the white cloth about his hips, holding himself steady in the universal posture of infants. The motif of the rose is a small theological machine: the rose — white, the flower of purity, held just beyond reach — carries the symbolic freight of the Virgin’s own title, and the baby’s reaching hand carries the human truth of incarnation; the picture’s meaning sits precisely in the traffic between the two, the sign and the baby given equal visual rights. Where a Byzantine Hodegetria pointed to the Child as to a doctrine, Giotto’s Virgin offers a flower — and the doctrine survives the translation intact.

The panel is the recognised centre of a dispersed polyptych whose wings, by the reconstruction now generally accepted, survive divided between the Horne Museum in Florence, which holds the Saint Stephen, and the Musée Jacquemart-André at the abbey of Chaalis, which holds the Saint John the Evangelist and Saint Lawrence — a reassembly argued from carpentry, tooling and scale, with the identity of the lost remaining saint and the altarpiece’s original destination still open questions; a Florentine church is assumed, and proposals have circulated that touch the Santa Croce orbit.

The attribution of the Washington panel itself stands among the least contested of late Giotto: the critical tradition from the panel’s emergence through its passage in the Kress Collection to the National Gallery has treated it as substantially autograph, the design and the principal heads unthinkable without the master, whatever the shop contributed to the fabric — and the dating in the years around 1320, between the Paduan decade and the late chapels, rests on exactly the qualities described: Ognissanti’s grandeur, relaxed.

The state is notably fine for a Trecento panel: the gold ground and tooled haloes survive with their punchwork legible, the blue has kept its depth, and the faces their modelling; the craquelure is even and honest. The panel’s modern history runs through the Kress acquisition to Washington, where it now represents Giotto for the American public almost alone — one reason this quiet picture bears, lightly, a disproportionate weight: for most visitors who will never stand in Padua, this Virgin with her rose is Giotto, and she does not misrepresent him.

## A Polyptych Dispersed: Washington, Florence, Chaalis

### Saint Stephen (Museo Horne, Florence)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-53.jpg" alt="Panel painting by Giotto on gold ground showing Saint Stephen in half length within an arched field. The young deacon, tonsured, with two pale stones resting on his head, wears a white dalmatic with richly patterned collar and an embroidered geometric panel on the breast; he turns his gaze to the left and holds a red, gold-ornamented book draped with a yellow and green cloth, one hand elegantly spread on its cover. The halo and the arch’s spandrels are tooled with floral ornament in the gold">
  <figcaption>Saint Stephen, c. 1320–1325. Tempera and gold on panel, 84 × 54 cm. Florence, Museo Horne. Lateral panel of the dispersed polyptych reconstructed between Washington, Florence and Chaalis.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Horne *Saint Stephen* is the finest surviving lateral of Giotto’s late panel production, and the one in which the master’s last manner — grave, luminous, touched by the new Gothic refinement — can be studied almost undisturbed. The protomartyr appears in half length under the arched field, a young deacon in the white dalmatic of his order, and the picture’s first statement is the turn: head and gaze swing left, out of the frame, toward the centre of the dispersed altarpiece this panel once flanked — the Virgin and Child now in Washington, treated above — so that the figure, seen alone in the quiet of the Horne Museum, is permanently mid-attention, a saint caught listening to a Madonna who is an ocean away. The face is among the most beautiful of the late shop: broad, firm, modelled in those long transparent passes of the brush that let the green preparation breathe through the flesh, the features drawn with a fineness — the long eyes, the small resolute mouth — in which criticism has long heard Giotto answering the elegance of his Sienese contemporaries without surrendering a gram of his weight.

The iconography is carried, with superb discretion, on the saint’s head: two pale stones rest on the crown, just breaking the silhouette of the tonsure. They are the instruments of the martyrdom — Stephen, deacon of the first church, was stoned outside Jerusalem, the first to die for the new faith, as the Acts of the Apostles relate — and the convention that sets the stones upon the head, as if they had alighted rather than struck, is the Trecento’s gentlest solution to the problem of painting a violent death inside a liturgical portrait: the attribute worn like a diadem, the wound transposed into a crown. Giotto paints them without a drop of blood, matter-of-fact as pebbles on a ledge, and their understatement is the picture’s theology — martyrdom carried lightly, as the liturgy carries it, by a young man otherwise absorbed in attending. The dalmatic beneath is a feast of the workshop’s textile painting: the collar and breast-panel dense with polychrome interlace on gold, the white cloth falling in broad lit planes, and over the forearm the yellow-green drape on which the saint supports his book — scarlet, patterned with gold rosettes, the long fingers of his right hand spread on the cover in one of those hands, articulate as faces, that the late Giotto made a signature.

The panel’s place in the painter’s œuvre is fixed by the reconstruction, now standard, of the dispersed polyptych to which it belonged: the Madonna and Child of the National Gallery in Washington at the centre, this Saint Stephen among the laterals, and the Saint John the Evangelist and Saint Lawrence of the Musée Jacquemart-André at Chaalis completing the known series — a five-part altarpiece, by the usual count, of which one saint remains lost and the original destination undocumented, with Florentine hypotheses in circulation. Within the group the Horne panel has always stood highest in critical esteem: the tradition descending from Herbert Horne himself — the English scholar-collector whose monograph on Botticelli remade that field, and whose bequest of 1916 gave Florence the house-museum in Via de’ Benci — treats the Stephen as substantially autograph, the head above all, and the dating in the first half of the 1320s, between the [Peruzzi Chapel](/Content/Churches/Cappella-Peruzzi.html) and the Baroncelli polyptych, is the prevailing view. That the panel entered the collection on the connoisseur’s eye alone, from the antiquarian market, and has held its attribution for a century, is itself a small monument of the discipline.

The state is exceptional: the gold ground with its tooled spandrels of flowering tendrils, the punched halo, the polychromy of the vestments and the transparent flesh survive with a freshness rare in Trecento panels, the craquelure fine and stable; the cut of the panel at the edges records the dismemberment of the ensemble, and the saint’s companion hand emerging at the lower margin — the left, supporting the book from below — marks where the composition once continued toward the frame. The panel hangs in the Museo Horne as the collection’s declared masterpiece: one saint, one book, two stones — and the whole of late Giotto’s art in the interval between a steady hand and an attentive, elsewhere-turned face.

### Saint John the Evangelist (Musée Jacquemart-André, Chaalis)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-54.jpg" alt="Tall panel painting by Giotto on gold ground showing Saint John the Evangelist in half length under an arched field, with a gabled pinnacle above containing a half-length angel in red with multicoloured wings. The aged apostle, bald-crowned with a full grey beard, wears a blue tunic and rose mantle; he turns his gaze to the left while writing with a pen on a long unfurling scroll inscribed with lines of text, which he steadies with his other hand at its rolled lower end">
  <figcaption>Saint John the Evangelist, c. 1320–1325. Tempera and gold on panel. Chaalis, Abbaye Royale, Musée Jacquemart-André (Institut de France). Lateral panel of the dispersed polyptych reconstructed between Washington, Florence and Chaalis, with its original gable of an angel.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Chaalis Saint John the Evangelist is the dispersed polyptych’s portrait of inspiration at work, and the pairing with its companion panel is the altarpiece’s inner argument in miniature: on one wing the church’s youngest witness, on the other its oldest. John appears as the aged seer of Patmos and Ephesus — the high bald crown, the long grey beard painted strand by strand in that silvery, transparent manner the late workshop perfected — and the picture’s wit is the divided attention: the eyes swing left, toward the central Madonna now in Washington, while the hands go on working, pen descending to a scroll that unfurls down the panel’s whole lower quarter. The Evangelist takes dictation from what he watches; seeing and writing, the two halves of his vocation, are given one body each. The scroll’s lines of lettering — summarily formed, hovering between legibility and pattern — fall in neat ranks across the curling parchment, and the saint’s left hand steadies the rolled end from beneath, a scribe’s grip observed from life. The palette is the ensemble’s noblest chord: blue tunic, rose mantle, gold-hemmed, the complementary pairing Giotto reserved across his œuvre for the beloved disciple, so that the figure reads as kin to the mourning John of the painted Crucifixions treated above — the same man, decades on, grief matured into testimony.

Above, in the gabled pinnacle, survives what the polyptych’s other panels have lost: a half-length angel in red, multicoloured wings spread within the triangular field, product of the same shop and the proof that the altarpiece’s laterals rose to gables crowned with the heavenly court — a datum of carpentry worth more than its small painted surface, since it allows the whole dismembered complex to be reconstructed in elevation and not merely in sequence. The attribution of the Chaalis panels follows the group: design and principal passages assigned to Giotto in his late maturity, the execution shared with the workshop in proportions criticism continues to weigh, with the Evangelist’s head generally counted among the finer autograph passages; the dating in the first half of the 1320s, between the Peruzzi Chapel and the Baroncelli polyptych, holds for the ensemble. The panels’ modern history belongs to the great nineteenth-century collecting of the French Primitifs’ admirers: acquired for the collections that Nélie Jacquemart-André bequeathed to the Institut de France, they hang today in the abbey of Chaalis north of Paris — the polyptych’s two voices in French keeping, answering the centre in Washington and the deacon in Florence.

The state is good: the gold grounds of field and gable survive with their tooling, the figure’s draperies keep their body, and the wear is honest — the panel’s edges and the wood’s exposed margins record the dismemberment, and the craquelure runs fine across the flesh. Seen at Chaalis beside its companion, the panel restores to the scattered altarpiece its deepest symmetry: the Virgin flanked by the writing elder and the deacon with his palm — the word being written on one side and, on the other, already paid for.

### Saint Lawrence (Musée Jacquemart-André, Chaalis)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-55.jpg" alt="Tall panel painting by Giotto on gold ground showing Saint Lawrence in half length under an arched field, with a gabled pinnacle above containing a half-length angel in red with multicoloured wings. The young tonsured deacon faces nearly frontally, wearing a red dalmatic with a richly patterned collar and breast panel and a blue stole crossing his shoulder; he holds a tall green martyr’s palm upright in one hand and a green, gold-ornamented book against his body with the other.">
  <figcaption>Saint Lawrence, c. 1320–1325. Tempera and gold on panel. Chaalis, Abbaye Royale, Musée Jacquemart-André (Institut de France). Lateral panel of the dispersed polyptych reconstructed between Washington, Florence and Chaalis, with its original gable of an angel.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Chaalis Saint Lawrence completes the polyptych’s pair of deacons, and the dialogue with the Horne Saint Stephen — the two protomartyr-deacons of Jerusalem and Rome, the church’s first blood and its most famous — was surely the altarpiece’s designed rhyme: the same dalmatic-clad office on either side of the Virgin, the same youth, the same liturgical composure, distinguished by attribute and by temperature. Where Stephen turns, listening, toward the centre, Lawrence holds nearly frontal, meeting the viewer with the steady, wide-eyed directness of a young cleric at the altar rail; and where Stephen’s dalmatic is white, Lawrence burns in red — the colour of his feast and, the medieval viewer would not have missed it, of his martyrdom by fire on the gridiron, here nowhere shown. For as at the Horne panel the violence is carried by emblem alone: the tall green palm, the martyr’s universal sign, rises in his right hand like a processional stalk, and the green book of his diaconal office — the Gospels he was ordained to carry and the church’s treasury he famously administered — rests against his body, its cover sown with gold rosettes answering the red book of Stephen. Legend made Lawrence the deacon who, ordered by the prefect of Rome to surrender the church’s wealth, presented the city’s poor as its true treasure, and went to the fire for the answer; the panel distils all of it into red cloth, green palm, and a face without a shadow of fear.

The painting of the vestments is the workshop at its most luxurious: the collar and breast panel dense with polychrome interlace on gold, sibling to Stephen’s, the blue stole crossing the shoulder in a single saturated band, the red of the dalmatic laid in broad planes whose folds carry real weight. The face, rounder and more frontal than its companions’, is generally given a larger share to the workshop than the Evangelist’s head — the group’s internal gradations of autography are part of its scholarship — but design, scale and ornament bind it seamlessly to the ensemble; and above, the gabled pinnacle preserves its angel, twin to the Evangelist’s, confirming that the whole altarpiece rose to a crowned elevation. Dating and reconstruction follow the group: the first half of the 1320s, a five-part complex by the usual count — Washington’s Madonna at the centre, Stephen in Florence, the two Chaalis saints, and one lateral lost, its identity and the altarpiece’s original destination the standing open questions, with Florentine hypotheses in circulation.

The state is good, with honest wear: the gold grounds and tooling survive in field and gable, the red keeps its saturation, and the exposed wood at the margins records the dismemberment. At Chaalis the pair hang as the abbey’s quiet treasure — and for the visitor who has stood before the Washington Madonna and climbed the stairs of the Horne, this panel is the last station of one of late Giotto’s most reconstructable altarpieces: four survivors in three countries, one angel-crowned elevation, and a missing fifth saint still waiting for a name.

## Giotto in Berlin: Two Panels in the Gemäldegalerie

### The Entombment of Mary

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-05.jpg" alt="Wide gabled panel by Giotto on gold ground showing the death of the Virgin. Mary lies on an ornamented bier at the centre, surrounded by a dense crowd of mourning apostles, haloed figures and wing-folded angels; at the midpoint Christ stands holding a small swaddled figure, the Virgin’s soul, while an apostle bends to embrace her body and another kneels before the bier. Winged angels crowd the right-hand end of the scene">
  <figcaption>The Entombment of Mary (Dormition of the Virgin), c. 1310. Tempera and gold on poplar panel, 75 × 179 cm. Berlin, Gemäldegalerie, Staatliche Museen zu Berlin. From the church of Ognissanti, Florence.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Berlin *Entombment of Mary* is Giotto’s painted requiem: the Dormition of the Virgin composed as a single slow chord of grief across a gabled field nearly one and eight-tenths metres wide. The subject, Byzantine in origin — the Koimesis, the Virgin’s falling-asleep attended by the apostles miraculously gathered and by Christ receiving her soul — is here translated into the painter’s own language of bodies and weight.

Mary lies on a draped bier whose cosmatesque ornament runs parallel to the frame, the one strict horizontal in a picture otherwise built of inclinations: every figure in the dense double chorus bends, by some degree, toward her, so that the crowd itself performs the mourning as a field of grain performs the wind. At the centre, on the picture’s vertical axis, stands Christ with the Virgin’s soul in his arms — a small swaddled figure, white as the infant she once wrapped — and the panel’s deepest thought is this reversal, as old as the Byzantine formula and never more tenderly stated: the Son holds the mother as the mother held the Son, the Nativity played backward at the threshold of heaven.

Around that axis the painter distributes grief by temperament, in the manner perfected at Padua. One apostle flings himself onto the body, cheek to the Virgin’s face, in the full embrace of loss; another kneels compact at the bier’s front, hands locked; Peter officiates with the book; others press their faces together, support one another, or simply stand and look. The angels — ranked at the right-hand end with folded candy-coloured wings — supply the liturgical frame, grief’s procession ordered into rite; and it is characteristic of Giotto that the most affecting figures are the least demonstrative: the haloed mourners at the far left who do nothing but incline together, attention made visible. The gold ground, which in the frescoes gives way to blue sky, here does its old iconic work — this death happens nowhere and always — while the bodies in front of it have all the gravity of the painted chapels: the picture holds the icon and the event in one hand.

The panel’s provenance is its documentation: it comes from Ognissanti, the church of the Umiliati in Florence for which the great Maestà now in the Uffizi was painted, and the early sources already admired it there — Ghiberti records the Virgin’s death among Giotto’s works in the church, and Vasari transmits the tradition that Michelangelo praised the panel, declaring that the scene could not have been more like the truth.

Its relation to the Ognissanti Madonna, treated above, remains the central question of its scholarship: companion panel, element of a larger ensemble, or independent commission for the same church — reconstructions differ, and the question remains open. The attribution travels the familiar arc of Giotto’s panel production: the design and the principal passages are the master’s, the execution shared with the shop in proportions that connoisseurship continues to negotiate; the dating around 1310, in the Ognissanti years after Padua, is the prevailing view.

The state is good across the figures, with the gold ground showing the wear of its travels — the panel passed through the nineteenth-century art trade into the Berlin collections — and the lower edge abraded. It hangs in the Gemäldegalerie as one of the two or three essential Giottos outside Italy: a metre and three quarters of painted mourning in which, as Michelangelo is said to have seen, nothing is generic — thirty figures, and each grieves alone.

### The Crucifixion

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-06.jpg" alt="Small gabled panel on gold ground showing the Crucifixion. Christ hangs on a tall cross at the centre, pale against the gold, with mourning angels flying around the cross arms, two catching his blood in cups. Below, a dense crowd divides at the cross: at the left the fainting Virgin is supported among the holy women while the kneeling Magdalen in red embraces the foot of the cross; at the right soldiers and onlookers with lances and shields gesture upward, one pointing in recognition.">
  <figcaption>Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Crucifixion of Christ, c. 1315. Tempera and gold on panel, 56.4 × 33.7 cm. Berlin, Gemäldegalerie, Staatliche Museen zu Berlin.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Berlin *Crucifixion* compresses the full public theatre of Golgotha into a panel barely half a metre tall, and the compression is the artistry: this is Giotto’s monumental conception of the scene — the conception of Padua and of the great painted crosses treated above — miniaturised for private devotion without surrendering an ounce of its crowd, its grief or its gold. Christ hangs high on a tall, slender cross that divides the field, the body pale and heavy in the sagging S-curve the painter had made canonical: arms drawn long by real weight, head fallen, the loincloth transparent. Around the cross arms the sky of gold fills with mourning angels — wheeling, tearing their garments, and two of them catching the blood of the wounds in cups, the Eucharistic gloss painted as airborne liturgy — so that the upper half of the panel becomes a single lamentation in flight, rhymed in rose, blue and white against the ground.

Below, the earth is full. The crowd that the Paduan fresco distributes across a wall is here pressed shoulder to shoulder in two choruses that the cross divides and the gestures reunite. At the left, the collapse of the Virgin: she faints backward into the arms of the holy women, her pallor answering her Son’s, while John stands by in the red-and-rose of mourning; at the foot of the cross kneels the Magdalen in her burning red, embracing the wood — the motif of the painter’s Paduan Lamentation and of the Florentine crosses, here given its most compact statement. At the right, the world that watched: soldiers in steel and painted shields, horsemen whose lances rake the gold in a thicket of diagonals, and among them the figures of recognition — one pointing upward, the centurion’s party arriving at its vere Filius Dei — so that the little panel contains, complete, the double audience that is the subject’s deepest structure: those who mourn because they know, and those who are just now finding out.

The panel belongs to the constellation of small-format Crucifixions produced in Giotto’s orbit in the years around and after Padua, and its attribution is correspondingly debated: the Berlin gallery’s tradition has oscillated between the master himself and his closest collaborators, and modern criticism generally sees the design as Giotto’s own — the figure of Christ and the fainting group are inconceivable outside his invention — with execution in which the workshop had a part; its relation to the comparable small panels in other collections, with which reconstructions have sometimes grouped it, remains unsettled. The dating about 1315 places it in the painter’s central maturity, when the shop’s portable production carried the new art to patrons who would never see a chapel: panels like this one are how Giotto travelled in his own lifetime.

The state is good in the figures and the Christ, with the gold ground bearing honourable wear and some restored passages at the edges; the gabled format suggests the panel stood as the wing or centre of a small devotional complex, a tabernacle or diptych, its companions unidentified. It hangs in Berlin a room away from the Entombment of Mary — the pair making the Gemäldegalerie, almost quietly, one of the essential stations of Giotto’s panel art north of the Alps.

## The Signed Altarpieces: Giotto's Name on Panel

Three altarpieces carry Giotto's name, and no other works in his corpus do. The Stigmatization of St Francis in the Louvre is inscribed OPUS IOCTI FLORENTINI, the Baroncelli Polyptych in Santa Croce OPUS MAGISTRI JOCTI, and the Bologna Polyptych in the Pinacoteca Nazionale OPUS MAGISTRI IOCTI DE FLORENTIA. One would expect such inscriptions to settle questions of authorship. In Giotto studies they have done the opposite: much twentieth-century criticism assigned all three to the workshop, producing the paradox that the only works certified as his own were denied to him.

The more balanced view in current scholarship reads the signature as a fourteenth-century workshop guarantee. It meant that the master had supplied the design and answered for the execution, not that every stroke was his hand. The formula "opus magistri" belongs to a world in which a painter's name worked as a warranty, and the nineteenth-century cult of the autograph imposes a notion of solitary authorship that the period did not share. The signature therefore cannot mean nothing, but neither does it promise what modern connoisseurship wanted from it.

The three panels also span the whole arc of the career and carry different evidentiary weight. The Louvre Stigmatization, painted around 1295–1300 for San Francesco in Pisa, reproduces compositions from the Assisi cycle and thus stands as the pivot of the debate over the Upper Church frescoes. The Baroncelli Polyptych, made for the chapel founded in 1328, belongs to the documented late Giotto and to the collaboration with Taddeo Gaddi. The Bologna Polyptych, probably of the early 1330s, records the painter's reputation as an export, his name carried to the legate's city as a brand.

Each panel also has its own history of loss and alteration, to be kept apart from the question of authorship. The Louvre panel was taken from Pisa in 1812 and reached Paris in 1813. The Baroncelli altarpiece was reframed in the later fifteenth century and lost its Gothic gables, one of which, with God the Father, is now in San Diego. The Bologna polyptych, by contrast, preserves its cuspidate carpentry almost complete. Where the literature leaves points unsettled, such as the heraldry on the Pisan frame, the transcription of the Baroncelli inscription, and the identification of the outer predella saints, they are noted as open rather than asserted.

Read together, the three entries trace a single development: the young master's inventions at Assisi and their replication in a signed panel, the mature workshop in Florence, and the old master's name travelling to courts and legations. What links them is the signature itself, which testifies less to a hand than to an institution. By the 1330s Giotto's art had become something that patrons commissioned, imported and displayed under his name, and these altarpieces are that institution's surviving certificates.

### Saint Francis Receiving the Stigmata: The Signed Witness

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-09.jpg" alt="Gabled panel by Giotto: Saint Francis kneels on a rocky mountainside between two small chapels, receiving golden rays from a crucified Christ who appears above within crimson seraph wings; below, a three-part predella shows the Dream of Innocent III, the pope approving the Franciscan Rule, and Francis preaching to the birds">
  <figcaption>Saint Francis of Assisi Receiving the Stigmata, with predella scenes of the Dream of Innocent III, the Approval of the Rule and the Sermon to the Birds, c. 1295–1300, tempera and gold on panel, 313 × 163 cm, inscribed OPUS IOCTI FLORENTINI. Paris, Musée du Louvre, inv. 309, from the church of San Francesco, Pisa.</figcaption>
</figure>

Among the works discussed here, the Louvre Stigmatization occupies a unique evidentiary position: it is signed. Along the lower edge runs the inscription

> OPUS IOCTI FLORENTINI

the work of Giotto the Florentine — and in Giotto studies that signature opened a discussion instead of closing one. Much twentieth-century criticism, measuring the panel against the Paduan touchstone and against its own convictions about Assisi, assigned it to the workshop, the paradox that Luciano Bellosi singled out as among the most disconcerting in the whole literature. On the more balanced current view, the signature guarantees a workshop product under the master's direction and design, not every stroke.

The provenance is Pisan and the exile Napoleonic. The panel stood in the church of San Francesco at Pisa — Vasari, who in his 1568 revision added Pisa to Giotto's itinerary, describes there a Stigmatization by the master — until the French administration removed it in 1812; it reached Paris in 1813 and remained in the Louvre, where it bears the inventory number 309, one of the earliest and greatest Italian "primitives" in the national collection.

The engaged frame carries painted heraldry, the arms of a family of Pisa or resident there, the one trace of the original patronage; their secure identification belongs to the specialist literature. The Louvre's own dating places the panel at the end of the thirteenth century, around 1295–1300 — on the eve of Padua and in the closest possible proximity to the Assisi cycle — and that bracket is adopted here.

The main field stages the culminating miracle of Franciscan hagiography: La Verna, September 1224, the saint in solitary retreat receiving in his flesh the wounds of the Crucified. Giotto builds the mountain as a habitable place — a rising diagonal of pale rock seamed with shadow, planted with the compact, rounded trees that are his botanical signature from Assisi to Padua, and furnished with two small buildings, the chapel of the retreat at the left and a second oratory lower right, their doors open on real interior darkness, their pink and white masonry modelled in light. The hermitage architecture is not scenery; it anchors the event in topography, and its oblique settings rehearse in miniature the spatial grammar of the fresco cycles.

Above, the apparition. Christ crucified appears within six crimson seraph wings — the *Christus-seraph* of Bonaventure's account, in which the saint saw a seraph bearing the image of the Crucified — his arms spread as on an invisible cross, his gaze bent down upon the saint. From his five wounds spring thin golden rays that travel the diagonal of the panel to imprint the corresponding wounds on Francis: and here the painter commits an exactness that is almost notarial, for the rays cross — right hand to left hand, left to right — so that the saint receives the stigmata as a mirror receives an image, each wound answering the wound that faces it. The theology of conformity to Christ, *alter Christus*, is rendered as optical geometry; the miracle is drawn as though it obeyed the laws of projection.

Francis himself kneels in the lower left, turned up toward the vision in a torsion of astonishing structural confidence: the body pivots on the knee, the habit swings with the turn in deep, weighted folds, the hands rise open to receive. The grey-brown sackcloth is modelled with the full tonal gravity of the Santa Maria Novella Christ, and the head — the small tonsured skull, the short beard, the parted lips — holds amazement and submission in a single expression. Nothing levitates, nothing dissolves; the supernatural enters a world of stone, cloth and anatomy, and is believed because the world is.

The predella distributes three further moments of the legend. At the left, the Dream of Innocent III: the pope asleep under his canopied bed sees the Lateran basilica tilting and the little poor man shouldering it upright — the Church saved by the beggar. At the centre, the Approval of the Rule: Francis and his first companions kneel before the enthroned pontiff amid his cardinals, beneath a coffered ceiling rendered in confident recession. At the right, the Sermon to the Birds: the saint bends toward the flock gathered at the foot of a tree, his companion behind him caught between wonder and haste. The three scenes correspond, in subject and in essential composition, to three frescoes of the Legend of St Francis in the Upper Church at Assisi — the sixth, seventh and fifteenth of the cycle — and the main field itself answers the nineteenth, the fresco Stigmatization.

It is this correspondence that makes the panel the pivot of the Assisi question, and the logic is delicate. For the attributionist tradition, a signed panel reproducing Assisi compositions is the cycle's certificate of authorship: the variations between panel and frescoes — and they are real, in the landscape, the architecture, the disposition of figures — show an inventor revising his own inventions, replicas updated at will rather than copies, which was precisely Miklós Boskovits's argument. For the sceptical tradition descending from Richard Offner, the inference runs the other way: a workshop panel translating famous frescoes proves only the frescoes' fame, and the signature certifies the shop, not the designs' paternity. The panel thus cannot settle the controversy, because each side reads it through its prior conclusion; but it constrains every account, since whoever denies the cycle to Giotto must explain why his signed workshop broadcast its compositions under his name within years of their painting.

In the itinerary of the early career, the Louvre panel is the hinge on which the Florentine and Assisiate halves of the early career turn. Its figure style belongs with the Santa Maria Novella Crucifix and the San Giorgio alla Costa Madonna; its narrative and landscape belong with the Upper Church; its signature belongs to Pisa and to the public claim of authorship that a great Franciscan house was willing to display. Painted for the order whose founder it celebrates, exported by an empire, and interrogated for two centuries by a debate it quietly refuses to resolve, the Stigmatization remains what its inscription declares and its critics dispute in the same breath: *opus Iocti* — the work, in whatever sense the fourteenth century intended, of Giotto the Florentine.

### Baroncelli Polyptych: The Coronation and the Celestial Court

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-10.jpg" alt="Five-panel altarpiece by Giotto in a Gothic-style frame: at the centre Christ crowns the kneeling Virgin, both in rose and white, while angels kneel before the throne; the four side panels hold dense ranks of gold-haloed saints and music-making angels; below, a predella with five bust medallions including the dead Christ at the centre">
  <figcaption>Giotto di Bondone (and workshop), Coronation of the Virgin with Angels and Saints (Baroncelli Polyptych), c. 1328–1334, tempera and gold on panel, 185 × 323 cm, inscribed OPUS MAGISTRI JOCTI. Florence, Basilica di Santa Croce, Baroncelli Chapel.</figcaption>
</figure>

With the Baroncelli Polyptych the account crosses from the reconstructed early Giotto into the documented late one, and the evidence changes character accordingly. The altarpiece is signed

> OPUS MAGISTRI JOCTI

the work of Master Giotto, and it stands to this day in the chapel for which it was made, at the end of the right transept of Santa Croce, whose foundation by the Baroncelli family an inscription outside the chapel dates to February 1328. Vasari saw the gold signature and built an argument on it: craftsmen who considered in what age Giotto, without any light of the good manner, began the good manner of drawing and colouring would be compelled, he wrote, to hold him in the highest veneration. Few works by the master are so densely certified — signature, in-situ survival, dated foundation, early testimony — and few have been so energetically taken away from him, a paradox that has to be addressed directly.

The subject is the Coronation of the Virgin, and its choice is itself historically significant. In the central panel Christ, enthroned beneath a draped canopy, sets the crown on the head of his mother, who kneels before him with crossed hands, both figures robed in whites and gold-flowered rose of extraordinary chromatic refinement; four angels kneel at the throne's step, two bearing vessels. The theme — the Virgin received and crowned in heaven — had entered Tuscan painting only recently, and the Baroncelli altarpiece is among its monumental foundations: not the earthly Maestà of the Ognissanti panel but its celestial sequel, the queenship there promised by the crown-bearing angels here conferred. The dynastic resonance for a family chapel dedicated, like the chapel's fresco cycle, to the Virgin's glorification needs no forcing.

Around the central act, the four lateral panels unfold the court of heaven, and it is here that the altarpiece makes its most consequential formal move. Dozens upon dozens of saints and angels — the literature speaks of a crowd approaching two hundred figures across the whole — rise in ranks behind one another, haloes behind haloes in deep recession, exactly as the court of the Ognissanti Madonna first stood in depth; but now the assembly continues across the panel divisions, the crowd conceived as one continuous space that the frame's colonnettes merely interrupt, like mullions before a single view. In the foreground of each panel kneel musical angels with trumpets, vielles, portative organs and psalteries, rendered with an organological precision that has made the polyptych a document for the history of instruments. In this continuity of field across a divided format, criticism has long recognised the conceptual seed of the unified *pala* — the single-space altarpiece that the fifteenth century would make canonical.

The predella answers the glory above with five quatrefoil busts on ornamented grounds: a sainted bishop and the Baptist to the left, Saint Francis and a long-bearded hermit saint to the right, and at the centre — directly beneath the Coronation, on the axis of the altar and its Eucharist — the dead Christ, the Man of Sorrows, pale against gold. The juxtaposition is the altarpiece's theology in miniature: the crowned Mother above, the sacrificed Son below, triumph founded on Passion.

The present appearance of the whole, however, is a palimpsest whose layers have to be separated. The resplendent frame — classical entablature, egg-and-dart, a frieze of gilded cherub heads on blue, candelabra pilasters — is not Trecento work but a Renaissance reframing of the later fifteenth century, in whose course the five panels lost their original Gothic gables and were cut to their present rectangular format. One casualty of that operation has been identified: the crowning panel of God the Father adored by angels, once the central cusp, is today in the San Diego Museum of Art. Whether the signature itself was transcribed onto the new frame from the lost original carpentry or survives in its first material form is a question the technical literature debates, so that the inscription cannot simply be cited as physically autograph; its wording, in any case, is accepted as the authentic Trecento formula.

The attribution history turns on Taddeo Gaddi. Giotto's godson and closest pupil frescoed the walls of this same chapel with the Life of the Virgin — a campaign the documents place in the years around 1328–1338 — and the stylistic proximity of the altarpiece to Taddeo's manner, together with its distance from the Paduan Giotto, led much twentieth-century criticism to make Taddeo the polyptych's principal executant, or to date it deep into the 1330s as a workshop production under the master's name. More recent criticism has moved back toward Giotto: the design and a substantial share of the execution are now widely returned to him, in the moment immediately around the chapel's foundation of February 1328 — that is, on the eve of his departure for Naples, where the Angevin registers record him from 1328. The dating adopted in the caption, c. 1328–1334, spans this debate deliberately: the earlier terminus follows the foundation inscription and the pre-Naples reading, the later one the tradition that extends execution into the years of Taddeo's chapel campaign.

What the dispute itself reveals is the historical truth of Giotto's late enterprise, and the Baroncelli Polyptych is its clearest emblem. The master of the 1320s ran his activity like a building site — Florence, Naples, Bologna, Milan — supplying designs, supervising, painting the decisive passages, and guaranteeing the whole with his name; the question "Giotto or Taddeo?" assumes a notion of solitary authorship that the fourteenth-century workshop did not share, and the signature means precisely what the period meant by it: the master answers for this work. The polyptych is thus neither a diminished Giotto nor a disguised Taddeo but the product their collaboration was designed to produce — and the visible fact that its gentle luminosity, its blond flesh and its Gothic sweetness flow directly into Taddeo's frescoes on the surrounding walls makes the chapel, taken whole, the best surviving portrait of the Giotto shop in operation.

Within the arc of the panels, the Baroncelli altarpiece closes a development that began with five half-lengths on gold in the Badia. There the polyptych became architecture; at Ognissanti the court gained depth; here the court becomes a population and the divided altarpiece a single heaven. The visitor who stands in the chapel today — the signed polyptych on the altar, Taddeo's Annunciation to the Shepherds breaking night with painted light on the wall beside it — stands at the point where Giotto's art, through the pupil it formed and the format it reinvented, passes out of his hands and into the century he made possible.

### Bologna Polyptych: The Signature in the Legate's City

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-11.jpg" alt="Five-panel cuspidate polyptych by Giotto on gold ground: at the centre the Virgin in deep blue sits on a white Gothic throne with the Child, the signature inscribed on the step; at the sides stand Saint Peter with keys, the archangel Gabriel with the Ave Maria scroll, Saint Michael in red wings spearing a dragon, and Saint Paul with sword and book; a predella below holds five roundels around the Man of Sorrows.">
  <figcaption>Giotto di Bondone (and workshop), Madonna and Child Enthroned with Saints Peter, Gabriel, Michael and Paul (Bologna Polyptych), c. 1330–1335, tempera and gold on panel, 91 × 340 cm, inscribed OPUS MAGISTRI IOCTI DE FLORENTIA. Bologna, Pinacoteca Nazionale, from the church of Santa Maria degli Angeli.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Bologna Polyptych completes the trio of works that carry Giotto's name, and it carries it in the most prominent position of the three: not along a frame's edge but on the very step of the Virgin's throne, where the dedicatory zone of an altarpiece traditionally speaks

> OPUS MAGISTRI IOCTI DE FLORENTIA,

the work of Master Giotto of Florence. The declaration of origin is not a formality. In Bologna, in the orbit of the papal legation, "de Florentia" advertised the importation of the most famous painter in Italy, and the signature functions as a brand upon an export. The polyptych comes from the church of Santa Maria degli Angeli, whose construction began in 1328; it passed with the Napoleonic suppressions into the civic collections and belongs today to the Pinacoteca Nazionale, where it stands as the one certain anchor of Giotto's presence in Emilia.

The historical frame of that presence is reconstructed from converging indications rather than from a single document, and the layers must be kept distinct. A source reports that Giotto painted in the Castello di Galliera, the fortress-palace that Cardinal Bertrando del Poggetto, legate of the Avignon popes, began building at Bologna in 1329 and which the Bolognese razed when they expelled him in 1334; a fourteenth-century commentator on Dante preserves the memory of a sharp exchange between the legate and the painter.

The Neapolitan registers, which record Giotto in Robert of Anjou<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-robert-anjou" class="footnote"><sup>25</sup></a>'s service from 1328 to 1333, show a gap between January 1330 and May 1331 in which no document names him — room enough for a Bolognese sojourn. A classic line of reconstruction has accordingly proposed that the polyptych was conceived within the legatine enterprise, perhaps for the Galliera chapel itself, and found its way to Santa Maria degli Angeli after the fortress fell; the church's own foundation in 1328 and the style of the work point in any case to the early 1330s. All of this coheres, and none of it is documented in the strict sense: this is a plausible itinerary, not an archival fact.

The iconography lends the political reading its strongest internal support. The lateral panels do not assemble the usual titular saints of a local church; they build a programme. Peter, at the far left, bears the keys and the triple-barred cross-staff of his Roman primacy; Paul, at the far right, answers him with the sword and the book — the two princes of the Apostles, the double foundation of the papacy, framing the ensemble. Within them stand the two archangels: Gabriel, displaying the scroll of his salutation, whose opening words — Ave gratia plena — remain legible, so that the whole altarpiece becomes an implicit Annunciation with the enthroned Virgin as its addressee; and Michael, red-winged, the orb of dominion in one hand and his lance driven into the dragon coiled at his feet — the Church militant trampling its enemy. For an altarpiece born in the shadow of a legate laboring to restore papal dominion in a rebellious city, the selection reads almost as a manifesto; though here too the interpretation is an inference from the images to their moment.

At the centre, the Virgin and Child restate, at reduced scale and in late accent, the formula perfected at Ognissanti. The white marble throne, Gothic in its pinnacles and crockets, recedes in confident foreshortening above the signed step; the cloth of honour behind glows with patterned gold on red; the Virgin, in the deep blue mantle over red, holds a Child who twists in her arms with startling liveliness — grasping at his mother, one small foot braced, the infant energy of the Badia panel heightened to near restlessness. Her long-eyed, delicately drawn face carries the refined, almost courtly cadence of the master's last decade. In the central gable above, a bust of the Eternal blessing crowns the axis, completing the vertical of Father, Spirit's messenger below in Gabriel's word, and incarnate Son.

The predella condenses the Passion into five roundels set in fields of polychrome ornament: at the centre the Man of Sorrows, the dead Christ upright in his tomb, pale against gold within his red-rimmed clipeus; beside him the mourning Virgin, mantled in blue with her cheek on her hand, and the young John the Evangelist in the same attitude of grief; at the outer ends the Baptist and a youthful female saint generally identified as the Magdalen. As on the Baroncelli predella, the axis is doctrinal — the Virgin enthroned in glory directly above her Son displayed in death, triumph and sacrifice aligned above the altar table where the sacrifice is renewed.

The tituli beneath the main panels — SCS PETRUS, SCS GHABRIEL, SCS MICCHAEL, SCS PAULUS, in gold on red — and the carpentry itself deserve a word, for the Bologna polyptych is among the best-preserved Giottesque altarpiece structures in existence: the five cuspidate panels keep their crocketed gables, their pierced and gilded mouldings, their inscribed plinth zone, giving a rare image of what the Badia polyptych looked like before its losses and the Baroncelli before its reframing. How much of the present framing is Trecento work and how much later consolidation is, as always, a question for the technical literature, and so is the history of the work's modern restorations; but the ensemble's completeness is itself exceptional.

The attribution history replays, in compressed form, the drama of the signatures. For much of the twentieth century the polyptych was handed to the workshop — the execution judged too summary, the types too mannered for the master of Padua — and it stood beside the Louvre and Baroncelli panels in that paradoxical class of works too signed to be Giotto's. More recent criticism has reversed the judgment, returning the design and a substantial part of the execution to Giotto himself, with workshop collaboration of the kind his late enterprise everywhere presupposed. The reversal rests partly on cleaning and closer looking, partly on the historiographical shift described above: the recognition that the late Giotto is not a diminished Paduan but a different artist — swifter, more linear, more Gothic in cadence — and that a fourteenth-century signature guarantees the shop's product under the master's responsibility, not the solitude of his hand.

Within the geography of the career, Bologna completes a triangle with Naples and Milan: the decade in which Giotto's art became a commodity that courts and powers imported, his name travelling as warranty. Of the Neapolitan cycles fragments remain; of Milan nothing; Bologna alone preserves an intact, signed, exportable Giotto of the 1330s — which gives this modest-sized polyptych an evidentiary weight comparable to the Rimini cross for the opposite end of the career. There the young master seeded a school; here the old master stamps a product; and between the two lies the whole transformation of the painter into an institution.

## Assisi Again, Rome, and Padua: The Magdalen Chapel, the Stefaneschi Altarpiece, and the Arena Crucifix

The first document naming Giotto dates from January 1309, and it takes him back to Assisi. In that month Palmerino di Guido, a painter documented at Assisi in the deed of 4 January 1309 (acting for himself and for Giotto), and, under the same name, in the records of Gubbio, repaid a loan of fifty pounds of Cortonese money<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-gold-florin" class="footnote"><sup>26</sup></a> to a Jolo Juntarelli

> pro se et Iocto Bondoni de Florentia.

The document says nothing about paintings. Much recent criticism nonetheless connects it with the decoration of the Magdalen Chapel in the Lower Church, commissioned, to judge from the painted arms, by Teobaldo Pontano, bishop of Assisi. Its Raising of Lazarus and Noli me tangere are closely related to the Paduan versions. Views on the chapel have moved from "school of Giotto" towards substantial autograph participation. In the attributionist reading the Paduan comparisons show original development, not workshop repetition: fewer figures, greater attention to setting, and sharper psychological characterisation. On the same argument, an assistant called the "Expressionist Master", identified with Palmerino himself, had a limited and clearly defined role.

### The Raising of Lazarus: Padua Rethought at Assisi

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-12.jpg" alt="Fresco in the Magdalen Chapel at Assisi: Christ, at the left with his apostles, stretches his arm toward Lazarus, who stands bound in white grave-wrappings at the door of a rock tomb, supported by onlookers who shield their faces; a kneeling woman adores at Christ's feet while two labourers remove the stone slab, all beneath a rocky landscape and dark sky">
  <figcaption>Giotto di Bondone and workshop, The Raising of Lazarus, c. 1307–1309, fresco. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Lower Church, Magdalen Chapel, left wall, middle register.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Raising of Lazarus occupies the middle register of the left wall of the Magdalen Chapel, the first chapel on the right of the nave of the Lower Church, and it belongs to the one Assisi campaign that can be brought near a document bearing Giotto's name.

A word on the date, since the older literature — and the caption tradition of several image repositories — still carries "1320s". That dating descends from the period when the chapel was held to be a late workshop production; the current consensus has moved it back to the years immediately after Padua, around 1307–1309, in the orbit of the Palmerino document, and the broader cataloguing brackets (the Fototeca Zeri gives 1305–1320) accommodate rather than contradict that view. External events also press against a late dating: the Ghibelline<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-ghibellines" class="footnote"><sup>27</sup></a> uprising at Assisi of September 1319 and the plundering of the basilica's treasury make a major decorative campaign in the following years difficult to credit. The caption therefore gives c. 1307–1309 — a critical judgment, supported by style and circumstance, not a documented fact.

The composition itself is the strongest argument for the developmental reading, because it rethinks rather than repeats the Paduan Lazarus. Christ enters from the left at the head of his apostles — the young John beside him, Peter's grizzled profile behind — and the entire narrative is discharged through his extended right arm, the straight line of command that crosses the painted field toward the tomb. At his feet kneels the adoring sister: in this chapel she is no mere supporting figure, for the Magdalen — whom medieval tradition identified with Mary of Bethany — is the titular of the cycle, and the scene is told as an episode of her story as much as of her brother's. Beside Christ’s hand, red letters on the rock preserve his command in an order inverted from the Vulgate’s “Lazare, veni foras”: the inscription reads

> FORAS VENI LAZARE,

the final word has also been transcribed as LAZARUS, but the vocative ending visible on the wall supports the former reading.

The right half belongs to the miracle's human weather. Lazarus stands at the door of his rock-cut tomb, bound head to foot in white grave-wrappings, his bandaged face just emerging — not yet risen so much as returned, propped by the onlookers who crowd him. And around him Giotto distributes the Gospel's own realism: the man in the golden mantle who draws the cloth across his nose against the stench of the fourth day, the grey-bearded elder who throws up both hands in astonishment, the practical labourers — one in white bending to his work, a dark-skinned youth crouching at the grave's mouth, a third walking the heavy slab away on the diagonal. This diagonal of the slab is a compositional invention of quiet brilliance: it opens the foreground, gives the miracle a stage, and introduces into the sacred scene the measured prose of physical labour.

Set beside the Paduan version of a few years earlier, the differences are systematic and all point one way. The Arena fresco compresses the event against its blue ground; here the figures are fewer in proportion to the field, the rocky landscape rises high behind them under a darkened sky, trees punctuate the ridgelines, and the tomb is no sarcophagus but a doorway cut into the living rock — a setting observed, not notated.

The psychological characterisation sharpens correspondingly: each witness carries a distinct quantity of belief, doubt, disgust or wonder, and the drama is distributed across the crowd rather than concentrated in a single confrontation. For the attributionist tradition this is precisely what distinguishes an inventor revising his invention from a workshop copying it — the same argument that the Louvre Stigmatization sustains for the Upper Church — while the sceptical tradition reads the chapel as the most gifted of the Paduan reflections. Both positions remain in play; the quality of passages such as the Lazarus group makes the first the more economical.

The scene's condition and frame deserve note. The fresco has suffered the abrasions and losses common to the Lower Church — the sky's azurite darkened and fallen in places, the surface worn — but the essential fabric survived the 1997 earthquake, whose devastation fell on the vaults of the Upper Church, and the basilica-wide campaigns that followed have stabilised the chapel; the specific restoration history of these walls belongs to the technical literature. At the right edge, the painted Cosmatesque border meets the chapel's actual rib, a reminder that the scene belongs to a total decorative system — marbled dadoes, ornamental bands, stained glass — conceived as one Gothic interior.

The Lazarus stands on the most defensible ground of the Assisi attributions. Here, uniquely, the elements that elsewhere must be argued separately converge: a document naming Giotto at Assisi, a patron identified by his arms, a style in evident dialogue with securely documented Padua, and a quality of invention that resists delegation. Whoever wishes to deny the master any brush at Assisi must begin by explaining this wall; whoever grants it has already conceded that the road between Padua and the basilica ran in both directions.

### Noli me tangere: The Gardener of the Resurrection

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-13.jpg" alt="Fresco in the Magdalen Chapel at Assisi: Mary Magdalene in a red mantle kneels on rocky ground, reaching toward the risen Christ, who stands at the right in white robes within a radiance of gold rays, holding a long-handled implement; at the left two white-robed angels sit on the open pink marble sarcophagus, two more angels fly in the dark sky above a sparse hillside of trees.">
  <figcaption>Giotto di Bondone and workshop, Noli me tangere, c. 1307–1309, fresco. Assisi, Basilica of San Francesco, Lower Church, Magdalen Chapel, lunette of the left wall, above the Raising of Lazarus.</figcaption>
</figure>
