The Noli me tangere fills the lunette of the left wall of the Magdalen Chapel, directly above the Raising of Lazarus, and the superposition is the cycle's own theology: below, the Magdalen's brother called back to mortal life; above, the risen Lord who will die no more, met in the garden by the same woman. The two frescoes share wall, workshop and the critical history set out for the Lazarus — the Palmerino di Guido document of January 1309, the patronage of Bishop Teobaldo Pontano legible in the painted arms, the dating around 1307–1309 that current criticism prefers to the old "1320s", and the long journey of the attribution from "school of Giotto" to substantial autograph participation with a circumscribed role for assistants. What this scene adds to that shared dossier is a series of inventions that belong to no routine hand.

The composition takes its canon from Padua and then quietly dismantles it. In the Arena Chapel the episode shares its field with the Resurrection: sleeping soldiers fill the foreground, and the Magdalen's encounter is one half of a double scene. At Assisi the soldiers are gone. The lunette belongs entirely to the Magdalen — as befits the titular of the chapel — and the drama contracts to the essential triangle of Gospel text: the empty tomb with its angelic witnesses, the kneeling woman, the risen Christ who withdraws even as he reveals himself. The pink-veined marble sarcophagus, set obliquely against the left edge with its lid removed, anchors the composition exactly as the slab on the diagonal anchors the Lazarus below; the two scenes answer one another across the register line, closed tomb opened and opened tomb emptied.

The angels are the lunette's doctrinal machinery, and Giotto doubles them. Two sit upon the sarcophagus in white, one frontal with folded wings, the other turning outward with wings spread and arm extended toward the Magdalen — the pair of John 20:12, "two angels in white, sitting, one at the head and one at the feet, where the body of Jesus had been laid", a textual fidelity that sets this image apart within the iconographic tradition, where a single angel is far more common. Above them, two further angels fly against the night sky, swooping together in a knot of white and green drapery. And here the fresco holds its most extraordinary secret, recovered to scholarly attention by a recent study: the faces of the angels at the tomb are gilded and modelled in actual relief, projecting physically from the wall — a solution without parallel in fresco technique, and best read as a literal rendering of Matthew's description of the angel of the tomb, "erat autem aspectus eius sicut fulgur", his countenance like lightning. The worshipper in the chapel met not a painted radiance but a real one, gold catching the candlelight on raised flesh.

Christ stands at the right in the white garments of the risen, and his whole figure is an essay in presence withheld. He turns back toward the Magdalen even as his weight moves away — the contrapposto of refusal that Padua perfected — his right hand lowered toward her in the gesture that at once blesses and forbids. Around him the painter has laid a radiance of gold rays, streaming from the body itself, and finer rays spring from the wounds of hands and feet: the glorified body announced by light, a solution more explicit than Padua's and calibrated, like the relief angels, to the dim luminosity of the Lower Church. In his left hand he holds a long-handled implement, the hoe of the gardener for whom the Magdalen mistook him — "supposing him to be the gardener", John 20:15 — so that the image contains both moments of the recognition at once: the error and its correction, the gardener and the God.

The Magdalen herself kneels at the centre in the red mantle that is her signature throughout the chapel, caught in the instant between lunge and arrest. Her body presses forward on its knees, both arms stretched toward Christ with open hands, the fingers nearly touching his; her profile, lifted and candid under the gathered blond hair, carries longing without contortion. It is the chapel's most concentrated demonstration of what has been called Giotto's narrative economy: the whole Gospel exchange — *Maria. Rabboni. Noli me tangere* — legible in the metre of space between two hands.

The setting, finally, may be the lunette's boldest stroke. The hillside rises in pale, faceted rock behind the figures, seamed with shrubs and small flowering bushes — the "garden" of the Gospel reduced to Mediterranean scrub — and against the deep sky stand trees of two kinds: compact leafy crowns on the ridge, and, lower, a scatter of bare, leafless trees drawn branch by branch against the darkness. Criticism has long remarked this passage, for the naked trees at dawn on the day of Resurrection carry the season of the Passion into the landscape itself — early spring, the world not yet releafed — and painted winter trees are a rarity in Trecento art that the camera of every visitor to the chapel still singles out. The azurite sky has darkened and suffered losses, as throughout the Lower Church, but the nocturnal-to-dawn tonality is original in conception: this is the garden "cum adhuc tenebrae essent", while it was yet dark.

Set beside its Paduan sibling, the Assisi Noli me tangere thus argues the same case as the Lazarus below, and more intimately. Every departure from the Arena version — the suppression of the guards, the doubled angels, the relief-and-gold faces, the irradiated Christ, the wintry garden — is a rethinking in the direction of the chapel's particular devotion and the crypt-church's particular light. These are the decisions of a mind, not the economies of a copyist; and whether one assigns the brushwork to the master, to the gifted assistant the literature has long detected in the chapel, or to their collaboration, the inventing intelligence is hard to name otherwise than as Giotto. With this lunette and the Lazarus, the Magdalen Chapel pair is complete: the two scenes in which the chapel's saint meets, across death, the two bodies of her Lord.

The right transept of the Lower Church, with the Infancy of Christ and posthumous miracles of Francis, and the four Franciscan Allegories in the vault over the high altar, pose a different problem. Vasari assigned the Allegories to Giotto and a sixteenth-century Assisi friar did the same for the Infancy scenes. From the early twentieth century, modern criticism almost unanimously rejected their autograph status in favour of pupils, grouped under conventional names such as the "Maestro delle Vele".

A hypothesis from the late 1960s grouped much of this production, together with the Stefaneschi altarpiece and other panels, under an assistant called the "Parente di Giotto", a relative of the master employed in the workshop. Some later writers turned this figure into a true alter ego. The Dizionario Biografico takes the opposite view: there is no sharp boundary between the "Parente" and the "Maestro delle Vele" groups, and the cycle, one of the great works of Trecento painting, should be returned in substance to Giotto himself, in a new phase closer than ever to the elegance and colour of Western Gothic.

The chronology is bracketed in an instructive way. Giotto is documented in Florence up to September 1314. The Ghibelline uprising at Assisi led by Muzio di Francesco in September 1319, and the plundering of the basilica's treasury, make a large decorative campaign after that date unlikely. [Simone Martini](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Simone-Martini.html)'s St Louis of Toulouse<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-louis-toulouse" class="footnote"><sup>28</sup></a> beneath one of the posthumous miracles implies that the Giottesque campaign was over by 1317, the year of Louis's canonisation. These are good arguments. They are inferences, not documents.

### Stefaneschi Triptych: The Double Altarpiece of Old St Peter's

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-14.jpg" alt="Three-gabled side of Giotto's double-faced Stefaneschi Triptych: at the centre Christ enthroned in blue blesses amid angels, with the small kneeling Cardinal Stefaneschi at the throne's step; at the left Peter is crucified upside down between a pyramid and a white conical monument; at the right Paul is beheaded while Plautilla's veil descends from the sky; below, a predella with the enthroned Madonna, two censing angels and the twelve apostles">
  <figcaption>Giotto di Bondone and assistants, Stefaneschi Triptych, recto: Christ Enthroned with Angels and Cardinal Jacopo Stefaneschi, between the Crucifixion of St Peter and the Beheading of St Paul; predella with the Madonna and Child Enthroned between Two Angels and the Twelve Apostles, c. 1315–1320, tempera and gold on poplar panel, central panel 178 × 89 cm (whole triptych c. 220 × 245 cm), modern gilded frames. Vatican City, Pinacoteca Vaticana, inv. MV.40120.10, from the Basilica of Old St Peter's.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Stefaneschi altarpiece in the Pinacoteca Vaticana is the other major Roman commission documented through the cardinal's necrology in the *Liber benefactorum* of St Peter's, which records the panel as "de manu Jocti" — by Giotto's hand — at a cost of eight hundred gold florins, just as it credits the Navicella to "the hands of the same most singular painter". The record is a posthumous compilation, drawn up decades after the commission, which should temper how much it is asked to prove: it attests what the chapter of St Peter's held about the work and had paid for it, not the distribution of labour on the scaffold. In the early seventeenth century the Vatican archivist Giacomo Grimaldi, describing the old basilica before its demolition, recorded the altarpiece as painted "circa annum MCCCXX".

The date is disputed around that anchor. It fits the style, which links back to the Lower Church transept and forward to Naples and the Bardi Chapel, and the Vatican Museums themselves catalogue the work as by "Giotto di Bondone and assistants", between 1315 and 1320.
The triptych's defining fact is that it has two faces, because it had two congregations. Painted on both sides, it stood free upon an altar of Old St Peter's — the long debate between the high altar and the canons' altar in the nave remains open — so that celebrant and faithful each received a complete programme. The side presented here, inventoried by the Pinacoteca as the recto, carries Christ in glory between the martyrdoms of the apostles; the other face answers with St Peter enthroned, flanked by paired standing saints, receiving from the kneeling Stefaneschi the famous model of the triptych itself — a painting of the painting, which preserves the lost original carpentry with its gables and pinnacles — while St George presents the donor and Pope Celestine I<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-pope-celestine-i" class="footnote"><sup>29</sup></a> attends.

The museum's own materials waver over which face is "front": the Italian notice turns the Peter side toward the faithful and the Christ side toward the priest, the English notice states the reverse, and the scholarly literature has argued both arrangements from the liturgical geography of the old basilica. The inventory's neutral "recto" is kept here, and the orientation is left to that debate.

The central panel enthrones Christ as the judge and blesser of the apostolic city. He sits in a deep blue robe thickly chased with gold within a Gothic throne of extreme slenderness — twisted colonnettes, a crocketed gable, a traceried rose — set against a cloth of honour whose dense gold patterning recalls the Bologna polyptych's; a court of angels banks steeply behind and beside the throne in the ranked recession followed since Ognissanti. His right hand blesses; the left holds the jewelled book. And at the throne's step, minute against the marble, kneels the cardinal himself in profile, hands joined — the second of his two appearances on the triptych, the donor admitted into the immediate presence not of his patron saint but of Christ. The precise identification of this kneeling figure as Stefaneschi follows the museum and the standard literature.

The left panel stages the Crucifixion of Peter with a topographical exactness that is the panel's celebrated feature. Peter hangs head downward on his cross — the inverted martyrdom he requested — between two monuments rendered with antiquarian care: at the left a brick pyramid, at the right a white conical structure ringed with marble courses. These are the *meta Romuli* and the pyramid — the two funerary landmarks of the Vatican quarter between which medieval tradition, following the apocryphal acts, located the apostle's crucifixion "inter duas metas". Giotto, or his designer, has painted the martyrdom not in a generic Jerusalem but in Rome itself, on the very ground where the basilica housing the altarpiece stood; mounted officials and mourning onlookers crowd the foreground, and angels dive in grief about the cross. For the canons of St Peter's, the panel was a picture of their own foundation.

The right panel answers with the Beheading of Paul. The apostle kneels beheaded at the centre of a rocky landscape, the executioner sheathing his stroke, soldiers and horsemen massed at either side with banners; on the hill above, a tower marks the site, and from the sky an angel-borne figure drops a white cloth toward a mourner on the ridge — the veil of Plautilla, which in the apocryphal tradition the matron lent Paul for his blindfold and received back from the martyr after death, returned from heaven. The inclusion of this tender apocryphal coda, rare at such scale, turns the panel of judicial violence toward consolation, and the diagonal of the descending veil binds sky to earth exactly as the golden rays bind Christ to Francis on the Louvre panel.

The predella beneath completes the ecclesiology. At its centre the Virgin sits enthroned with the Child between two censing angels and two apostles; five further apostles stand in each lateral compartment, so that the full college of twelve attends her across the three fields — the Church assembled beneath its enthroned Lord. Of the opposite face's predella a single compartment survives, with three standing saints; the rest is lost, one of the few mutilations in an ensemble otherwise exceptionally complete, down to painted framing elements, while the present gilded frames are restoration work.

The attribution history is the familiar late-Giotto drama in its Roman act. The necrology's "de mano Iotti" notwithstanding, much twentieth-century criticism — reluctant to reconcile the triptych's Gothic refinement with Padua — assigned the execution to the workshop, and the hypothesis of the "Parente di Giotto", the master's hypothetical kinsman-assistant, was built in large part around this very work. Recent decades have moved the pendulum back: the design is universally Giotto's, substantial autograph intervention is widely admitted — it is hard to imagine the master delegating wholesale a commission for the altar of Christendom's first basilica, or the cardinal who paid eight hundred florins failing to notice — and the Vatican's own formula, Giotto and assistants, states the sane middle position. The work's profile has risen accordingly: in 2015 it left the Vatican for the first time, for the Milan exhibition *Giotto, l'Italia*, and the campaign of technical study surrounding that loan issued in 2016 in the volume *Ricerche sul polittico Stefaneschi* (Edizioni Musei Vaticani–Electa), now the point of departure for all material questions about the triptych.

The Stefaneschi Triptych is the Roman keystone. It joins the Navicella's patron to a surviving object; it carries the court style of the 1310s between the Lower Church campaigns and the signed altarpieces of the 1330s; and in its two faces, its painted model of itself, and its martyrdoms staged on Roman ground, it is the most self-aware altarpiece of the early Trecento — a work that depicts its own donor, its own body, and its own city, under the enthroned Christ to whom all three are offered.

### Crucifix of the Scrovegni Chapel: The Cross of the Arena

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-15.jpg" alt="Double-sided painted cross by Giotto from the Scrovegni Chapel: the pale dead Christ hangs against a blue field framed by gold damask panels within an elaborately carved and gilded mixtilinear frame; the blessing Redeemer occupies the polylobed summit above a red titulus, the praying Virgin and mourning Saint John the quatrefoil terminals, and the rock of Golgotha with Adam's skull the triangular foot">
  <figcaption>Crucifix, c. 1304, tempera and gold on poplar panel, 223 × 164 cm. Padua, Musei Civici agli Eremitani, from the Scrovegni Chapel.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Paduan Crucifix closes this corpus where the documented Giotto is most at home: inside the Arena Chapel's own furnishings. The cross belongs to the decoration of Enrico Scrovegni's chapel, whose chronology is the firmest in the whole career — land purchased in February 1300, the indulgence of March 1304 proving the building usable, the Venetian loan of hangings for a consecration in March 1305 — and it was made to stand within that ensemble, painted on both faces so that it could be read from either side; the older literature connects it with the screen dividing lay space from chancel, and its double-sidedness, like the Stefaneschi Triptych's, encodes its two congregations. Riccobaldo Ferrarese, whose memories of Padua date to 1306–1308, already names the Arena among the proofs of Giotto's excellence. Today the cross hangs a few steps from its birthplace, in the Musei Civici agli Eremitani, the museum complex of which the chapel itself forms part.

The dating travels with the chapel, and the caption's c. 1304 states the majority view: a furnishing conceived with the fresco campaign of c. 1303–1305, in the same breath as the painted Crucifixion of the north wall and the God the Father panel set into the chancel arch — the chapel's other work of painting on panel, and the proof that Giotto's Paduan enterprise included the joiner's art from the start. A strand of criticism has preferred a somewhat later execution, within the second decade, and the extent of the master's own hand against the workshop's has been debated here as everywhere in the mature œuvre; the restoration and technical study of recent decades have strengthened the case for Giotto's governing design and substantial intervention, and the question of the hands, as throughout, belongs finally to the specialist literature. What no one disputes is that the cross is the chapel's: its palette is the chapel's palette, and its Christ is the chancel-arch generation of the painted figures made object.

The carpentry alone would date it. No cross in the corpus — not Santa Maria Novella, not Rimini — carries framing of this exuberance: a continuous carved cresting of gilded acanthus leaves breaks into flame around the entire silhouette, the terminals swell into great quatrefoils, the summit into a polylobed medallion, and the field steps and re-steps in mouldings of gold. The Rimini cross had begun to profile the panel around the body; Padua completes the process and then enriches it, turning the cross into a piece of Gothic micro-architecture, a reliquary at monumental scale. How much of this carved apparatus is original Trecento work — it is generally so presented, and its survival would be a rarity of the first order — is a question for the technical literature.

Within that glittering armature, the painted scheme is of studied sobriety. The field behind the body is deep blue, bordered by panels of gold textile patterning — the apron reduced to slender lateral bands, since the profiled format leaves little field at all — and the four figural stations distribute the familiar company. In the summit medallion the Redeemer blesses, half-length in blue and red with the jewelled book: the cimasa type that Santa Maria Novella has lost and Rimini preserves only in exile, here intact in place. Below him the red tablet of the titulus carries three lines of gold lettering — the Latin inscription. In the left quatrefoil the Virgin mourns with hands joined in prayer, a gesture more liturgical, more interceding, than the Florentine cross's gathered mantle; in the right, John inclines in the ancient attitude of sorrow. At the foot, the triangular suppedaneum opens the rock of Golgotha, and in its fissure lies the skull of Adam, the thread of blood from the feet descending to find it — the programme of Santa Maria Novella restated more than a decade on, in miniature and in jewelled tones.

The Christ gathers the whole development of the crosses. From Florence he keeps the truthful suspension — the fallen head, the dragged shoulders, the weight — and from Rimini the slender luminosity, the long limbs, the continence of blood; and he adds the specifically Paduan qualities, the ivory flesh modelled in long soft transitions, the transparent loincloth knotted low on the hip and painted with the same intelligence of veiled form as the Louvre panel's gauze, the face emptied into the quiet of the chapel's own dead Christ of the Lamentation. Set beside the Santa Maria Novella Christ, he measures exactly what more than a decade had changed: the demonstration has become a style, the argument a language. The faithful of the Arena, turning from the frescoed Crucifixion of the wall to the painted cross among the furnishings, met the same figure twice — once in the story, once in the cult image the story authorises.

As the corpus's final station, the Paduan cross performs a quiet summary. It is the one crucifix in the group that stands inside a documented ensemble, datable by the building that housed it and witnessed by a chronicler within five years; and it shows the type that Santa Maria Novella revolutionised and Rimini refined now fully domesticated — no longer the manifesto of a new truth but the serene possession of it. The young painter nailed a real body to the cross of the Dominicans; the master of the Arena hangs the same body in gold and azure, inside the most complete pictorial world the fourteenth century ever built around a single patron's conscience. Between those two crosses runs the whole arc of the crosses.

## The Peruzzi Chapel: A Masterpiece Painted on Dry Ground

The Peruzzi Chapel, dedicated to the two Saints John, has three scenes of the Baptist on the left wall and three of the Evangelist on the right. Its date is inferred rather than recorded, and the inference rests on a documentary chain that begins remarkably early. In his will of 1292 — the oldest surviving document relating to any extant family chapel in Florence — the banker Donato di Arnaldo Peruzzi left the friars two hundred lire for a chapel in the new church of Santa Croce, then still to be built, stipulating that it be constructed within ten years of his death; he is last recorded alive in 1299. The chapel's fabric thus belongs to the opening years of the fourteenth century, and close examination has shown that the putlog holes used by the masons were reused for the painters' scaffolding, which implies that the decoration followed construction without a break. The murals themselves were probably commissioned by a later generation of the family — Donato's grandson Giovanni di Rinieri Peruzzi has been proposed — and recent opinion generally places the cycle at the beginning of the second decade of the fourteenth century, around 1310–1315; according to others, c. 1318–1322.

Technique is what defines the chapel. It is painted in a mixed technique, largely *a secco*, on dry plaster with organic binders — egg among them — rather than in true fresco alone: the only known murals by Giotto executed this way, and the reason so much has been lost and the condition remains so precarious. According to the Opera di Santa Croce, a mid-nineteenth-century restoration conducted in the spirit of the Risorgimento, which revered Giotto as the founding father of Italian art, used mechanical cleaning and chemical solvents that caused irreparable losses, and further unsuccessful interventions followed. Between 1958 and 1961 all the additions were removed. The Opificio delle Pietre Dure restored the murals between 1983 and 1985 and carried out a major diagnostic campaign in 2009–2010, supported by the Getty Foundation and by Villa I Tatti, The Harvard Center for Italian Renaissance Studies. That campaign photographed the walls under ultraviolet light: because the organic binders of the secco layers fluoresce, it recovered to the camera the modelling, expressions and details of figures no longer visible in ordinary light — a hidden Giotto beneath the ruin, which the project later presented to the public in Milan alongside the exhibition dedicated to the master.

Despite the damage, the scale of the compositions remains fully legible. Each scene occupies a field of roughly two and a half by four metres, and the perspective of the architectural settings is organised around a viewing point at the chapel's entrance arch, so that the narrow space's visitors, who see the walls obliquely, read the buildings correctly — structures of such observed solidity that they have the air of portraits of real ones. Gestures such as the Evangelist's outstretched arm calling Drusiana back to life were copied by later artists, a tradition that runs from Masaccio to Michelangelo, whose pen studies after these walls survive. The altarpiece was probably the polyptych with the half-length Christ between the Virgin, the two Saints John and Saint Francis now in the North Carolina Museum of Art in Raleigh: the selection of saints matches the chapel's dedication within its Franciscan church, and the panel's dimensions accord with the chapel's altar.

### The Baptist Wall of the Peruzzi Chapel: Three Scenes from the Life of Saint John the Baptist

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-16.jpg" alt="Lunette fresco in the Peruzzi Chapel: within a Gothic marble baldachin, the aged priest Zacharias swings a censer at the draped altar of incense while the angel appears beside it; musicians and bystanders attend at the left, and at the right the waiting congregation stands beneath the loggia of the temple precinct; a grey-blue loss interrupts the lower right of the scene">
  <figcaption>The Annunciation to Zacharias, c. 1310–1315, fresco and a secco, lunette of the left wall. Florence, Basilica di Santa Croce, Peruzzi Chapel.</figcaption>
</figure>

The cycle of the Baptist opens in the lunette with the episode that Luke sets inside the sanctuary: the aged priest Zacharias, serving his course at the altar of incense, receives from the angel the announcement of a son to be called John, doubts, and is struck dumb until the child's naming. Giotto builds the scene around the architecture of the rite. At the centre rises the baldachin of the altar — a Gothic tabernacle of pale marble on slender columns, its gable pierced by a rose, its flanks by lancets, an ideal Trecento reliquary enlarged to the scale of the Temple — and beneath it Zacharias stands at the white-draped mensa, the censer swinging from his hand, caught in the very act the angel interrupts. The messenger appears at the altar's far side, so that the supernatural enters the composition exactly where the liturgy locates the divine presence: across the altar, as across a threshold.

The flanking groups translate Luke's stage directions into standing figures. At the left, within the sanctuary, attendants and Levites assist — among them a figure with a stringed instrument, the musical service of the Temple made visible — their heavy mantles falling in the long columnar folds of the mature style. At the right, beyond the sanctuary's pier, the scene opens into the arcaded precinct where two veiled women and their companions wait: the "omnis multitudo populi orans foris", the whole multitude praying outside at the hour of incense, whom Zacharias will shortly face unable to speak. The division of the field — rite within, people without, the angel between — is the Gospel's own structure given architectural form, and the oblique setting of the buildings, angled toward the chapel's entrance, belongs to the optical intelligence that governs the whole wall: these scenes were designed to be read from the corner of the eye, by a viewer standing in the narrow chapel's mouth.

The condition declares the chapel's material history at first glance. A grey lacuna breaks the lower right of the lunette, where plaster and paint have fallen, and the surface throughout has the bleached, grave-toned transparency of the Peruzzi murals — the consequence of the largely *a secco* execution described above, which made this cycle at once the most refined and the most fragile of Giotto’s Florentine works. The wall now shows what survives and nothing more; the ultraviolet campaign of 2009–2010 recovered modelling and features the eye no longer sees — the "Giotto segreto" beneath the ruin.

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-17.jpg" alt="Fresco in the Peruzzi Chapel showing two rooms of one house: at the left the mute Zacharias, seated, writes the name of his son while women present the infant John to him; at the right Elizabeth rests in her curtained bed attended by three women; the architecture opens in section beneath a projecting upper storey">
  <figcaption>The Birth and Naming of Saint John the Baptist, c. 1310–1315, fresco and a secco, middle register of the left wall. Florence, Basilica di Santa Croce, Peruzzi Chapel.</figcaption>
</figure>

The second scene fuses two Gospel moments — the birth and, eight days later, the naming — into a single domestic interior, and the fusion is accomplished by architecture. Giotto opens the house of Zacharias in section, a two-roomed structure seen without its front wall beneath a projecting upper storey with its cornices, brackets and a glimpse of rooftop loggia: the doll's-house convention of the Arena Chapel matured into something close to an architectural portrait, a Florentine casa of the early Trecento laid open for inspection, down to the single vase standing in its wall niche above the old man's head — one of those still-life inventions, observed and unnecessary, by which Giotto certifies that a painted room is a real one.

The narrative climax belongs to the left chamber. Zacharias, mute since the lunette above — the wall's own registers thus narrate his silence between them — sits on a low stool with the writing tablet on his knee, setting down the words that end it: *Iohannes est nomen eius*, his name is John. Before him a woman holds up the swaddled infant, as though the child himself were presented for the verdict, and behind her the kinswomen who had objected — "there is none of thy kindred that is called by this name" — crowd the doorway between the rooms, their profiles graded from curiosity to wonder. The right chamber holds the birth proper in its quieter key: Elizabeth rests in the great curtained bed on its dark panelled chest, propped and turning toward her attendants, while three women serve her — the ministrations of the lying-in room rendered with the same matter-of-fact tenderness as the Paduan Birth of the Virgin. The two moments read in sequence across the shared façade, the house's central doorway serving as the hinge of time.

It is on this wall above all that the Peruzzi Chapel earned its long afterlife in the drawing books of the Renaissance. The grave, simplified masses of these women, the architectural clarity, the psychological economy of the seated scribe made the chapel a school for the draughtsmen who followed. The scene's legibility today depends, as everywhere in the chapel, on reading losses charitably — the *a secco* surfaces have thinned to a luminous ghost of their first state, and the ultraviolet photography of the 2009–2010 campaign restores, in the laboratory image, the fallen modelling of exactly these faces.

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-18.jpg" alt="Fresco in the Peruzzi Chapel: under a classical loggia crowned by statues and garlands against a dark sky, Herod sits at the banquet table as the Baptist's head is presented before him; at the left a young musician plays the vielle; at the right, in an adjoining room, the kneeling Salome offers the head on a dish to the seated Herodias">
  <figcaption>The Feast of Herod, c. 1310–1315, fresco and a secco, lower register of the left wall. Florence, Basilica di Santa Croce, Peruzzi Chapel.
</figcaption>
</figure>

The Baptist wall closes with the feast at which he dies, and Giotto stages it in the most astonishing architecture of the chapel. The banquet loggia is conceived all'antica: a flat-roofed classical structure on slender supports, its long entablature hung with festoons and crowned by a file of free-standing statues silhouetted against the night — nude and draped figures on their plinths, a painted sculpture gallery a full century before the antiquarian revivals of the Quattrocento. At the left the complex rises into a tower with an arcaded upper storey; at the right an arched opening leads to the inner room. The dark sky behind, with its pale disc, sets the whole precinct at night, the lamp-lit interior of a palace where, as the Gospel has it, a birthday banquet turns into an execution.

The narrative is distributed with lethal calm. At the table under the baldachin-like canopy sit Herod and his guests, and before the king the dish has arrived: the head of the Baptist, presented at the feast exactly as the girl had demanded, the horror served among the tableware. Herod's gesture — the hand drawn back, the gaze fixed — registers the Gospel's own note that the king was grieved by his oath. At the far left stands the scene's most celebrated figure, the young musician bowing his vielle, absorbed in his playing, his back half-turned to the catastrophe: the music of the feast continuing while the feast's meaning changes, an invention of narrative counterpoint that painters studied here for two hundred years.

The right-hand chamber completes the chain of guilt: Salome, kneeling, delivers the head on its dish to the seated Herodias — "and the damsel gave it to her mother" — while the group of women between the two rooms, elegant in their long gowns, turn and murmur, the court absorbing the event. The dance of Salome, the episode's usual centrepiece, is conspicuously absent or subsumed; Giotto narrates not the seduction but the consequence, and the wall that began with an angel at an altar ends with a head on a plate carried between women.

The scene's pictorial fortune matches the Birth's. The classical loggia with its statues stands at the head of a long Florentine lineage — Donatello's bronze relief of the same subject for the Siena font and the painted Herods of the Quattrocento are unthinkable without it — and the chapel as a whole, Baptist wall and Evangelist wall together, served the Renaissance as its anthology of grave narrative. The condition is the chapel's common fate: the *a secco* surfaces abraded to a pale unity in which the night sky has lost its depth and the faces their finish, legible now as magnificent drawing. Between the nineteenth century's repaints, their removal in 1958–1961, the Opificio's treatment of 1983–1985 and the diagnostic campaign of 2009–2010, the Feast of Herod has become, like its companions, a double object: the ruin on the wall, and the recovered image in the ultraviolet archive.

### The Evangelist Wall of the Peruzzi Chapel: Three Scenes from the Life of Saint John the Evangelist

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-19.jpg" alt="Fresco lunette by Giotto showing Saint John asleep on the rocky island of Patmos, surrounded by the sea, while apocalyptic visions appear in the sky above: a white-clad figure seated on clouds holding a sickle, a winged dragon, and a reclining woman with a child. The surface is heavily abraded, with large losses at the left">
  <figcaption>Saint John the Evangelist on Patmos, c. 1310–1315. Fresco and a secco, lunette. Florence, Santa Croce, right transept, Peruzzi Chapel, right wall.</figcaption>
</figure>

The cycle of Saint John the Evangelist opens, like that of the Baptist on the facing wall, in the lunette, and it opens with the only scene in the chapel that is not an event but a state of mind. John, exiled to the island of Patmos under Domitian — “I was in the isle that is called Patmos, for the word of God” (Revelation 1:9) — sits at the centre of a rocky islet ringed by a green, fish-bearing sea, his head sunk onto his hand in the ancient posture of the visionary sleep. He does not look at what surrounds him. The visions are present to his closed eyes alone, and Giotto has painted them as the island’s sky: an upper zone of blue in which the apparitions of the Apocalypse float untethered to any ground, while the saint below remains anchored to his rock. The lunette thus stages the Book of Revelation as its own frontispiece describes it — a thing seen in the spirit — and the viewer is placed in the singular position of seeing both the dreamer and the dream.

The apparitions have suffered gravely, and their identification calls for corresponding care. At the left of the sky a figure robed in white sits enthroned upon a bank of cloud, holding what is legible as a long-handled sickle: the “one like unto the Son of man” of Revelation 14, who sits on a white cloud “having in his hand a sharp sickle” for the harvest of the earth. Towards the centre a winged dragon, its body a faded red, twists through the air — the great adversary of Revelation 12 — and at the right reclines the Woman of that same chapter, the mulier amicta sole, stretched upon the clouds with her newborn child beside her. The flying figure at the damaged left edge appears to belong to the same narrative moment, the child “caught up unto God and to his throne” beyond the dragon’s reach, though the losses here are too severe for the reading to be pressed. In the lower corners of the lunette, at the water’s edge, two further haloed figures survive in fragmentary state. What Giotto has done, in any case, is without close precedent in Tuscan mural painting: he has compressed several chapters of the most intractable book of Scripture into a single simultaneous sky, trusting the sleeping witness below to bind them into one vision.

The condition of the lunette is the worst in the chapel, and it is itself historical evidence. The scene is painted, like the rest of the cycle, largely on dry plaster with organic binders, and the upper reaches of the wall have shed their secco finish most completely: whole passages at the left survive only as bare preparation, and the neutral patches that interrupt the surface mark the losses stabilised in the twentieth-century interventions. Yet even in ruin the design retains its intelligence. The island is set low and central, the sea runs as a continuous band along the base, and the visions are distributed across the curve of the lunette so that the composition breathes with the architecture rather than fighting it — the same accommodation to the given field that governs the Annunciation to Zacharias in the lunette opposite, where the Temple’s canopied enclosure performs the equivalent service.

The choice of subject rewards reflection. Franciscan Santa Croce had particular reasons to honour the Apocalypse — the order’s own theology had long read Revelation with urgent attention — and the Evangelist whose visions it records was, with the Baptist, titular of the chapel. For the Peruzzi, bankers whose name-saints and civic saints meet on these walls, the Patmos lunette carried the cycle’s one note of the world beyond history: everything below it, on both walls, happens in cities, among buildings and crowds; here alone the ground dissolves into sea and sky. That the chapel’s two lunettes should answer one another — the aged priest receiving an angel’s word in the Temple, the aged apostle receiving the word of God on his island — is a symmetry too deliberate to be accident, and it establishes at the top of each wall the divine commission from which the earthly narratives below descend.

The attribution of the design to Giotto himself has never been seriously contested, whatever share his workshop took in the execution; the cycle is anchored to his name by Ghiberti’s testimony that he painted four chapels in Santa Croce, and the Peruzzi murals are unanimously counted among them. The dating follows that of the chapel as a whole, in the first half of the second decade of the fourteenth century, by the documentary and technical chain set out above.

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-20.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto showing Saint John the Evangelist, in an orange mantle, extending his right arm toward the widow Drusiana, who rises from her bier wrapped in grave clothes. Behind them stretch the crenellated walls, towers and domed buildings of Ephesus against a dark sky. Apostles stand at the left, a dense crowd of mourners at the right; two figures kneel at John’s feet">
  <figcaption>The Raising of Drusiana, c. 1310–1315. Fresco and a secco. Florence, Santa Croce, right transept, Peruzzi Chapel, right wall.</figcaption>
</figure>

The central field of the Evangelist wall carries the cycle’s great public miracle, and one of the most influential compositions of Giotto’s maturity. The story comes from the apocryphal Acts of John as transmitted to the Trecento through the Golden Legend: Drusiana, a devout widow of Ephesus who had longed in vain to see the apostle again, died while he was still in exile; as her funeral procession passed out of the city it met John returning from Patmos, and at the mourners’ plea he commanded her to rise. Giotto seizes the instant of the command. John, isolated against the pale city wall in a mantle of burnt orange, throws his right arm forward in a gesture of absolute authority; Drusiana, still bound in her winding-cloth, has already lifted herself upright on the bier, her face turned to her deliverer; between the outstretched hand and the risen woman runs the empty interval across which the miracle travels, as charged as the gap between God and Adam that it would one day help to inspire.

The city is the scene’s second protagonist. Ephesus rises behind the figures as a continuous screen of crenellated walls, square towers and gates at the left, and at the right a cluster of centrally planned buildings crowned with ribbed, melon-like domes — architecture deliberately foreign, eastern in flavour, built to tell the Florentine viewer that this resurrection happened far away, in the apostle’s Asia. The buildings stand parallel to the picture plane but are turned very slightly, their flanks opening toward the chapel entrance, in obedience to the oblique viewing point from which the narrow chapel obliges its visitors to read the walls. Against this high, calm backdrop the human drama unfolds as a frieze: the apostles grouped behind John at the left, their faces a study in grave expectation; the kneeling youths at his feet; the bearers stooped under the bier; and at the right a deep crowd of mourners, among them women muffled in white and citizens in the green, crimson and violet mantles whose broad fall is one of the glories of Giotto’s late draughtsmanship. The famous blank patches that interrupt the crowd are losses, left as neutral fills by the restorers — wounds of the secco technique, not veils.

It is this wall above all that founded the Florentine tradition. The gesture of John’s extended arm, the gravity of the bystanders, the architecture used as a sounding-board for human action: these were studied on the spot by the painters of the fifteenth century, and the chain of drawn and painted responses described above begins here. Vasari’s generation already treated the Peruzzi murals as a school; the irony, given what the nineteenth century’s reverence would later cost the surface, is that the chapel was loved very nearly to death.

Of all the scenes in the chapel, the Drusiana is the one in which the ultraviolet campaign of 2009–2010 restored most to the mind’s eye: the fluorescence of the organic binders recovered, invisible to ordinary light, the modelling of faces in the crowd and the finish of draperies that the historic cleanings had scoured away. The visitor before the wall today must therefore perform a double act of reading — the composition, which survives entire and magnificent, and the surface, which is the ghost of itself. That the design holds its power even in this state is perhaps the strongest argument the chapel offers for the primacy of disegno that Florence would later claim as its birthright.

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-21.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto showing Saint John the Evangelist rising bodily from his open grave inside a columned building shown in section, lifted on rays of light toward Christ, who leans down from a cloud of haloed figures to grasp him. Astonished witnesses stand at left and right beneath two balconied structures; a figure crouches by the empty tomb below">
  <figcaption>The Ascension of Saint John the Evangelist, c. 1310–1315. Fresco and a secco. Florence, Santa Croce, right transept, Peruzzi Chapel, right wall.</figcaption>
</figure>

The cycle closes with the strangest and most audacious of its inventions. According to the legend that the Golden Legend gathered from the apocryphal tradition, John — alone among the apostles — did not die: in extreme old age at Ephesus he had a grave dug beside the altar, descended into it alive, and was enveloped in a blinding light; when the light passed, the grave was found empty. The Latin West read the story, cautiously, as a bodily assumption, and Giotto paints it as exactly that. From the open slot of the tomb, cut into the pavement at the centre of the composition, the aged apostle rises through the air in his rose-coloured mantle, arms lifted; above him Christ leans down out of a crowded glory of the blessed, rays of light binding the descending hand to the ascending saint. The picture is, in effect, a resurrection and an ascension compressed into a single vertical event, and no painter before Giotto had given the scene this form.

The setting is an interior displayed by the device the Peruzzi Chapel made its own: the building opened in section. A basilican structure, its arcades and side chambers exposed as if the front wall had been removed, occupies the full width of the field, flanked by two balconied pavilions of rose-coloured masonry whose upper storeys project on colonnettes. Within this transparent architecture the witnesses divide into two choruses. At the left, figures recoil and shield their eyes — one stoops heavily, overcome; at the right, a group of men in pale mantles gazes upward, one pointing along the path of the miracle, their draped verticality so sculptural that Michelangelo singled them out: his early pen copies after the standing spectators of this scene are among the most celebrated documents of Giotto’s authority over the Florentine Cinquecento. At the foot of the grave a solitary figure in red crouches over the opening, peering into the emptiness — the eyewitness of the legend’s conclusion, who finds only the void the viewer also sees.

Here more than anywhere in the chapel Giotto’s late style declares itself. The architecture no longer frames the event; it contains it, and the figures inhabit its spaces with a physical ease that the Paduan scenes of a few years earlier approach but do not equal. The composition is built on calibrated intervals — the empty centre of pavement around the tomb, the air through which John travels, the measured recession of the arcades — and its emotion is carried less by faces, which the secco losses have in any case ravaged, than by posture: the bowed back of the stricken witness, the vertical calm of the men at the right, the reach of Christ. In the dark sky above the rooflines two small pale discs survive; whether they are celestial bodies belonging to the iconography or simply losses in the blue is a question the wall itself, in its present state, does not answer, and it remains open here as in the Feast of Herod opposite.

For the chapel’s program the scene is the necessary terminus. The Baptist wall ends with a severed head carried to a banquet — the martyr’s death in the midst of the world’s pomp; the Evangelist wall ends with a body that does not die at all, drawn up out of the world into the light. Between those two endings the Peruzzi, bankers burying their dead beneath these floors, set their hopes; and it is worth remembering that the wall on which John escapes the grave stands directly above the space where the family’s own tombs lay. Few funerary programs of the Trecento argue their case with such economy.

The scene belongs with the rest of the cycle to the first half of the second decade of the fourteenth century, designed by Giotto and executed with his workshop in the mixed fresco and secco technique whose fragility has determined everything in the chapel’s later history.

## The Bardi Chapel: The Life of Saint Francis in Seven Scenes

The Bardi Chapel, to the right of the main chapel and probably commissioned by Ridolfo de' Bardi of the banking family, carries seven scenes from the life of Saint Francis on about 180 square metres of wall: six inside, running from the Renunciation of Worldly Goods to the posthumous apparitions, and the Stigmatization painted outside, over the entrance arch. The date is inferred rather than recorded. The *terminus post quem* is the canonisation of Saint Louis of Toulouse in 1317, since he is represented among the saints of the altar wall; the Opificio delle Pietre Dure, in presenting its recent restoration, framed the open chronological question as running from the years after that canonisation to a moment close to Giotto's return to Florence from Naples in 1333, while most earlier scholarship has favoured the 1320s, before the Neapolitan sojourn.

The chapel's material history reads like a summary of the history of restoration itself. The paintings disappeared under whitewash, probably in the first half of the eighteenth century, when the transept was refurbished; in 1812 and 1818 the cenotaphs of the architects Giuseppe Salvetti and Niccolò Gaspero Paoletti were set into the lower registers of the side walls, destroying the plaster behind them. Portions of the Trecento painting came to light in 1851, during preparations for a new decoration of the chapel, and Gaetano Bianchi uncovered the cycle in 1852–1853, restoring it as a full neo-medieval reconstruction in which everything missing was repainted.

The mechanical removal of the whitewash caused much of the abrasion still visible, and the removal of the cenotaphs revealed that the painting beneath them was entirely lost — the blank silhouettes that still scar the lowest scenes of both walls. Between the summer of 1957 and the end of 1958 the Florentine superintendency took the opposite course: Ugo Procacci and the restorer Leonetto Tintori removed all of Bianchi's repainting and renounced pictorial reconstruction altogether, leaving the thousands of losses exposed under a neutral tone so that only what remained of Giotto would speak — the recovery of what Tintori himself called the "grande luce di autenticità" of the original. The chapel is thus a case study in two opposed philosophies, nineteenth-century stylistic restoration and the twentieth-century ethics of authenticity, standing a few metres apart on the same walls.

The latest chapter opened in June 2022, when the Opificio delle Pietre Dure began, for the Opera di Santa Croce, the first full restoration in some seventy years, built on the diagnostic campaign of 2010 and the treatment of the Stigmatization in 2011–2013; the findings of its first phase were presented on 19 September 2024, and the Opera di Santa Croce announced the conclusion of the work on 11 September 2026. Its findings have rewritten the technical description of the cycle. The Bardi scenes were painted as true fresco in *giornate* — the master sketching each composition in advance to plan the patches of fresh plaster — but with extensive finishing in colours bound with an organic medium, probably egg: a deliberate mixed technique that carries forward the experiment of the neighbouring Peruzzi Chapel. Thermographic survey located the putlog holes and allowed the painters' scaffolding to be reconstructed — erected first at the height of the lunettes for the vault, then lowered stage by stage to the base of each scene — and beneath Giotto's plaster an earlier decorative layer, probably geometric, came unexpectedly to light.

### The Stigmatization of Saint Francis

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-22.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto showing Saint Francis kneeling on the rocky slope of La Verna, arms raised, as a crucified seraph with six red wings appears in the dark sky above; golden rays run from the seraph’s wounds to the saint’s hands, feet and side. A small chapel with a rose window stands at the right, a bird perches on the cliff at the upper left">
  <figcaption>The Stigmatization of Saint Francis, c. 1325. Fresco. Florence, Santa Croce, right transept, exterior wall above the entrance arch of the Bardi Chapel.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Bardi cycle begins before the visitor has entered the chapel. On the exterior wall, above the entrance arch and facing the transept, Giotto placed the event that made Francis unique among the saints: the impression of the wounds of Christ on Mount La Verna in September 1224, two years before his death. The position is itself the interpretation. The stigmata were the seal of Francis’s conformity to Christ — Bonaventure’s Legenda maior, the cycle’s textual source throughout, calls him the man marked with the sign of the living God — and by setting the scene over the threshold Giotto makes it the frontispiece under which all six interior scenes are read: whoever enters passes, literally, beneath the seal.

The composition is the third and most concentrated of the surviving treatments of the subject attributed to Giotto. The saint kneels on the bare mountain, his whole body torqued upward toward the apparition; the seraph, Christ crucified borne on three pairs of carmine wings, plunges down from the upper right; and the rays that bind wound to wound cross the picture as taut golden threads, the mystical transaction rendered as geometry. Against the Louvre panel signed OPUS IOCTI FLORENTINI — painted for San Francesco in Pisa and discussed above — the Bardi fresco is drastically pruned: no witnessing companion, no narrative predella, only the rock, the saint, the seraph, and the small chapel of La Verna at the right, its rose window and open door the one note of human architecture in the wilderness. A bird — read by some as the falcon which, the sources tell, kept vigil over the saint on the mountain — perches on the cliff at the upper left, and sparse trees cling to the ledges of a rock face whose faceted planes still descend from the Byzantine mountain-convention, now weathered into something observed.

The fresco shares the conservation history of the chapel it announces, set out scene by scene below: whitewashed in the eighteenth century, recovered in the mid-nineteenth, purged of its repaints by Leonetto Tintori in the campaign directed by Ugo Procacci, and newly consolidated and cleaned in the restoration completed in 2026. Its surface is worn, and the once-blue sky has darkened; but the essential structure — the diagonal of the saint answering the diagonal of the seraph — survives with its force intact, and remains the image through which generations of Florentines first learned what the chapel behind it contains.

### The Renunciation of Worldly Goods

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-23.jpg" alt="Fresco lunette by Giotto showing the young Francis, naked to the waist and praying, wrapped in the blue mantle of the Bishop of Assisi at the right, while at the left his angry father, in a yellow robe and holding the clothes his son has shed, is restrained by a bystander. Two groups of onlookers, including women and children, flank a pink palace raised on a high masonry podium; a child at each edge reacts to the scene.">
  <figcaption>Saint Francis Renouncing His Worldly Goods, c. 1325. Fresco, lunette. Florence, Santa Croce, right transept, Bardi Chapel, left wall.</figcaption>
</figure>

The narrative inside the chapel opens in the lunette of the left wall with the episode that Bonaventure placed at the root of everything: the public break with the father. Summoned before Bishop Guido of Assisi to answer Pietro di Bernardone’s demand for restitution, Francis stripped off his clothes, returned them with the words that henceforth he would say only “Our Father who art in heaven”, and was covered by the bishop’s own mantle.

The scene had been painted before, in the Assisi cycle attributed to Giotto; the Bardi version is its ruthless distillation. The crowd of the earlier fresco is cut to two compact choruses, the city of Assisi is compressed into a single pink palace on a high ashlar podium, and between the two parties — the family and the Church, the world renounced and the world embraced — opens the famous void: a blank interval of wall and sky at the very centre of the composition, across which father and son face each other and do not touch. No painter before Giotto had dared to make emptiness the protagonist of a picture; none would do it again with this authority until the fifteenth century.

The psychology is distributed with a dramatist’s economy. Pietro di Bernardone surges forward, his son’s shed garments over his arm, and is held back by the wrist — the gesture that tells us his rage is physical; behind him the men of his party mutter and stare. On the other side Francis, naked within the bishop’s blue mantle, prays with raised hands, his eyes already elsewhere, while Guido enfolds him with the slightly embarrassed tenderness of an official performing an unprecedented act. Giotto then adds the touch that has no textual source and needs none: at either edge, a child. One shelters against a woman’s skirts at the left; the other, at the right, is restrained by an adult as he raises his arm — street children drawn to a scandal, the city’s future watching the city’s saint being born. The Legenda maior says the onlookers were moved to tears and jeers; Giotto gives both, at knee height.

The lunette holds the first position in a program whose order is thematic as much as chronological. It faces, across the chapel, the Confirmation of the Rule in the opposite lunette: on one wall Francis strips himself of his earthly inheritance, on the other he receives his heavenly charter; the private rupture and the public institution answer one another at the same height. The registers below continue the system, so that the visitor reads the chapel not as a strip cartoon but as a set of balanced arguments — a construction only possible because the Bardi Chapel, unlike the twenty-eight-scene nave at Assisi, compresses the whole life into six fields and can therefore afford to think in pairs.

The scene is painted, like the rest of the cycle, in true fresco with secco finishes — a mixed technique like that of the neighbouring Peruzzi Chapel, but with far more of the work done in true fresco, which is the reason its surfaces came through the nineteenth-century uncovering in so much better state. Its modern history is that of the chapel: whitewash in the eighteenth century, recovery and integral repainting by Gaetano Bianchi in 1852–53, removal of the repaints by Tintori in the Procacci campaign, and the full conservation completed by the Opificio delle Pietre Dure in 2026, which returned to the lunette a legibility it had not had in three centuries.

### The Apparition at the Chapter of Arles

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-24.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto showing the interior of a chapter house rendered in section, its three arched bays framed by slender columns under a red-tiled roof. Saint Francis appears at the centre, standing with arms raised in the form of a cross, visible only to some of the hooded friars seated on benches around the room; Saint Anthony of Padua stands preaching at the left">
  <figcaption>The Apparition of Saint Francis at the Chapter of Arles, c. 1325. Fresco. Florence, Santa Croce, right transept, Bardi Chapel, left wall.</figcaption>
</figure>

The middle register of the left wall carries the quietest miracle of the cycle, and compositionally its most radical invention. Bonaventure relates that while Anthony of Padua was preaching to the provincial chapter at Arles on the inscription of the Cross, Francis — far away in body — appeared in the air of the chapter house, arms extended as if crucified, and blessed the brethren; the vision was seen by one friar and confirmed by the saint’s own knowledge of it.

The episode had been treated at Assisi; here Giotto rebuilds it from the ground up. The chapter house is displayed in perfect section, its front wall removed: three arched bays, slender colonnettes, a shallow coffered space under a red-tiled roof, marble revetment on the bench backs — an architecture of such measured transparency that the fresco has been called, with pardonable anachronism, the first interior in European painting that one could furnish.

Within this glass box the drama is entirely a matter of attention. Francis stands at the exact centre, frontal, arms lifted in the orant-cruciform gesture that quotes his own stigmatization; Anthony stands to the left, caught mid-sentence; and around them the friars, huddled in their habits on the perimeter benches, are divided — with a subtlety the Assisi version does not attempt — between those who see and those who do not. Some heads tilt upward, arrested; others continue to listen to the sermon; one props his chin in meditation, oblivious. The miracle, Giotto insists, happens inside an ordinary afternoon, and the picture’s power lies in the coexistence of the two: the supernatural visitor and the drowsy, shuffling actuality of a roomful of monks. The seated figures, seen largely from behind, their backs broad simplified masses of grey-brown, are among the boldest passages of his late style — volumes before they are persons, and persons precisely because they are volumes.

The scene stands opposite the Trial by Fire in the corresponding register of the right wall, and the pairing is again deliberate: on one side Francis present in spirit among his own brethren, on the other present in body before the unbelievers; the word preached within the order, the word witnessed outside it. In both, the architecture of the setting — chapter house and throne — does the work of defining the community to which the saint manifests himself.

The fresco’s material fortunes follow the chapel’s: eighteenth-century whitewash, Bianchi’s recovery and repainting, Tintori’s purge of 1957–58, and the recent Opificio campaign, in which the Arles scene served as one of the principal case studies for the technical reinvestigation of Giotto’s working method in the chapel. It is, with the Death of Saint Francis below it, the best-preserved field on the wall, and the one in which the painter’s control of an architectural stage reaches its furthest point before the Renaissance.

### The Death and Ascension of Saint Francis

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-25.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto showing the dead Saint Francis laid on a bier, surrounded by friars who kneel to kiss his hands and feet, raise their arms in grief, or stand in silent mourning; clerics and a knight attend at the sides. Above, angels carry the saint’s soul to heaven in a mandorla of light against the blue sky. A large irregular loss, left as a neutral patch, interrupts the centre of the scene">
  <figcaption>The Death and Ascension of Saint Francis, c. 1325. Fresco. Florence, Santa Croce, right transept, Bardi Chapel, left wall.</figcaption>
</figure>

The lowest field of the left wall, at eye level above the tombs of the Bardi dead, holds the scene for which the chapel has been loved longest: the death of Francis at the Porziuncola on the evening of 3 October 1226, fused — in the sky above — with the ascent of his soul, borne up by angels in a glory of light. The composition is a slow horizontal chord. The saint lies on his bier across the full width of the field, and around him the brotherhood breaks into every register of grief that the human body commands: one friar bends to kiss the stigmatized hands, another the feet; others throw up their arms in the ancient gesture of lamentation; others again stand rigid, grief turned inward. At the left a group of clerics and laymen attends with the composure of office; at the right the file of friars with processional candles and cross closes the scene like a cadence. Among the mourners kneels the figure the Legenda maior names Jerome — the learned and doubting knight who, Thomas-like, probed the wound in the saint’s side with his own hand and believed: doubt and devotion admitted to the same bedside.

It is this measured polyphony of mourning, rather than any single invention, that made the fresco canonical. Vasari already singled out the weeping brothers for praise, and when Domenico Ghirlandaio came to paint the obsequies of Francis for the Sassetti Chapel a century and a half later he built his composition openly upon this one — the homage of a Renaissance that regarded the wall as a foundation document. The comparison with the equivalent scenes at Assisi, where the death is divided among several fields, shows what the Bardi compression achieves: a single, unified elegy in which nothing moves except feeling.

The great pale silhouette that interrupts the centre of the scene is a wound of taste, and the chapel’s history is written in it. In the eighteenth century the frescoes were whitewashed over; in 1812 the funerary monument of the architect Giuseppe Salvetti was built into this wall, destroying the plaster behind it. When Gaetano Bianchi uncovered the cycle in 1852–53 he reinvented the lost passages with his own brush, and for a century the scene read as complete; the campaign conducted by Leonetto Tintori under Ugo Procacci then removed the nineteenth-century flesh and left the loss honestly exposed, toned in neutral — the choice that still governs the wall, maintained through the Opificio delle Pietre Dure’s conservation completed in 2026. What survives around the void is, by the fortune of true fresco, substantially Giotto’s own surface, and it includes some of the most moving painting of his career.

### The Confirmation of the Franciscan Rule

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-26.jpg" alt="Fresco lunette by Giotto showing the pope seated at the left under a coffered canopy crowned by a pediment with a bust medallion, while a standing mitred prelate in red holds out the scroll of the Rule to Saint Francis, who kneels before them at the head of a compact group of kneeling friars. Two pairs of standing figures in antique mantles frame the scene at the outer edges. Large plaster losses interrupt the upper left of the field.">
  <figcaption>The Confirmation of the Franciscan Rule, c. 1325. Fresco, lunette. Florence, Santa Croce, right transept, Bardi Chapel, right wall.</figcaption>
</figure>

The lunette of the right wall answers the Renunciation opposite: there Francis gave up his inheritance, here he receives the charter of his new family. The scene shows the solemn papal approval of the Franciscan Rule — read by most scholars as the confirmation by Honorius III with the bull of 1223, though the earlier, verbal approval by Innocent III has also been proposed, and the question remains open. The pope, enthroned beneath a classicizing aedicule whose pediment carries a painted bust in a medallion, presides while a standing prelate in red holds out the sealed document; Francis kneels to receive it, and behind him his companions kneel in a single dense phalanx, their tonsured heads a studied rhythm of obedience. Mitred prelates flank the throne; and at the outer edges, beyond the architecture, stand two pairs of figures in long antique mantles, gravely detached from the event — a framing chorus whose identity has exercised commentators, and whose statuesque drapery is among the most deliberately classical passages in Giotto’s entire oeuvre.

The subject had been painted at Assisi a generation earlier, in the cycle attributed to Giotto, and the comparison measures the distance of his late style. The Assisi version is a ceremony observed; the Bardi version is a ceremony constructed. The architecture no longer backs the figures but houses them — the coffered ceiling recedes over the papal court, the lateral bays admit the standing witnesses — and the kneeling friars are modelled with a breadth that makes their Assisi counterparts look drawn rather than built. The lunette also carries the program’s institutional argument at its highest point on the wall of the Church: beneath it, in descending registers, follow the saint’s witness before Islam and the visions that announced his glory, so that the right wall reads as the public, ecclesial life of the order, as the left wall reads as the personal and fraternal life of its founder.

The field has suffered more than its pendant: broad losses, left as neutral patches since the twentieth-century restoration, interrupt the canopy and the upper left of the scene. Its history is the chapel’s — eighteenth-century whitewash, Bianchi’s recovery and integral repainting of 1852–53, Tintori’s removal of the repaints under Procacci’s direction, and the Opificio’s recent campaign, concluded in 2026, which consolidated the plaster and recovered the legibility of the original surfaces that survive.

### The Trial by Fire before the Sultan

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-27.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto showing the Sultan of Egypt enthroned at the centre on a tall marble throne against a green patterned hanging, one hand resting on his knee, while his priests retreat at the left, one hiding his face in an orange mantle, and Saint Francis stands at the right beside a blazing fire with a companion friar behind him">
  <figcaption>Saint Francis before the Sultan (The Trial by Fire), c. 1325. Fresco. Florence, Santa Croce, right transept, Bardi Chapel, right wall.</figcaption>
</figure>

The middle register of the right wall carries the cycle’s confrontation with the world outside Christendom. In 1219, during the Fifth Crusade’s siege of Damietta, Francis crossed the lines and preached before al-Malik al-Kāmil, Sultan of Egypt; Bonaventure relates that he offered to enter a great fire together with the sultan’s priests, so that the true faith might be known by ordeal — and that the priests slipped away, while the sultan, unwilling to risk a tumult, declined the test but was moved to admiration. Giotto here reorganizes the episode already painted at Assisi in the cycle attributed to him, around its moral centre: the throne. The sultan sits elevated at the exact middle of the field, and his composed, almost immobile figure holds the judgement at the centre of the scale, between the retreating priests and the friar who is willing. The composition is a balance with a man as its fulcrum, and the irony is complete: it is the infidel prince who, by his very stillness, holds the balance between the two parties.

The two sides of the scale are characterized with Giotto’s driest observation. At the right Francis, grave and unhurried, gathers his habit as if already stepping toward the flames that rise in a red mass at his feet, his companion shrinking behind him; at the left the priests of the court make their escape — one glances back, caught, while another buries his face in his great orange mantle, a figure of such eloquent cowardice that it became proverbial in the literature on the chapel. Among the group at the left, white-robed figures in turbans are rendered without caricature; the patterned green hanging behind, the pseudo-epigraphic ornament of the throne, and the turbans of the fleeing clergy are Giotto’s economical shorthand for the East — a stage orient, but one built from dignity rather than fantasy.

For the Franciscans of Santa Croce the subject carried present meaning: the order held the custody of the Holy Land, and the encounter at Damietta was the founding warrant of its mission among the Muslims. Set opposite the Apparition at Arles, the scene completes the pairing of the middle registers — the saint present in spirit to his brethren, present in body before the unbeliever; the word within and the word without.

The fresco shares the chapel’s history of whitewash, nineteenth-century recovery and repainting by Bianchi, Tintori’s de-restoration of 1957–58 and the Opificio delle Pietre Dure’s campaign concluded in 2026. A loss at the left margin, left neutral, has taken the edge of the retreating group; the rest of the surface, in true fresco, preserves the velocity of the sultan’s gesture essentially as Giotto left it.

### The Visions of Fra Agostino and of the Bishop of Assisi

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Giotto/giotto-28.jpg" alt="Heavily damaged fresco by Giotto, its centre lost to a vast neutral patch shaped like a vanished funerary monument. At the left a dying friar sits up in bed, drawing back a curtain as he sees the ascending Saint Francis; at the right the sleeping Bishop of Assisi receives the same vision in his bed. A fragment at the centre preserves a group of friars’ heads, and two mourning figures survive at the lower right">
  <figcaption>The Visions of Fra Agostino and of Bishop Guido of Assisi, c. 1325. Fresco. Florence, Santa Croce, right transept, Bardi Chapel, right wall.</figcaption>
</figure>

The last field of the cycle, at the base of the right wall, is the most ruined and the most strangely affecting. Its subject is the double miracle that Bonaventure attaches to the moment of Francis’s death: in the Terra di Lavoro, Fra Agostino, provincial minister, long mute and himself at the point of death, suddenly cried out “Wait for me, father, I am coming with you”, and died; and far away the Bishop of Assisi, Guido, on pilgrimage to the shrine of Saint Michael on Monte Gargano, saw the saint in his sleep, who said to him: “Behold, I leave the world and go up to heaven.”

Giotto set the two visions in two chambers at the opposite ends of a single field — the dying friar at the left, starting up in his bed and drawing back the curtain as if light had entered the room; the sleeping bishop at the right, receiving in dream what Agostino received awake — so that the fresco became a meditation on the ways the same truth reaches the dying and the dreaming. Between them, in the lost centre, the ascending Francis must once have appeared, the object of both gazes; around the bier below, companions mourned, two of whom — one sunk forward in sleep or sorrow, one pressing a hand to his head — survive at the lower right.

The vast white silhouette that now occupies the heart of the scene is the negative imprint of the funerary monument of Niccolò Gaspero Paoletti, built into the wall in 1818, when the whitewashed chapel had forgotten what it contained; the corresponding monument of 1812 wounded the Death of Francis opposite. When the monuments were removed in the nineteenth-century rediscovery, the painting beneath them was gone. Gaetano Bianchi filled the void with his own invention in 1852–53; the campaign of Leonetto Tintori under Ugo Procacci stripped the fiction away and left the loss declared, preserving within it the one island of original plaster that survives — a cluster of friars’ heads, grave and attentive, floating in the blank like a quotation from a destroyed text. No field in the chapel states more plainly the ethics of modern restoration: what remains is true, and what is absent is not pretended.

Even as a fragment, the scene completes the program with precision. The left wall had ended with the death seen from within — the body, the grief, the doubter’s probing hand; the right wall ends with the death seen from afar, as vision and as promise. For the Bardi, whose own dead lay beneath these walls, the pairing framed the chapel’s purpose: mourning below, assurance above, and between them the saint who passed from one to the other. The fresco shared in the Opificio delle Pietre Dure’s conservation concluded in 2026, which stabilized the surviving plaster and its fragments.

## Late Career: Naples, Bologna and Milan

Naples is the best-documented stage of the late career and the most completely lost. Robert of Anjou's chancery registers record payments and gifts to Giotto every year from 1328 to 1333. The registers were destroyed in 1943 and are known through earlier transcriptions. The Dizionario Biografico dates the first payment, for unspecified work, to 8 September 1328. Another reconstruction places at the beginning of December 1328 the king's order to his treasurers to pay Giotto a monthly salary "pro gagiis suis", the amount of which is unknown.

The difference probably reflects how the lost documents have been read, and neither date can be given with false precision. The central document is the privilege of 20 January 1330, known from a transcription published in Dresden in 1860. The king declares that "magister Ioctus de Florentia pictor, familiaris et fidelis noster, fulcitur providis actibus et exercitatur servitiis fructuosis", and therefore receives him "in familiarem nostrum" and keeps him in the royal household, with the honours and privileges of the other *familiares*.

The wording rewards services already given ("servitiis fructuosis"), not reputation alone. This matters when assessing the common claim that Giotto was made "court painter". The 1330 privilege makes him a member of the royal household. Titles such as *protomagister* and *prothopictor* are reported in documents of 1331 and 1332, and those texts are cited in the secondary literature far more often than they are quoted. Popular accounts that make the privilege itself a "primo pittore di corte" appointment run two documents together.

Of the works Giotto made for Robert and his queen, very little survives. The largest commission seems to have been the palace chapel of Castel Nuovo. Its cycle covered more than 1,800 square metres of wall, according to the Dizionario Biografico's estimate. What remains are fragments of decorative bands and medallions with heads of prophets or ancestors of Christ in the window splays.

They show that, unlike at Padua or in the Lower Church at Assisi, Giotto here relied on a large workshop of mixed origins. Some of the heads have been linked with Maso di Banco, though that proposal has been firmly contested. Petrarch, writing his *Itinerarium* in 1358, advises the traveller landing at Naples not to miss the king's chapel, "in qua conterraneus olim meus, pictorum nostri evi princeps, magna reliquit manus et ingenii monimenta". It is a rare case of a contemporary humanist directing visitors to a specific work by Giotto.

Ghiberti says Giotto painted "la sala del re Uberto de huomini famosi" and worked in the Castel dell'Ovo. The cycle of Famous Men, which placed Giotto at the start of a humanist and courtly theme with a long future, has left no trace.
