Vasari adds that Giotto painted Old and New Testament scenes in several chapels of Santa Chiara, the Clarissan foundation built for Queen Sancia, and an Apocalypse in one of them, "per quanto si dice, invenzione di Dante".

That last detail is chronologically impossible as a direct collaboration, since Dante died in 1321, and it is best read as another legend attaching the painter to the poet. Fragments of a Calvary and Lamentation found after the Second World War in the nuns' choir of Santa Chiara, and the illusionistic painted choir stalls there, have been attributed to Giotto or to his design. Two small Apocalypse panels in the Staatsgalerie in Stuttgart, copied around 1350 in the Neapolitan Hamilton Bible now in Berlin, may reflect the Santa Chiara Apocalypse. The Naples years mattered most for their effect on others. A local following grew from them, including the painter known as the Master of Giovanni Barrile and Roberto d'Oderisio, whose own later *familiaritas* at the Angevin court has been studied as a sequel to Giotto's.

The signed Bologna polyptych, discussed above, belongs to the same years and suggests a Bolognese episode inside the Neapolitan period. The Dizionario Biografico notes that no Neapolitan document names Giotto between January 1330 and May 1331, which would leave room for a stay at the Castello di Galliera, where a source reports him at work for the papal legate Bertrando del Poggetto. This is a hypothesis built on converging indications, not a documented itinerary.

The final journey is to Milan. Villani states that the commune sent Giotto to serve the lord of Milan and that he died after returning. The Dominican chronicler Galvano Fiamma, a contemporary witness at the Visconti court, describes the luxurious palace Azzone Visconti<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-azzone-visconti" class="footnote"><sup>30</sup></a> built around 1335. In one hall was painted Worldly Glory (the *Vanagloria*) surrounded by famous princes, Aeneas, Hector, Hercules, Attila, Charlemagne and Azzone himself, figures "ex auro azurro et smaltis distincte in tanta pulcritudine et tam subtili artificio" that nothing like them could be found in the whole world.

Galvano does not name the painter. An eighteenth-century Milanese antiquary first proposed that the description preserved the only record of Giotto's Milanese work, and modern scholarship has generally accepted the connection as probable. Two layers are again involved here. That Giotto went to Milan at the commune's behest is documented by Villani. That the *Vanagloria* was his is a reasonable inference from Galvano's description combined with Villani's, and Ghiberti and Vasari support it. Nothing survives. Giotto's influence on Lombard painting of the Trecento, visible for example in the Crucifixion in San Gottardo in Corte, Azzone's palace chapel, is the only material echo, and it was produced by local painters.

## Giotto as Architect: The Campanile and the Last Years

The Florentine appointment of 12 April 1334 is one of the few documents in Giotto's life that is quoted in full. The communal authorities decided

> eligere et deputare dictum magistrum Giottum in magistrum et gubernatorem laborerii et operis ecclesie Sanctae Reparatae, et constructionis et perfectionis civitatis Flor(entiae), et fortificationis ipsius civitatis et aliorum operum dicti comunis.

The scope is broad. Giotto was put in charge not only of the cathedral works but of the city's building programme, its fortifications and the commune's other works. That reflects the medieval understanding of a *magister* as a designer and organiser of large projects, not a specialised modern architect, and it also reflects Giotto's standing. The administrative context is known from the cathedral's own history. In 1331 the commune had entrusted the Opera di Santa Maria del Fiore to the patronage of the Arte della Lana and arranged regular funding from public revenues. The wool guild, freshly in charge, turned three years later to the most famous artist of the day. It has been said that, before 1334, Giotto's fame rested entirely on painting, and that he had no known experience directing building sites. The claim that he designed the Arena Chapel has been cast into doubt by evidence that the building was modified before or during the painting.

Work on the campanile began on 18 July 1334, according to Villani, evidently on the basis of a design or model already prepared. During Giotto's lifetime probably only the base was built. It is faced with hexagonal reliefs of biblical scenes and the Labours of Man, designed by Giotto according to the tradition. Ghiberti (*Commentarii*, II) says that the first reliefs ("le prime storie") were "di sua mano scolpite e designate" by Giotto, and that he himself saw Giotto's drawings for them; he also reports, "si dice", that Giotto sculpted the first two. Most of them were carried out, probably largely after his death, by [Andrea Pisano](/Content/Artists/XIV-c/Andrea-Pisano.html), who succeeded him on the project. The originals were removed in the 1960s and are now in the Museo dell'Opera del Duomo, with copies in place. How much of the finished tower corresponds to Giotto's intentions, and how his successors altered the design, belongs to the tower's later architectural history. The name "Campanile di Giotto" honours the initiator, not the author of the finished building.

Giotto died on 8 January 1337, after returning from Milan. The commune buried him with great honour in Santa Reparata, the old cathedral then being replaced by the new one. Far more visible today is the monument erected in the cathedral in the age of Lorenzo de' Medici. It combines a portrait bust in a roundel with a Latin epitaph composed by Poliziano, which begins with the painter speaking in the first person: "Ille ego sum, per quem pictura extincta revixit". The epitaph is the humanist conclusion of the revival scheme Boccaccio had outlined a century earlier.

## Workshop, Following, and the Giotteschi

Nearly every problem discussed so far has turned on the workshop. The Assisi cycle had teams of painters and a trusted assistant who finished it. The Arena Chapel was painted by a master closely supervising anonymous collaborators. The Lower Church transept and the Stefaneschi altarpiece have been divided between the master and the "Parente" or "Maestro delle Vele". The signed panels have been judged as workshop products. Naples required a large, mixed workshop. The Baroncelli polyptych stands at the meeting point of Giotto and Taddeo Gaddi.

Giotto seems to have run his enterprise more like a building site than a traditional painter's shop. It was organised to take on several large commissions at once in widely separated cities, with the master supplying designs, supervising and painting the key passages. That model accounts both for the scale of his output and for the persistent difficulty of attribution. It also shows how misleading the question "Giotto or not Giotto?" can be. That question is historically legitimate, but it often assumes an idea of individual authorship, the nineteenth-century cult of the autograph, that the fourteenth-century workshop did not share.

Among the documented or early-attested members of the circle, Taddeo Gaddi stands first. Cennini, who traces his own training back to Giotto through Agnolo and Taddeo Gaddi, calls Taddeo Giotto's pupil, and Taddeo was his godson. His Baroncelli Chapel frescoes in Santa Croce, documented in the 1330s, show a Giottesque vocabulary pushed towards new effects of light and architectural complexity. Maso di Banco, whose presence in Naples is debated, and the author of the St Sylvester chapel in Santa Croce, is the pupil in whom Giotto's volumetric gravity was pared down to an almost abstract clarity.

Stefano Fiorentino, praised by fourteenth-century sources for illusionism and reported by the seventeenth-century historian [Baldinucci](/scholars.html#baldinucci-filippo) to have been Giotto's grandson through the marriage of Giotto's daughter to the painter Ricco di Lapo, has been proposed as the hand behind the "Parente di Giotto". The Dizionario Biografico objects that such a grandson would have been barely a child in the mid-1310s. Beyond the Florentine core, Giotto's work in Rimini and Padua produced local schools that long outlived his visits, as did his work in Naples and Milan. The Paduan antiphonaries, the Riminese crucifixes, the Neapolitan masters and the Lombard painters of San Gottardo each testify to a "giottismo" transmitted through the example of the works themselves as much as through training.

The history of misattribution belongs to the same subject. A doctoral study published in 2025 used Offner's phrase "Non-Giotto" for the many and varied works attributed to Giotto in the nineteenth century and now given to other artists. It showed how collectors, dealers and critics in Britain built a Giotto out of pictures that were not his.

The Dormition from Ognissanti, now in Berlin, and the small panels of the life of Christ dispersed among New York, Boston, Munich, Florence and London are cases where attributions have gone back and forth. The Assisi Lower Church, the Bargello chapel and numerous small panels have passed between Giotto and his following over the generations. Critical fortune, in other words, has not only praised Giotto. It has repeatedly redrawn his body of work, and the Giotto of any given period reflects that period's idea of what a founding genius should look like.

## Critical Fortune and Conclusion: What Giotto Changed

The first reception of Giotto was literary and civic. Dante registered his fame, Villani his naturalism, Boccaccio the combination of illusion and intellect, Petrarch his standing among connoisseurs, Cennini his conversion of painting from Greek to Latin, and Filippo Villani his place in Florentine glory. By the early fifteenth century he was a humanist commonplace. Ghiberti gave the tradition its first catalogue and a sober formula of natural art "non uscendo dalle misure". Vasari gave it its canonical biography and, through anecdotes of the sheep, the fly and the O, the image of the untaught natural genius.

In the seventeenth century, municipal rivalries such as the dispute between Bolognese and Florentine historians over the priority of their schools kept Giotto at the centre of arguments about primacy rather than about painting. The Jesuit art historian who published his *Storia pittorica della Italia* in 1795–1796 gave a catalogue close to the modern one and set Giotto within the general development of Italian art.

The nineteenth century brought the documentary and philological revolution: archival publications from Florence, Padua and Naples, the first attempts to separate hands at Assisi, the Victorian English enthusiasm that made the Arena Chapel a pilgrimage site, and the great German Franciscan monograph of 1885 that first set out, at length, the case for the Assisi cycle as Giotto's. Bibliographies compiled up to 1970 already counted close to two thousand items. The twentieth century added the Giotto exhibition of 1937, Offner's skepticism, the technical study of *giornate*, and the large restoration campaigns at Assisi after the earthquake of 1997, at Padua in 2001–2002 and at Santa Croce from 2010 to the present.

What, finally, did Giotto change? The early sources agree remarkably well, and on this point they are more reliable than on dates or anecdotes. Villani says he drew figures and gestures from nature. Boccaccio says his painted things seemed not like reality but reality itself, and that his art addressed the understanding of the wise. Cennini says he turned the art from Greek to Latin and made it modern. Ghiberti says he brought natural art and grace within measure. Reduced to what the surviving works can show, these statements describe three connected achievements.

The first is spatial. Giotto built painted spaces that seem habitable: rooms, thrones and buildings seen at an angle, with figures standing in them rather than against a flat ground. The Arena Chapel's coretti and the Ognissanti throne are its clearest demonstrations. The second concerns the human figure, now modelled as a weighted, three-dimensional body. The Santa Maria Novella Christ hangs from the nails, and the Paduan figures have the density of relief sculpture. The third is narrative. A story is told through the exchange of looks and the economy of gesture, so that the viewer grasps the moral and emotional core of an episode by reading bodies rather than inscriptions.

None of this was invented from nothing. Roman painting of Cavallini's generation, the sculpture of Nicola Pisano and Arnolfo, the example of Cimabue and the art of classical antiquity all lie behind it. The Isaac Master, whoever he was, had already opened the door at Assisi. Giotto's historical significance lies in having combined these possibilities into a coherent language and in having set up a workshop able to spread that language from Padua to Naples and from Rome to Milan. He also had the good fortune, rare among medieval artists, to be celebrated in writing by the most influential writers of his century.

The layered picture of evidence argued for at the start leads to several conclusions. The documented facts are few, firm and more interesting than the legends. There is the appointment of 1334, the death of January 1337, the Paduan documents of 1300–1305, the Naples privilege of 1330, the loan of 1309, the will of Riccuccio di Puccio of 1312 that names him, the loom lease of the same year, and the goods left in Rome in 1313.

The early testimony is valuable in proportion to its closeness to Giotto. Dante, Riccobaldo and Villani carry weight that Vasari's anecdotes never can, although Ghiberti's sheep may preserve an older kernel than Vasari's elaborations. Scholarly consensus has settled the Arena Chapel, the Santa Maria Novella crucifix, the Ognissanti Madonna, the Badia polyptych and the Santa Croce chapels as the core of the œuvre. Consensus is weaker for the signed polyptychs, the Stefaneschi altarpiece and the Lower Church at Assisi, and there the balance has shifted in recent decades towards more generous attribution.

The central question remains open. Whether Giotto painted the Legend of St Francis in the Upper Church at Assisi, and whether he was the Isaac Master, cannot be decided from the documents, and the most sophisticated technical studies have not decided it either. Most Italian specialists answer yes, with strong arguments from continuity and economy. The skeptical tradition that runs from the early-nineteenth-century German critics through Offner to the American study of 1960 continues to argue, with equal seriousness, that the cycle's spatial language cannot be reconciled with Padua. On the present evidence the attribution to Giotto remains the more economical reading, but neither answer can be treated as established fact.
